# SKG: A Law-Induced Engine from Inference to Induction

Jian An Chaoshi Wu Jinhui Wu

October 8, 2026

## Abstract

This paper proposes the SKG (Symplectic-Kernel Gradient) reasoning engine, a lawinduced framework where inference is geometric evolution under objective laws and data only provide instances. The SKG system consists of eight components, four of which correspond to conservation laws, constitutive relations, symmetry groups, and axiom systems. Main results: First, invariants enter the metric through the Jacobian, and a complete inverse reconstruction theory is established, including rank condition, consistency criterion, and uniqueness theorem. Second, the kernel bandwidth matrix is uniquely determined by Fisher information and is task-independent. Third, the metric reconstruction error decomposes into four terms, with the squared condition number as the dominant amplification factor. Fourth, a state-dependent intrinsic metric theory is built on matrix-valued RKHS. Fifth, three-layer projections handle axiom, symplectic-step, and conservation constraints. Sixth, residual theory yields an observable formula, a four-dimensional rank condition, and structural unidentifiability of the metric block. Seventh, kernel parameterization provides an RKHS extrapolation bound and a strict out-of-distribution gain theorem in group directions. Eighth, a learning-decoupling theorem shows that induction reduces to learning only the task energy.

Keywords: law-induced inference; SKG; invariant metric; Fisher information; OOD error; learning flow; induction decoupling

## 1 Introduction

## 1.1 Two Paradigms of Inference

The mainstream paradigm of modern machine learning is data-driven: given large amounts of data, learn a mapping from inputs to outputs. This paradigm has achieved significant success in image classification, speech recognition, and natural language processing [1, 2], but sufers a fundamental limitation: no extrapolation guarantee. When the test distribution difers from the training distribution, model predictions may fail entirely [3, 4].

Data-driven methods mix laws with data. What is learned from data is the correlation on the training distribution, not laws that hold on all distributions. When the distribution changes, the learned correlations fail.

Another paradigm is law-induced: laws are given a priori; data only provide instances. Laws are objective and distribution-independent; data are concrete and distribution-dependent. The inference process is: given laws and a few instances, derive conclusions under law constraints. This idea has appeared in physics, control theory, and scientific computing, but has not been systematically unified into a provable inference engine.

## 1.2 Limitations of Existing Methods

Existing methods handle local aspects of law-induced inference but do not establish a complete framework.

Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINN) [5, 6] embed physical equations as loss regularization terms. Their limitations: constraints are soft and can be violated, with violation cost only in the loss; no OOD guarantee; laws are not hard structures but part of the training signal.

Hamiltonian and Lagrangian Neural Networks [7, 37] embed Hamiltonian or Lagrangian structure into network architectures. Their limitations: only handle conservative systems, not dissipation; no OOD bound; structural constraints target specific dynamical forms.

Group-Equivariant Networks [26, 27, 28] embed group structure into architectures to achieve equivariance. Their limitations: equivariance requires per-group design; constraints and conservation laws are not handled; equivariance is an architectural property, not an inference guarantee.

Neural ODE [38] treats network propagation as continuous-time dynamics. Its limitations: no law prior; dynamics entirely learned from data; no OOD bound.

RKHS Methods [42, 41, 43] provide OOD bounds, but their goal is data fitting, not physical laws; the metric is a statistic of the data distribution, not a geometric object induced by physical laws.

All the above methods add law elements within a data-driven framework. This paper proposes a diferent approach: laws as the core of the inference engine, data only for calibration.

## 1.3 Core Idea of This Paper

The core idea is: inference should be driven by objective laws; data only provide instances.

Specifically, we decompose the inference process into three levels. The first is the law level: symmetry, axioms, conservation laws, invariants, projections. These are objective properties of the system, given a priori. The second is the geometric level: laws are encoded as mathematical objects (symplectic structure, metric, invariant space) through geometric structures. The third is the inference level: given laws and geometry, inference equals geometric evolution—from an initial state along a geometric path determined by the structure, derive the final state.

The role of data is limited to calibrating the unknown parts of the framework. In our framework, the unknown parts are the objective–task decomposition of the energy: the objective part $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } }$ is given by physical laws, and the task part $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ is learned from task data.

## 1.4 Contributions of This Paper

We establish a complete law-induced inference and induction engine. Specifically, we propose the SKG octuple

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \boldsymbol { S } = ( M , g , \omega , H , S , G , \Delta , \Pi ) , } \end{array}\tag{1}
$$

where M is the configuration manifold, g is the kinetic metric, ω is the symplectic structure, H is the Hamiltonian, S is the entropy functional, G is the generator of the dissipative metric, $\Delta$ is the invariant, and Π is the projection triple. This octuple converts law-induced inference from a philosophical concept into a mathematical object. The four levels of laws—symmetry group, axiom system, conservation laws, constitutive relations—map to the four components:

Table 1: Four levels of laws and four SKG components
<table><tr><td>Law level</td><td>Mathematical object</td><td>SKG component</td><td>Task relevance</td></tr><tr><td>Symmetry group</td><td>G</td><td>∆</td><td>Irrelevant</td></tr><tr><td>Axiom system</td><td>A</td><td>Ⅱ</td><td>Irrelevant</td></tr><tr><td>Conservation laws</td><td>ω#, E</td><td>ω</td><td>Irrelevant</td></tr><tr><td>Constitutive relations</td><td>H, S</td><td>H,S</td><td>Partially relevant</td></tr></table>

The first three levels are entirely task-independent—they are objective properties of the system. The fourth level is partially task-relevant—the form of constitutive relations depends on the material, but the functional form is given a priori [10, 11]. This hierarchical structure allows SKG to clearly distinguish known parts from parts to be estimated.

Around this framework, we establish the following main results.

Invariant-induced metric and intrinsic metric reconstruction. Invariants enter the metric construction through the Jacobian:

$$
g = J ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J + \varepsilon _ { G } I ,\tag{2}
$$

where J is the invariant Jacobian, Σ is the kernel bandwidth matrix, and $\varepsilon _ { G }$ is the regularization constant. In the constant case, we establish a complete inverse reconstruction theory: solvability rank condition (Theorem 3.9), consistency criterion (Theorem 3.11), and uniqueness theorem (Theorem 3.14). The kernel bandwidth matrix is uniquely determined by Fisher information and satisfies task-independence (Theorem 3.19). The metric reconstruction error decomposes into four terms (Theorem 3.21), with the squared condition number $\kappa ( \Sigma ^ { * } ) ^ { 2 }$ as the dominant amplification factor (Theorem 3.22). In the state-dependent case, we establish a function-space reconstruction theory on matrix-valued RKHS, proving pointwise identifiability (Theorem 4.8) and function-space uniqueness (Theorem 4.9).

Three-layer projections and residual theory. Axiom projection $\Pi _ { A } .$ , symplectic-step projection $P _ { \perp }$ , and conservation projection $\Pi _ { E }$ handle three types of constraints [31, 32, 19].

The residual equation yields the observable formula (Proposition 6.3), the four-dimensional rank condition (Proposition $6 . 4 )$ , and noise propagation (Proposition 6.5). The structural unidentifiability of $g _ { q q }$ (Proposition 6.6) directly guides the engineering implementation of setting $g _ { q q } = I$

Structural OOD advantages. Kernel parameterization yields the RKHS extrapolation bound (Theorem 4.15). The strict OOD gain theorem in group directions (Theorem 8.15): under orthogonal representations, invariant metrics satisfy $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( K ) \leq \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K )$ , while unstructured MLP metrics satisfy the Lipschitz lower bound $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( M ) \geq L _ { M } \cdot d - C$

Learning flow and induction decoupling. The inference flow and learning flow share the same geometric structure. The metric $g _ { q }$ is objectively determined by Fisher information (Theorem 3.19) and is not learned by gradient descent. The Fisher metric $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ in parameter space is estimated once from task data, also not learned by gradient descent. The learningdecoupling theorem (Theorem 9.1) proves that the sole object of induction is the parameters $\phi$ of the task energy $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$

Experimental validation. On a 1D damped oscillator and a 2D rotation-invariant system: 6 rigorous unit tests (machine precision), 10 core experiments (multi-seed paired t-test, $p \textless$ 0.01).

## 1.5 Closed Loop from Inference to Induction

The core of this paper is to build a law-induced inference and induction engine. Chapters 2–8 establish the inference flow: given a metric and energy, inference is pure geometric evolution. Chapter 9 establishes the learning flow: given the structure of the inference flow, induction is decoupled into learning only the task energy. The two flows share the same geometric skeleton:

Table 2: Symmetric structure of inference and learning flows
<table><tr><td>Aspect</td><td>Inference flow</td><td>Learning flow</td></tr><tr><td>Space</td><td>State space q</td><td>Parameter space  $\phi$ </td></tr><tr><td>Metric</td><td> $g _ { q }$  (objective, task-independent)</td><td> $F _ { S } ( \phi )$  (task-relevant)</td></tr><tr><td>Driving force</td><td>∇S (energy gradient)</td><td>∇L (loss gradient)</td></tr><tr><td>Dynamics</td><td> $\dot { q } = - g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ </td><td> $\dot { \phi } = - F _ { S } ^ { - 1 } \nabla \mathcal { L }$ </td></tr><tr><td>Learning object None</td><td></td><td>φ(parameters of  $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } )$ </td></tr><tr><td>Purpose</td><td>Reach target</td><td>Determine task energy</td></tr></table>

## 1.6 The Pullback Principle

The framework is unified by a single structural principle: pullback. A metric on a coarser space is pulled back to a finer space through the Jacobian of the embedding.

In the inference flow (Chapter 3), the intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta }$ on the invariant space is pulled back to the state space through $J _ { 1 } = \partial \Delta / \partial q \mathrm { . }$

$$
g _ { q } = J _ { 1 } ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J _ { 1 } + \varepsilon _ { G } I .\tag{3}
$$

The intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta }$ is objective, task-independent, and determined by Fisher information (Theorem 3.19). The pulled-back metric $g _ { q }$ inherits these properties.

In the learning flow (Chapter 9), the state-space metric $g _ { z }$ is pulled back to the parameter space through $J _ { 2 } = \partial z / \partial \theta$ :

$$
g _ { \theta } = J _ { 2 } ^ { \top } g _ { z } J _ { 2 } + \varepsilon _ { \theta } I .\tag{4}
$$

The state-space metric $g _ { z }$ is objective and task-independent. The pulled-back metric $g _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } }$ inherits these properties.

The two pullbacks share the same structure. The two flows are not independent; they are two instances of the same geometric mechanism, applied at two levels of the hierarchy

$$
{ \mathrm { i n v a r i a n t ~ s p a c e } } \ { \xrightarrow { J _ { 1 } } } \ { \mathrm { s t a t e ~ s p a c e } } \ { \xrightarrow { J _ { 2 } } } \ { \mathrm { p a r a m e t e r ~ s p a c e } } .\tag{5}
$$

The properties of the pulled-back metrics are inherited from the intrinsic metric: taskindependence, parameterization invariance, and transferability. This inheritance is the structural reason the learning problem simplifies to determining the task energy alone (Theorem 9.1).

The pullback principle is the unifying thread of the framework. It appears twice, once in the inference flow and once in the learning flow, and it accounts for the symmetric structure of the two flows (Table 2).

The objectivity of the metric leads to learning decoupling, reducing induction to determining the task energy. This is the closed loop from inference to induction.

## 1.7 Why the Symplectic–Dissipative Structure

Research on symplectic–dissipative structures has two main threads. The first is Metriplectic theory, proposed by Morrison [12, 13], which establishes the basic framework for decomposing a dynamical vector field into a conservative part and a dissipative part:

$$
\dot { z } = L \nabla E + M \nabla S ,\tag{6}
$$

where L is an antisymmetric matrix and M is a positive semi-definite symmetric matrix. The second is GENERIC theory, proposed by Grmela and Ottinger [<sup>¨</sup> 14, 15], which generalizes this framework to non-equilibrium thermodynamics.

SKG takes the symplectic–dissipative structure as its instance for three reasons. First, it is the simplest law structure with a complete theory. Second, it cleanly exhibits the law-induced advantage: when the symplectic part is known, OOD error comes only from the dissipative part. Third, its mathematical tools (symplectic geometry, Riemannian geometry, dissipative mechanics) are mature and rigorous.

We note that SKG is not restricted to symplectic–dissipative systems. Chapter 11 explains that the framework applies to any law structure with a known-part-plus-estimated-part decomposition.

## 1.8 Comparison with PINN

The diference between SKG and PINN is paradigmatic, not technical.

Table 3: Paradigm comparison of SKG and PINN
<table><tr><td>Dimension</td><td>PINN</td><td>SKG</td></tr><tr><td>Role of laws</td><td>Soft regularization</td><td>Hard structure</td></tr><tr><td>Violating laws</td><td>Tolerable (cost is loss)</td><td>Mathematically forbidden</td></tr><tr><td>OOD guarantee</td><td>None</td><td>RKHS bound</td></tr><tr><td>Constraint handling</td><td>Loss term</td><td>Three-layer projection</td></tr><tr><td>Equivariance</td><td>Data augmentation</td><td>Structural guarantee</td></tr><tr><td>Estimated part</td><td>All network parameters</td><td>Only parameters of  $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ </td></tr></table>

PINN is a data-driven method with law regularization. SKG is a law-driven method with data calibration. The former is data-centric with laws as auxiliary; the latter is law-centric with data as auxiliary.

## 1.9 Structure of This Paper

Chapter 2 establishes the geometric foundations and the octuple definition. Chapter 3 establishes invariant-induced metrics and intrinsic metric reconstruction. Chapter 4 establishes state-dependent intrinsic metrics. Chapter 5 establishes three-layer projections. Chapter 6 establishes residual theory and identifiability analysis. Chapter 7 discusses kernel parameterization and OOD error decomposition. Chapter 8 proves structural OOD advantages. Chapter 9 establishes the learning flow and induction decoupling. Chapter 10 reports experimental validation. Chapter 11 discusses the overall positioning of SKG as a law-induced inference engine. The appendices provide higher-order geometric supplements.

The paper distinguishes three types of conclusions. Rigorous conclusions have mathematical proofs and experimental validation. Assumption-dependent conclusions depend on explicit assumptions. Heuristic observations have no rigorous proof but are used as engineering diagnostics.

H denotes the Hamiltonian (a scalar function).

M is used both for the manifold and the invariant dimension; the context distinguishes. $J = \partial \Delta / \partial q$ denotes the invariant Jacobian. Σ denotes the kernel bandwidth matrix (a positive definite matrix on the invariant space). $g _ { \Delta }$ denotes the intrinsic metric (a metric on the invariant space). $\varepsilon _ { G }$ denotes the metric regularization constant.

Function spaces. ${ \mathcal { H } } _ { \kappa }$ denotes a reproducing kernel Hilbert space. κ denotes a kernel function. $\| \cdot \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa } }$ denotes the RKHS norm.

Others. d denotes the state-space dimension. M denotes the invariant dimension in the invariant context. n denotes the number of degrees of freedom. α denotes the Rayleigh damping coeficient. λ denotes the regularization coeficient.

## 2 Geometric Foundations

This chapter establishes the geometric foundations of SKG. Section 2.1 starts from GENERIC theory and explains the structure of the symplectic–dissipative decomposition and the degeneracy conditions. Section 2.2 defines the SKG octuple. Section 2.3 establishes the second-order SKG flow and explicitly expands the geometric force. Section 2.4 discusses the physical meaning of the geometric force.

## 2.1 From GENERIC to SKG

## 2.1.1 Core Claim of GENERIC Theory

The core claim of GENERIC (General Equation for Non-Equilibrium Reversible-Irreversible Coupling) theory is that the dynamics of any non-equilibrium system decomposes into reversible and irreversible parts.

Let z be the state variables, taking values in the state space Z. The variable z can be a finite-dimensional vector (e.g., position and momentum of a mechanical system) or an infinitedimensional field $( \mathrm { e . g . }$ , density and velocity fields of a fluid). Let $E : \mathcal { Z }  \mathbb { R }$ be the energy functional and $S : { \mathcal { Z } }  \mathbb { R }$ the entropy functional. GENERIC theory asserts that the dynamical equation can be written as [14, 15]

$$
\dot { z } = L ( z ) \nabla E ( z ) + M ( z ) \nabla S ( z ) ,\tag{7}
$$

where $L ( z )$ is an antisymmetric matrix (or operator) and $M ( z )$ is a positive semi-definite symmetric matrix (or operator). ∇E and $\nabla { S }$ are the gradients of energy and entropy (in the appropriate functional sense).

## 2.1.2 Degeneracy Conditions

The GENERIC decomposition is meaningful because it satisfies two degeneracy conditions:

$$
L ( z ) \nabla S ( z ) = 0 , \quad M ( z ) \nabla E ( z ) = 0 .\tag{8}
$$

The first condition states: the reversible part does not produce entropy. The second states: the irreversible part does not destroy energy conservation.

These two conditions are the core of GENERIC theory. They encode energy conservation and the second law as geometric structures. Verification: along the dynamics, the energy rate of change is

$$
\frac { d E } { d t } = \langle \nabla E , \dot { z } \rangle = \langle \nabla E , L \nabla E \rangle + \langle \nabla E , M \nabla S \rangle .\tag{9}
$$

The first term vanishes because L is antisymmetric. The second vanishes because of the degeneracy condition $M \nabla E = 0$ . Hence energy is conserved. Along the dynamics, the entropy rate of change is

$$
\frac { d S } { d t } = \langle \nabla S , \dot { z } \rangle = \langle \nabla S , L \nabla E \rangle + \langle \nabla S , M \nabla S \rangle .\tag{10}
$$

The first term vanishes because of the degeneracy condition $L \nabla S = 0$ . The second is $\geq 0$ because M is positive semi-definite. Hence entropy does not decrease.

## 2.1.3 From GENERIC to SKG

SKG takes the symplectic–dissipative structure as a concrete instance of GENERIC. For mechanical systems, we take

$$
E = H , \quad S = S ( p ) , \quad L = \omega ^ { \sharp } , \quad M = g ^ { - 1 } ,\tag{11}
$$

where H is the Hamiltonian, $S ( p )$ is the entropy functional depending only on momentum, $\omega ^ { \sharp }$ is the map induced by the symplectic form, and $g ^ { - 1 }$ is the inverse metric. Substituting into (7):

$$
\dot { z } = \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H - g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S .\tag{12}
$$

This form agrees with Morrison’s Metriplectic flow [12].

## 2.1.4 Violation of the Degeneracy Condition

When $S = S ( p )$ , the entropy functional depends only on momentum $p ,$ not on position q. Hence

$$
\nabla S = ( 0 , \partial _ { p } S ) ,\tag{13}
$$

where $\partial _ { p } S$ is the partial derivative of S with respect to $p .$ The first degeneracy condition requires $\omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla S = 0$ . But the standard symplectic structure satisfies

$$
\omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla S = \omega ^ { \sharp } \left( { 0 \atop \partial _ { p } S } \right) = { \binom { \partial _ { p } S } { 0 } } \neq 0 .\tag{14}
$$

Here $\omega ^ { \sharp }$ in standard coordinates is $\left( \begin{array} { l l } { 0 } & { I } \\ { - I } & { 0 } \end{array} \right)$ . Acting on $( 0 , \partial _ { p } S ) ^ { \top }$ gives $( \partial _ { p } S , 0 ) ^ { \top }$ , which is nonzero.

The first degeneracy condition is violated. This is a structural conflict between $S = S ( p )$ and the standard symplectic structure. $S = S ( \boldsymbol { p } )$ is common in dissipative mechanics: entropy depending on momentum means dissipation acts on momentum (e.g., friction), not on position.

## 2.1.5 Resolution: Symplectic-Step Projection

The resolution is to project the symplectic step onto the orthogonal complement of the entropy gradient. Let

$$
P _ { \perp } = I - \frac { \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } } { \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } } ,\tag{15}
$$

where I is the identity matrix and $\| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } = \nabla S ^ { \top } \nabla S . \ P _ { \perp }$ is the orthogonal projection onto the orthogonal complement of ∇S. It satisfies $P _ { \bot } \nabla S = 0$ , and $P _ { \perp }$ is symmetric and idempotent $( P _ { \perp } ^ { 2 } = P _ { \perp } , P _ { \perp } ^ { \top } \overset { - } { = } P _ { \perp } )$

Correcting the symplectic step with $P _ { \perp }$ :

$$
\dot { z } = P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H - g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S .\tag{16}
$$

Verification of the degeneracy condition:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { ( P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \top } \nabla S = ( \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \top } P _ { \perp } ^ { \top } \nabla S = ( \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \top } P _ { \perp } \nabla S = 0 . } \end{array}\tag{17}
$$

The first equality uses $P _ { \perp } ^ { \top } = P _ { \perp }$ ; the second uses $P _ { \bot } \nabla S = 0$ . The degeneracy condition is restored.

Key insight: although this projection is introduced to resolve the degeneracy condition, it has no efect on the residual. Because the residual only involves the dissipative part $g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ and the symplectic step $P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H$ cancels exactly in the residual equation. This is detailed in Chapter 6.

## 2.2 SKG Octuple

## 2.2.1 Definition

Definition 2.1 (SKG System). An SKG system is the octuple

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \boldsymbol { S } = ( M , g , \omega , H , S , G , \Delta , \Pi ) , } \end{array}\tag{18}
$$

where:

• M: the configuration manifold, a finite-dimensional smooth manifold representing all possible configurations.

$g \colon$ the kinetic metric, a Riemannian metric on M mapping tangent vectors to cotangent vectors, equipping the state space with a metric structure.

• ω: the symplectic structure, a closed non-degenerate second-order antisymmetric tensor field on M, defining the geometry of conservative forces.

• H: the Hamiltonian, a smooth function H : M → R representing the total energy.

• S: the entropy functional, a smooth function $S : M \to \mathbb { R }$ representing the dissipative drive.

• G: the generator of the dissipative metric, encoding the parameters of the dissipative structure, to be calibrated from data.

• ∆: the invariant, a map $\Delta : M \to \mathbb { R } ^ { M }$ representing quantities invariant under the symmetry group.

• Π: the projection triple, $\Pi = ( \Pi _ { A } , P _ { \perp } , \Pi _ { E } )$ , denoting the axiom projection, symplectic-step projection, and conservation projection respectively.

## 2.2.2 Four Components and Levels of Laws

Among the eight components, four correspond to the four levels of laws:

Table 4: Four components of SKG and levels of laws
<table><tr><td>Component</td><td>Mathematical object</td><td>Law level</td><td>Function</td></tr><tr><td>ω</td><td>Symplectic form</td><td>Conservation law</td><td>Geometry of conservative forces</td></tr><tr><td>H,S</td><td>Scalar functions</td><td>Constitutive relation</td><td>Conservative quantity and dissipative drive</td></tr><tr><td> $\Delta$ </td><td>Invariant map</td><td>Symmetry group</td><td>Physically important differences</td></tr><tr><td>Ⅱ</td><td>Projection operators</td><td>Axiom system</td><td>Allowed states</td></tr></table>

The first three levels (conservation laws, symmetry groups, axiom systems) are entirely task-independent. They are objective properties of the system, independent of any downstream task. The fourth level (constitutive relations) is partially task-relevant. The specific form of the constitutive relation depends on the material, but the functional form is usually given a priori.

## 2.2.3 Prior Nature of the Four Components

The four components are given a priori. Conservation laws are given by Noether’s theorem [10, 11]. Symmetry groups are intrinsic to the system. Axioms are logically necessary. The functional forms of constitutive relations are given by physical laws.

Data are only used to calibrate $G ,$ the generator of the dissipative metric. G is the only part to be estimated. This design greatly simplifies the learning problem of SKG.

## 2.2.4 Objective–Task Decomposition of Energy

The SKG energy S decomposes into an objective part and a task part:

$$
S ( q ) = S _ { \mathrm { o b j } } ( q ) + S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } ( q ) ,\tag{19}
$$

where $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } }$ is given by physical laws (known) and $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ is learned from task data (to be estimated).

This decomposition decouples the inference flow and learning flow. The inference flow is

$$
\dot { q } = - g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } \nabla S _ { \mathrm { o b j } } - g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } \nabla S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } .\tag{20}
$$

The first term is completely known; the second is the learning target. Learning reduces to learning only the task energy. Chapter 9 rigorously establishes this decoupling.

## 2.3 Second-Order SKG Flow and Geometric Force

## 2.3.1 From First to Second Order

The first-order SKG flow (12) describes the overdamped limit. In systems with finite inertia, the dynamics is second-order. The second-order SKG flow is

$$
\nabla _ { \dot { z } } \dot { z } = P _ { \dot { \perp } } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H - g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S - \alpha \dot { z } ,\tag{21}
$$

where $\nabla _ { \dot { z } } \dot { z }$ is the covariant derivative and $\alpha > 0$ is the Rayleigh damping coeficient [49].

The left side $\nabla _ { \dot { z } } \dot { z }$ is the covariant derivative along the velocity field, given by the Levi-Civita connection [21, 22]. In coordinates:

$$
( \nabla _ { \dot { z } } \dot { z } ) ^ { i } = \ddot { z } ^ { i } + \Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } \dot { z } ^ { j } \dot { z } ^ { k } ,\tag{22}
$$

where $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i }$ are the Christofel symbols:

$$
\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } = { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } g ^ { i l } \left( \partial _ { j } g _ { l k } + \partial _ { k } g _ { l j } - \partial _ { l } g _ { j k } \right) .\tag{23}
$$

Here $g ^ { i l }$ are the inverse metric components satisfying $g ^ { i l } g _ { l j } = \delta _ { j } ^ { i } . \ \partial _ { j } = \partial / \partial z ^ { j }$

## 2.3.2 Explicit Expansion of the Geometric Force

Expanding the covariant derivative:

$$
\ddot { z } ^ { i } + \Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } \dot { z } ^ { j } \dot { z } ^ { k } = ( P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { i } - ( g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S ) ^ { i } - \alpha \dot { z } ^ { i } .\tag{24}
$$

The second term on the left, $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } \dot { z } ^ { j } \dot { z } ^ { k }$ , is the geometric force. It is determined by the derivatives of the metric $^ { g , }$ reflecting the non-Euclidean nature of the state-space geometry.

Physical meaning of the geometric force: as the state z evolves along a geodesic, the statedependence of the metric $g$ produces a correction to the acceleration through the connection term. In Euclidean coordinates the geometric force is zero; under a curved metric it is nonzero.

## 2.3.3 Meaning of the Three Forces on the Right

Equation (24) has three forces on the right.

Corrected symplectic force $P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H . \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H$ is the Hamiltonian vector field, determined by the symplectic structure ω and the energy gradient ∇H. $P _ { \perp }$ cuts of the component along $\nabla { S }$ , restoring the GENERIC degeneracy condition.

Dissipative force $- g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S . ~ - g ^ { - 1 }$ is the inverse metric; ∇S is the entropy gradient. The dissipative force points along entropy increase, driving the system toward maximum entropy.

Rayleigh damping $- \alpha \dot { z }$ . α is the damping coeficient; $- \alpha \dot { z }$ is a damping force proportional to velocity. This term introduces energy dissipation in second-order systems, ensuring convergence.

## 2.3.4 Two Limiting Cases of the Geometric Force

The geometric force takes limiting values in two cases.

Constant metric case $( J = I , \Sigma$ constant). Here $g$ is a constant matrix, $\partial _ { k } g = 0$ , so $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } = 0$ . The geometric force is zero. The second-order SKG flow reduces to

$$
\ddot { z } = P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H - g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S - \alpha \dot { z } .\tag{25}
$$

This is second-order dynamics in Euclidean space with no geometric correction.

State-dependent metric case $( J ( q )$ non-constant or $\Sigma ( q )$ state-dependent). Here g varies with state, $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } \neq 0$ . The geometric force cannot be omitted. It reflects the non-Euclidean nature of the coordinate system and is an intrinsic part of the inference geometric structure.

## 2.4 Physical Meaning of the Geometric Force

## 2.4.1 Geometric Force and Inertial Force

In classical mechanics, if one chooses a non-inertial reference frame, inertial forces $( \mathrm { e . g . }$ , Coriolis and centrifugal forces) appear. These are not real physical forces, but manifestations of the noninertial nature of the reference frame. Mathematically, inertial forces enter through Christofel symbols.

The geometric force in SKG has a similar nature. It is not an externally applied physical force, but a manifestation of the non-Euclidean nature of the state-space geometry. When the metric g is state-dependent, geodesic evolution produces an additional acceleration due to geometric curvature.

## 2.4.2 Geometric Force and Geodesic Deviation

Consider two neighboring states z and $z + \delta z$ , both evolving along geodesics. Their deviation δz satisfies the Jacobi field equation

$$
\frac { D ^ { 2 } \delta z } { d t ^ { 2 } } + R ( \dot { z } , \delta z ) \dot { z } = 0 ,\tag{26}
$$

where R is the Riemann curvature tensor. The geometric force $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } \dot { z } ^ { j } \dot { z } ^ { k }$ is the Christofel term, which is connected to the curvature R through the Bianchi identities. The growth rate of geodesic deviation is controlled by the curvature. This is the extrinsic connection between the geometric force and the curvature.

## 2.4.3 Role of Geometric Force in the Residual Equation

The residual equation is central to data calibration in the SKG framework. It is

$$
r = \ddot { z } + \Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } ) - P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H + \alpha \dot { z } ,\tag{27}
$$

where $\Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } )$ is the geometric force. When the model is exact,

$$
r = - g ( z ) \nabla S ( z ) .\tag{28}
$$

The symplectic part $P _ { \bot } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H$ cancels exactly in the residual (by the definition of the symplecticstep projection $P _ { \perp } \nabla S = 0 )$ . The geometric force $\Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } )$ does not cancel; it is part of the residual, produced by the state-dependence of the metric $g .$

When the metric is state-dependent $( J ( q )$ non-constant or $\Sigma ( q )$ state-dependent), $\Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } ) \neq 0$ and the geometric force contributes to the residual. When $J = I$ and Σ constant, $\Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } ) = 0$ and the geometric force vanishes, simplifying the residual equation. The derivation of the onedimensional observable formula (Proposition 6.3) is carried out precisely in this degenerate case where the geometric force vanishes.

Chapter 6 discusses the residual equation, the geometric force, and identifiability in detail.

## 2.5 Chapter Summary

This chapter establishes the geometric foundations of SKG. Starting from GENERIC theory, we explained the structure of the symplectic–dissipative decomposition and the degeneracy conditions, pointed out the structural conflict between $S = S ( p )$ and the standard symplectic structure, and introduced the symplectic-step projection as the resolution. We defined the SKG octuple $\begin{array} { r } { \boldsymbol { S } = ( M , g , \omega , H , S , G , \Delta , \Pi ) } \end{array}$ , in which the four components correspond to the four levels of laws. We established the second-order SKG flow with explicit expansion of the geometric force $\Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } )$ . We discussed the behavior of the geometric force in the two limiting cases of constant and state-dependent metrics, and its role in the residual equation.

The core formulas are:

• GENERIC decomposition: $\dot { z } = L \nabla E + M \nabla S ;$

• Degeneracy conditions: $\begin{array} { r } { L \nabla S = 0 , M \nabla E = 0 ; } \end{array}$

• Symplectic-step projection: $P _ { \perp } = I - \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } / \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } ;$

• Second-order SKG flow: $\nabla _ { \dot { z } } \dot { z } = P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H - g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S - \alpha \dot { z } ;$

• Geometric force expansion: $\ddot { z } ^ { i } + \Gamma _ { i k } ^ { i } \dot { z } ^ { j } \dot { z } ^ { k } = ( P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { i } - ( g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S ) ^ { i } - \alpha \dot { z } ^ { i } ;$

• Christofel symbols: $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } = { \textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } } g ^ { i l } ( \partial _ { j } g _ { l k } + \partial _ { k } g _ { l j } - \partial _ { l } g _ { j k } )$

## 3 Invariant-Induced Metrics and Intrinsic Metric Reconstruction

This chapter establishes the metric construction theory in the SKG framework. The core question is: how to construct a metric on the state space from a symmetry group and invariants? The answer proceeds in two steps. First (Sections 3.1–3.4), we establish the forward construction: invariants are pulled back through the Jacobian to yield the state-space metric. Second (Section 3.5), we establish the inverse reconstruction: given the state-space metric and multiple embeddings, reconstruct the intrinsic metric on the invariant space. Section 3.6 argues the Fisher origin of the kernel bandwidth matrix, giving an objective determination method for the intrinsic metric. Section 3.7 summarizes.

## 3.1 Metric Generated by Invariants

## 3.1.1 Problem

Let the state space be $\mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ , with state variable $q \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ . Define a metric $g ( q )$ on the state space as a $d \times d$ symmetric positive definite matrix field. The metric plays three roles in SKG: defining distances between states, defining OOD distances, and defining kernel functions. The question is: how to determine $g ( q ) ?$

Direct parameterization of $g ( q ) \ { \mathrm { ( e . g . } }$ , using a neural network to output a d × d matrix) sufers from coordinate dependence. Under a coordinate transformation $q ^ { \prime } = \phi ( q )$ , the metric transforms as

$$
g ^ { \prime } ( q ^ { \prime } ) = \left( \frac { \partial q } { \partial q ^ { \prime } } \right) ^ { \top } g ( q ) \left( \frac { \partial q } { \partial q ^ { \prime } } \right) ,\tag{29}
$$

where $\partial q / \partial q ^ { \prime }$ is the Jacobian of the coordinate transformation. The same physical state has diferent metric values in diferent coordinate systems. This means that a directly parameterized metric is not a physical quantity but an artifact of the coordinate system.

## 3.1.2 Symmetry Group and Invariants

Let a symmetry group G act on the state space. The group action is a smooth map $T : G \times \mathbb { R } ^ { d } $ $\mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ , denoted $T _ { g } ( q ) = T ( g , q )$ , satisfying the group action axioms: $T _ { e } = \mathrm { i d }$ (e is the identity), $T _ { g _ { 1 } } T _ { g _ { 2 } } = T _ { g _ { 1 } g _ { 2 } }$

Definition 3.1 (Invariant). Let a symmetry group $G$ act on the state space $\mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ . A map $\Delta$ : $\mathbb { R } ^ { d } \to \mathbb { R } ^ { M }$ is called an invariant under this group action $i f$

$$
\Delta ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = \Delta ( q ) , \quad \forall g \in G , \forall q \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d } .\tag{30}
$$

M is called the dimension of the invariant.

The invariant maps equivalent states under the group action to the same point. $\operatorname { I f } q _ { 1 }$ and $q _ { 2 }$ are equivalent under the group action $\left( q _ { 2 } = T _ { g } \cdot q _ { 1 } \right)$ , then $\Delta ( q _ { 1 } ) = \Delta ( q _ { 2 } )$ . The invariant extracts the “essential” information of the state under the group action.

Example 3.2 (Invariant of the rotation group). Let $G = S O ( d )$ act on $\mathbb { R } ^ { d } \colon T _ { R } \cdot q = R q$ , where R is $a \ d \times d$ orthogonal matrix. Then the invariant is $\Delta ( q ) = \| q \| ^ { 2 } = q ^ { \top } q , M = 1$ . Any rotation preserves the norm.

Example 3.3 (Invariant of the translation group). Let $G = \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ act on $\mathbb { R } ^ { d } \colon T _ { t } \cdot q = q + t$ . Then the invariant is in diference form. If there are two points $q _ { 1 } , q _ { 2 }$ , then $\Delta ( q _ { 1 } , q _ { 2 } ) = q _ { 1 } - q _ { 2 }$ is translation-invariant. This requires the state space to be $\mathbb { R } ^ { 2 d }$ (coordinates of two points).

## 3.1.3 Equivariance Requirement of the Metric

A direct consequence of constructing the metric from invariants is that the metric is equivariant under the group action. Let $g _ { \Delta }$ be a metric on the invariant space $\mathbb { R } ^ { M }$ , pulled back to the state space through the invariant map:

$$
g ( q ) = { J ( q ) } ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) ) { J ( q ) } ,\tag{31}
$$

where $J ( q ) = \partial \Delta / \partial q \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times d }$ is the invariant Jacobian. If $g _ { \Delta }$ is invariant under the group action, then g is equivariant on the state space.

Specifically, let the representation $\rho : G \to G L ( \mathbb { R } ^ { d } )$ of the group G satisfy $T _ { g } \cdot q = \rho ( g ) q$ Then

$$
g ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } .\tag{32}
$$

This states that the metric transforms as a tensor under the group action. If $\rho ( g )$ is orthogonal $( \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } = \rho ( g ) )$ , the equivariance simplifies to a similarity transformation.

## 3.1.4 Why State-Dependence Is Required

The metric must be state-dependent. This is a hard requirement of Riemannian geometry [21, 22]. Reasons:

Physical reason. Diferent states have diferent local behaviors. For example, a robotic arm near a singular configuration and far from it have drastically diferent response characteristics. The metric must reflect this state-dependence.

Statistical reason. Data distributions have diferent densities in diferent regions. If the metric is constant, it cannot reflect the local statistical structure of the data.

Geometric reason. The invariant space is usually a curved manifold $\left( \mathrm { e . g . } \right.$ ., the quotient space $M / G$ of a symmetry group action). Metrics on curved manifolds must depend on position.

## 3.2 How Invariants Enter the Metric

## 3.2.1 Kernel Function Method

Let $\kappa : \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \to \mathbb { R }$ be a kernel function. It satisfies symmetry $\kappa ( x , y ) = \kappa ( y , x )$ and positive definiteness (for any finite set of points $\{ x _ { i } \}$ and coeficients $\{ c _ { i } \} , \sum _ { i , j } c _ { i } c _ { j } \kappa ( x _ { i } , x _ { j } ) \geq 0 )$

Invariants enter the metric through the Hessian of the kernel at a reference point. Specifically, define the invariant diference

$$
\Delta ( z ) = i n v a r i a n t s ( z ) - i n v a r i a n t s ( z _ { r e f } ) ,\tag{33}
$$

where $z _ { r e f }$ is a reference state. When $\Delta = 0$ , the state is equivalent to the reference state under the symmetry group. When $\Delta \neq 0$ , the diference gives the physically meaningful diference between the two states.

The metric g is the Hessian of the kernel κ at $\Delta = 0$ . The reasons for choosing the Hessian are twofold. First, the Hessian is a local quadratic approximation, giving the second-order structure of distances. Second, the kernel attains its maximum at $\Delta = 0$ , the Hessian is negative definite, and after taking the negative sign we obtain a positive definite metric.

## 3.2.2 Explicit Form of the Gaussian Kernel

Let the kernel be the Gaussian kernel

$$
\kappa ( \Delta ) = \exp \left( - \frac { 1 } { 2 } \Delta ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } \Delta \right) ,\tag{34}
$$

where $\Sigma \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times M }$ is a positive definite matrix called the kernel bandwidth matrix. The Gaussian kernel attains its maximum $\kappa ( 0 ) = 1$ at $\Delta = 0$

First derivative with respect to $\Delta { \mathrm { : } }$

$$
\partial _ { \Delta } \kappa = - \Sigma ^ { - 1 } \Delta \cdot \kappa ( \Delta ) .\tag{35}
$$

At $\Delta = 0 , \partial _ { \Delta } \kappa | _ { 0 } = 0$ (odd derivatives vanish).

Second derivative with respect to $\Delta { : }$

$$
\partial _ { \Delta } ^ { 2 } \kappa = ( - \Sigma ^ { - 1 } + \Sigma ^ { - 1 } \Delta \Delta ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } ) \kappa ( \Delta ) .\tag{36}
$$

At $\Delta = 0 , \kappa ( 0 ) = 1 , \Delta = 0$ , hence

$$
\partial _ { \Delta } ^ { 2 } \kappa | _ { 0 } = - \Sigma ^ { - 1 } .\tag{37}
$$

The Hessian is negative definite at $\Delta = 0$ because $\Sigma ^ { - 1 }$ is positive definite.

## 3.2.3 Why Take the Negative Sign

The metric must be positive definite, but the kernel Hessian is negative definite. Taking the negative sign:

$$
g _ { \Delta } = - \partial _ { \Delta } ^ { 2 } \kappa | _ { 0 } = \Sigma ^ { - 1 } .\tag{38}
$$

The negative sign is not a formal choice. It is a direct consequence of the fact that the kernel attains its maximum at $\Delta = 0$ , so the Hessian is negative definite. If the kernel attained a minimum at $\Delta \ : = \ : 0$ , the Hessian would be positive definite and no negative sign would be needed. But the Gaussian kernel attains a maximum at $\Delta = 0$ , so we must take the negative sign.

## 3.3 Chain Rule and Jacobian

## 3.3.1 Chain Rule from $\Delta$ to q

Let κ have argument $\Delta ( q )$ . The composite function $\kappa \circ \Delta$ , when diferentiated once with respect to q:

$$
\partial _ { q } \kappa = \partial _ { \Delta } \kappa \cdot J ,\tag{39}
$$

where $J = { \partial \Delta } / { \partial q } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times d }$ is the invariant Jacobian. The $( a , i )$ element of J is $\partial \Delta ^ { a } / \partial q ^ { i }$

Second derivative with respect to $q \colon$

$$
\partial _ { q } ^ { 2 } \kappa = J ^ { \top } \partial _ { \Delta } ^ { 2 } \kappa \cdot J + \partial _ { \Delta } \kappa \cdot \partial _ { q } ^ { 2 } \Delta .\tag{40}
$$

This follows from the chain rule and the product rule. The first term is the pullback of the ∆-space Hessian through the Jacobian. The second term is the contribution of the $\Delta { \mathrm { - s p a c e } }$ gradient through the Hessian of $\Delta$

At $\Delta = 0 , \partial _ { \Delta } \kappa | _ { 0 } = 0$ (from Section 3.2), so the second term vanishes. The first term gives

$$
\partial _ { q } ^ { 2 } \kappa | _ { 0 } = J ^ { \top } \partial _ { \Delta } ^ { 2 } \kappa | _ { 0 } \cdot J = - J ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J .\tag{41}
$$

## 3.3.2 Kernel Hessian Gives the Metric

Theorem 3.4 (Kernel Hessian gives the metric). Let the kernel $\kappa ( \Delta )$ attain its maximum at $\Delta = 0$ , with Hessian $\partial _ { \Delta } ^ { 2 } \kappa | _ { 0 } = - \Sigma ^ { - 1 }$ , where $\Sigma \in \overset { \cdot } { \mathbb { R } } ^ { M \times M }$ is positive definite. Let $J ( q ) = \partial \Delta / \partial q$ be the invariant Jacobian. Then the state-space metric

$$
g ( q ) = - \partial _ { q } ^ { 2 } \kappa | _ { 0 } = J ( q ) ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J ( q )\tag{42}
$$

is positive semi-definite.

Proof. By the chain rule (41), $\partial _ { q } ^ { 2 } \kappa | _ { 0 } = - J ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J$ . Taking the negative sign gives $g ( q ) =$ $J ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J$

Positive semi-definiteness. For any tangent vector $v \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ 2

$$
\boldsymbol { v } ^ { \top } \boldsymbol { g } ( \boldsymbol { q } ) \boldsymbol { v } = \boldsymbol { v } ^ { \top } \boldsymbol { J } ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } \boldsymbol { J } \boldsymbol { v } = ( \boldsymbol { J } \boldsymbol { v } ) ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } ( \boldsymbol { J } \boldsymbol { v } ) .\tag{43}
$$

Since $\Sigma ^ { - 1 }$ is positive definite, $( J v ) ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } ( J v ) \geq 0$ . Equality holds if $J v = 0$

Positive definiteness. If J is column-full-rank $( r a n k ( J ) = d ,$ requiring $M \geq d )$ , then $J v = 0 { \mathrm { ~ i f f ~ } } v = 0$ , and $g$ is positive definite. If $r a n k ( J ) < d ,$ then g is rank-deficient and needs regularization (Section 3.4). □

Remark 3.5 (Covariant and contravariant metrics). In Riemannian geometry, the covariant metric $g _ { i j }$ maps tangent vectors to cotangent vectors, and the contravariant metric $g ^ { i j }$ maps cotangent vectors to tangent vectors. They are inverses: $g _ { i j } g ^ { j k } = \delta _ { i } ^ { k } ~ [ 2 1 , ~ 2 2 ]$

The covariant metric of this paper is

$$
g = J ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J + \varepsilon _ { G } I ,\tag{44}
$$

used to define state distances, kernel functions, OOD distances, and residuals. The contravariant metric is

$$
\boldsymbol { g } ^ { - 1 } = ( \boldsymbol { J } ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } \boldsymbol { J } + \varepsilon _ { G } \boldsymbol { I } ) ^ { - 1 } ,\tag{45}
$$

used for gradient updates $\dot { q } = - g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$

Distinguishing the two is a prerequisite for mathematical correctness. Mistaking g for the gradient update direction yields the wrong direction. Mistaking $g ^ { - 1 }$ for distance definition yields negative distances.

## 3.4 Rank Deficiency and Regularization

## 3.4.1 Origin of Rank Deficiency

If $J \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times d }$ with $M < d$ (fewer invariants than state-space dimensions), then $r a n k ( J ) \leq M <$ d. Then $g = J ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J$ is rank-deficient, ran $k ( g ) \leq M < d .$ g has a $d - M$ dimensional null space.

Meaning of the null space: there exists a direction $v \in \ker ( g ) \ ( { \mathrm { i . e . , ~ } } J v = 0 )$ along which state changes do not change the invariants. This direction is the direction of the group action (the direction of states equivalent under the symmetry group). Physically, diferences along this direction are unimportant (absorbed by the symmetry group). Mathematically, the metric in this direction is undefined.

## 3.4.2 Regularization

The solution is to add a regularization term to $g \colon$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { g ( \boldsymbol { q } ) = \boldsymbol { J } ( \boldsymbol { q } ) ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } \boldsymbol { J } ( \boldsymbol { q } ) + \varepsilon _ { G } \boldsymbol { I } , } \end{array}\tag{46}
$$

where $\varepsilon _ { G } > 0$ is the regularization constant and I is the $d \times d$ identity matrix.

The regularization term $\varepsilon _ { G } I$ fills the null space: for $v \in \ker ( J )$

$$
v ^ { \top } g v = v ^ { \top } J ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J v + \varepsilon { _ { G } v ^ { \top } } I v = 0 + \varepsilon _ { G } \| v \| ^ { 2 } > 0 .\tag{47}
$$

Thus $g$ is positive definite in the null-space directions as well. $g$ is positive definite on the whole space and invertible.

## 3.4.3 Regularization and Equivariance

The efect of the regularization term $\varepsilon _ { G } I$ on equivariance depends on the behavior of the group representation.

Orthogonal representation $( \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } = I \qquad $ , equivalent to $\rho ( g )$ orthogonal). Then

$$
\varepsilon _ { G } I { \mathrm { ~ i s ~ i n v a r i a n t ~ u n d e r ~ t h e ~ g r o u p ~ a c t i o n } } .\tag{48}
$$

Verification: $\rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } ( \varepsilon _ { G } I ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } = \varepsilon _ { G } \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } = \varepsilon _ { G } I$ . Hence the regularization term does not break equivariance.

Non-orthogonal representation $( \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } \neq I )$ . Then $\rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } ( \varepsilon _ { G } I ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } \neq \varepsilon _ { G } I$ and the regularization term breaks equivariance.

The equivariance proofs in this paper are restricted to orthogonal representations. Orthogonal representations cover the rotation group $S O ( d )$ , the rigid-body motion group $S E ( 3 )$ , and the translation group $\mathbb { R } ^ { d } [ 2 6 , 2 7 , 2 8 ]$ . The regularization design for non-orthogonal representations is an open problem in Chapter 11.

We emphasize that orthogonality is a property of the representation, independent of whether the group is compact. A compact group may have a non-orthogonal representation $( \mathrm { e . g . }$ , the scale group $\mathbb { R } ^ { + } )$ ; a non-compact group may have an orthogonal representation $( \mathrm { e . g . }$ , the translation group R<sup>d</sup>).

## 3.5 Intrinsic Metric Reconstruction (Constant Case)

## 3.5.1 Origin of the Problem

Section 3.4 established the forward construction: given the intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta }$ , obtain the statespace metric $g = J ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J + \varepsilon _ { G } I$ . This section studies the inverse problem: given the state-space metric g and multiple embeddings $\{ J ^ { ( k ) } \} _ { k = 1 } ^ { K }$ , reconstruct the intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta }$

The motivation for the inverse problem is threefold. First, transfer learning: if the intrinsic metric can be reconstructed from multiple systems, it can be transferred across systems. Second, coordinate-independence: the intrinsic metric is a geometric object on the invariant space, independent of the state-space coordinate system. Third, OOD distance: OOD distances are more naturally defined on the invariant space.

## 3.5.2 Multiple Embeddings

Definition 3.6 (Multiple embeddings). Let the invariant space $b e \mathbb { R } ^ { M }$ . Multiple embeddings are K maps

$$
\Delta ^ { ( k ) } : \mathbb { R } ^ { d }  \mathbb { R } ^ { M } , \quad k = 1 , \dots , K ,\tag{49}
$$

each giving a Jacobian

$$
J ^ { ( k ) } = \frac { \partial \Delta ^ { ( k ) } } { \partial q } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times d } .\tag{50}
$$

Meaning of multiple embeddings: the same physical system can have diferent invariant representations. Three cases:

Case A (coordinate representation). $\Delta ^ { ( k ) }$ is a diferent coordinate representation of the same invariant space. For example, rotation invariants can be represented in spherical or Cartesian coordinates.

Case B (functional form). $\Delta ^ { ( k ) }$ is a diferent functional form of the same invariants. For example, $\Delta ^ { ( 1 ) } ( q ) = \| q \| , \Delta ^ { ( 2 ) } ( q ) = \| q \| ^ { 2 }$

Case C (diferent invariants). $\Delta ^ { ( k ) }$ is a diferent set of invariants. For example, $\Delta ^ { ( 1 ) }$ is a rotation invariant, $\Delta ^ { ( 2 ) }$ is a scale invariant.

The inverse reconstruction theory of this paper applies to all three cases. The key assumption is that all embeddings map to the same M-dimensional invariant space.

## 3.5.3 Reference Embedding and Normalization

Choose a reference embedding $k = 1$ , and set $R _ { 1 } = I$ (I is the $M \times M$ identity matrix). The coordinate transformation matrices of the other embeddings are determined by the relative transformation between embeddings:

$$
R _ { k } = J ^ { ( k ) } ( J ^ { ( 1 ) } ) ^ { + } ,\tag{51}
$$

where $( J ^ { ( 1 ) } ) ^ { + }$ is the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse of $J ^ { ( 1 ) }$ [52]. The pseudoinverse is defined as

$$
( J ^ { ( 1 ) } ) ^ { + } = \left( ( J ^ { ( 1 ) } ) ^ { \top } J ^ { ( 1 ) } \right) ^ { - 1 } ( J ^ { ( 1 ) } ) ^ { \top } ,\tag{52}
$$

assuming $J ^ { ( 1 ) }$ is column-full-rank $( r a n k ( J ^ { ( 1 ) } ) = d ;$ , requiring $M \geq d )$

Meaning of $R _ { k } \colon R _ { k }$ is the coordinate transformation matrix from embedding 1’s coordinate system to embedding k’s coordinate system. If $J ^ { ( 1 ) }$ is row-full-rank and $M \leq d , R _ { k }$ may also be full-rank. If $J ^ { ( 1 ) }$ is not full-rank, $R _ { k }$ should be understood in the least-squares sense.

The normalized Jacobian is

$$
\widetilde { J } ^ { ( k ) } = R _ { k } ^ { - 1 } J ^ { ( k ) } .\tag{53}
$$

The purpose of normalization is to align all embeddings’ Jacobians to the same coordinate system. After normalization, all embeddings share the same intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta }$

## 3.5.4 Consistency Condition

Definition 3.7 (Consistency condition). The intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta }$ must satisfy

$$
g = ( \widetilde { J } ^ { ( k ) } ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } \widetilde { J } ^ { ( k ) } , \quad k = 1 , \dots , K .\tag{54}
$$

This is K equations constraining the same $g _ { \Delta }$

Meaning of the consistency condition: if all embeddings map to the same invariant space, the state-space metrics they pull back must be identical. This is because the state-space metric $g$ is a physical quantity, independent of the choice of embedding.

## 3.5.5 Dimension Analysis

g is a d × d symmetric matrix with $d ( d + 1 ) / 2$ independent components. $g _ { \Delta }$ is an $M \times M$ symmetric matrix with $M ( M + 1 ) / 2$ independent components. $J ^ { ( k ) }$ is an $M \times d$ matrix with Md components.

Number of equations: $K \cdot d ( d + 1 ) / 2$ (each embedding gives $d ( d + 1 ) / 2$ equations). Number of unknowns: $M ( M + 1 ) / 2$

If $K \cdot d ( d + 1 ) / 2 \geq M ( M + 1 ) / 2$ , the number of equations is not less than the number of unknowns, potentially solvable. But comparing equation and unknown counts is only a necessary condition; the true condition is the rank condition (Section 3.5.6).

## 3.5.6 Vectorization and Solvability

Definition 3.8 (Vectorization of symmetric matrices). For a symmetric matrix $A \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n \times n }$ define vech $( A ) \in \dot { \mathbb { R } } ^ { n ( n + 1 ) / 2 }$ as the vector of upper-triangular components. Specifically, the $( i , j )$ element of vech(A) (for $i \leq j )$ is $A _ { i j }$ , arranged in lexicographic order.

Diference between vech and vec: $v e c ( A )$ takes all $n ^ { 2 }$ elements; vech(A) takes only the $n ( n + 1 ) / 2$ upper-triangular elements. A symmetric matrix has only $n ( n + 1 ) / 2$ independent components, so vech is the natural vectorization.

The vectorized equation:

$$
v e c h ( g ) = A ^ { ( k ) } v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) , \quad k = 1 , \ldots , K ,\tag{55}
$$

where $A ^ { ( k ) } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d ( d + 1 ) / 2 \times M ( M + 1 ) / 2 }$ is constructed from $J ^ { ( k ) }$ . The explicit construction is in Appendix A.

Stacking all embeddings:

$$
\underbrace { \left( \begin{array} { c } { { v e c h ( g ) } } \\ { { \vdots } } \\ { { v e c h ( g ) } } \end{array} \right) } _ { K { \mathrm { ~ t i m e s } } } = \underbrace { \left( \begin{array} { c } { { A ^ { ( 1 ) } } } \\ { { \vdots } } \\ { { A ^ { ( K ) } } } \end{array} \right) } _ { \mathcal { A } } { v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) } ,\tag{56}
$$

where $\mathcal { A } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { K d ( d + 1 ) / 2 \times M ( M + 1 ) / 2 }$ is the stacked vectorization matrix.

Theorem 3.9 (Solvability rank condition). The intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta }$ is uniquely solvable if and only if

$$
r a n k ( { \cal A } ) = \frac { M ( M + 1 ) } { 2 } .\tag{57}
$$

Proof. Equation (56) is a linear system $\boldsymbol { \mathcal { A } } \boldsymbol { x } = \boldsymbol { b }$ , where $x = v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } )$ and b is the stacked $v e c h ( g )$

Suficiency. If $r a n k ( { \cal A } ) = { \cal M } ( M + 1 ) / 2$ , the columns of A are linearly independent, ker $\left( \mathcal { A } \right) = \left\{ 0 \right\}$ . Let $x _ { 1 } , x _ { 2 }$ be two solutions satisfying $\mathcal { A } \boldsymbol { x } _ { 1 } = \mathcal { A } \boldsymbol { x } _ { 2 } = \boldsymbol { b }$ . Then $\mathcal { A } ( x _ { 1 } - x _ { 2 } ) = 0$ , so $x _ { 1 } - x _ { 2 } \in \ker ( \mathcal { A } ) = \{ 0 \}$ , hence $x _ { 1 } = x _ { 2 }$ . The solution is unique.

Necessity. If ran $k ( \mathcal { A } ) < M ( M + 1 ) / 2$ , then ker $( A ) \neq \{ 0 \}$ . Take $x _ { 0 } \in \ker ( \mathcal { A } )$ with $x _ { 0 } \neq 0$ $\mathrm { ~ I f ~ } x ^ { \ast }$ is one solution, then $x ^ { * } + t x _ { 0 }$ is a solution for all $t \in \mathbb { R }$ , so the solution is not unique.

Hence uniqueness holds if $r a n k ( \mathcal { A } ) = M ( M + 1 ) / 2$

Proposition 3.10 (Equivalent form of the rank condition). The rank condition $r a n k ( \mathcal { A } ) =$ $M ( M + 1 ) / 2$ is equivalent to: there is no nonzero symmetric matrix $X \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times M }$ such that

$$
( \widetilde { J } ^ { ( k ) } ) ^ { \top } X \widetilde { J } ^ { ( k ) } = 0 , \quad \forall k = 1 , \dots , K .\tag{58}
$$

Proof. The column space of A has dimension less than $M ( M + 1 ) / 2$ if the columns of A are linearly dependent, i.e., there exists a nonzero $x \in \mathbb { R } ^ { \dot { M } ( M + 1 ) / 2 }$ such that $\mathit { A x } \ : = \ : 0$ . In vectorization terms: there exists a nonzero symmetric matrix X such that $( \widetilde J ^ { ( k ) } ) ^ { \top } X \widetilde J ^ { ( k ) } = 0$ for all k. Equivalently, the null space of $g _ { \Delta }$ (in the symmetric matrix space) has nonzero intersection with the pullback kernels of all embeddings:

$$
\bigcap _ { k = 1 } ^ { K } \ker \left( ( \widetilde { J } ^ { ( k ) } ) ^ { \top } ( \cdot ) \widetilde { J } ^ { ( k ) } \right) \neq \{ 0 \} .\tag{59}
$$

Hence the rank condition is equivalent to this intersection being zero.

## 3.5.7 Consistency Condition

Theorem 3.11 (Consistency criterion). Multiple embeddings are consistent (there exists $g _ { \Delta }$ satisfying all equations) if and only if vech $\iota ( g ) \in C o l ( \mathcal { A } )$ , where $C o l ( \mathcal { A } )$ is the column space of A.

Proof. The linear system $A x = b$ has a solution if $b \in C o l ( \mathcal { A } )$ . This is a standard result of linear algebra [52]. □

Corollary 3.12 (Inconsistency criterion). If $v e c h ( g ) \notin C o l ( \mathcal { A } )$ , the data are inconsistent. In this case, use the least-squares approximate solution rather than the exact solution.

Remark 3.13 (Sources of inconsistency). Data inconsistency has three possible sources. First, $J ^ { ( k ) }$ is inaccurate (numerical diferentiation or model error). Second, g is noisy (observation error). Third, the number of embeddings is insuficient (K too small). In engineering, the degree of inconsistency is assessed by checking the residual $\lVert A A ^ { + } v e c h ( g ) - v e c h ( g ) \rVert$ . A large residual suggests increasing the number of embeddings or improving data quality.

## 3.5.8 Uniqueness and Condition Number

Theorem 3.14 (Uniqueness). If $r a n k ( \cal { A } ) = M ( M + 1 ) / 2$ , then $g _ { \Delta }$ is unique. If $r a n k ( \mathcal { A } ) <$ $M ( M { + } 1 ) / 2$ , then $g _ { \Delta }$ has infinitely many solutions, and the solution space dimension is $\ker ( \mathcal { A } ) \ d s$ dimension, i.e., $M ( M + 1 ) / 2 - r a n k ( \mathcal { A } )$

Proof. Directly from the proof of Theorem 3.9.

Uniqueness does not equal identifiability. If the condition number $\kappa ( \mathcal { A } ) = \sigma _ { \mathrm { m a x } } ( \mathcal { A } ) / \sigma _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ( \mathcal { A } )$ of A is large, the solution is sensitive to noise. The error propagation bound is

$$
\frac { \| \delta v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) \| } { \| v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) \| } \leq \kappa ( \boldsymbol { \mathcal { A } } ) \cdot \frac { \| \delta v e c h ( g ) \| } { \| v e c h ( g ) \| } .\tag{60}
$$

This follows directly from singular value decomposition.

The empirical thresholds are:

## 3.5.9 Underdetermined Case

When rank $\because ( A ) < M ( M + 1 ) / 2 , g _ { \Delta }$ has infinitely many solutions. Three regularization criteria: Minimum-norm solution. Choose the solution minimizing $\| g _ { \Delta } \| _ { F } \mathbf { : }$

$$
v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) = \mathcal { A } ^ { + } v e c h ( g ) .\tag{61}
$$

$\mathcal { A } ^ { + }$ is the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse. This solution has minimum norm among all solutions satisfying $A x = b $

Ridge regression. Balance fitting error and solution norm:

$$
v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) = ( A ^ { \top } A + \lambda I ) ^ { - 1 } A ^ { \top } v e c h ( g ) ,\tag{62}
$$

where $\lambda > 0$ is the regularization coeficient. As $\lambda \to 0$ this reduces to least squares; as $\lambda \to \infty$ the solution tends to zero.

Sparse regularization. If $g _ { \Delta }$ is known to be sparse, use Lasso:

$$
v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) = \arg \operatorname* { m i n } _ { x } \| \mathcal { A } x - v e c h ( g ) \| ^ { 2 } + \lambda \| x \| _ { 1 } .\tag{63}
$$

The $\ell _ { 1 }$ norm induces sparse solutions.

## 3.5.10 Suficient Number of Embeddings

For the rank condition to hold, the number of embeddings K must satisfy at least

$$
K _ { \mathrm { m i n } } \geq \frac { M ( M + 1 ) / 2 } { d ( d + 1 ) / 2 } .\tag{64}
$$

This follows from the dimension analysis. It is a necessary condition, not suficient. The embeddings must be suficiently “diverse” to span the entire $\dot { \mathbb { R } } ^ { M ( M + 1 ) / 2 }$ . If the embeddings are linearly dependent, the rank may be deficient even for large K.

## 3.5.11 Solution Methods

Method 1 (least squares). If data are noise-free:

$$
v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) = ( A ^ { \top } A ) ^ { - 1 } A ^ { \top } v e c h ( g ) .\tag{65}
$$

If data are noisy: use ridge regression (62).

Method 2 (Cholesky parameterization). The unconstrained least-squares solution may not be positive definite. Let $g _ { \Delta } = L L ^ { \top }$ with L lower triangular and positive diagonal. Perform unconstrained optimization over L:

$$
\operatorname* { m i n } _ { L } \| A v e c h ( L L ^ { \top } ) - v e c h ( g ) \| ^ { 2 } + \lambda \| L \| _ { F } ^ { 2 } .\tag{66}
$$

$L L ^ { \top }$ is automatically positive definite. No projection needed. The disadvantage is nonconvexity, potentially with local minima.

Method 3 (manifold optimization). Perform Riemannian gradient descent on the positive definite matrix manifold $\mathcal { P } _ { M }$ [47]:

$$
g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( t + 1 ) } = R e t r _ { g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( t ) } } \left( - \eta \cdot g r a d \mathcal { L } ( g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( t ) } ) \right) ,\tag{67}
$$

where Retr is the retraction map, gradL is the Riemannian gradient, and $\eta$ is the learning rate. Preserves positive definiteness. On the positive definite matrix manifold, if the objective function is convex in $g _ { \Delta }$ , then Riemannian gradient descent converges globally to the optimum. Recommended as the primary method.

Selection criteria:

## 3.5.12 Engineering Applications

OOD distance. OOD distance is measured in the intrinsic space:

$$
d _ { O O D } ( \Delta _ { * } , \Delta _ { I D } ) = \sqrt { ( \Delta _ { * } - \Delta _ { I D } ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { * } - \Delta _ { I D } ) } .\tag{68}
$$

This is a local approximation of the geodesic distance. When $g _ { \Delta }$ is state-dependent, the exact OOD distance is the geodesic distance:

$$
d _ { g e o } ( \Delta _ { * } , \Delta _ { I D } ) = \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \gamma } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \sqrt { g _ { \Delta } ( \gamma ( t ) ) ( \dot { \gamma } ( t ) , \dot { \gamma } ( t ) ) } d t ,\tag{69}
$$

where $\gamma : [ 0 , 1 ] \to \mathbb { R } ^ { M }$ is a smooth curve from $\Delta .$ <sub>∗</sub> to $\Delta _ { I D }$ , with $\gamma ( 0 ) = \Delta _ { * } , \gamma ( 1 ) = \Delta _ { I D }$ . The geodesic is the curve minimizing the integral.

The Mahalanobis distance approximation (68) is valid when the curvature is small and the distance is close. The error is $O ( \| R _ { \Delta } \| \cdot \| \delta \| ^ { 3 } )$ , where $R _ { \Delta }$ is the Riemann curvature tensor of the invariant space and δ is the distance between the two points [22].

Transfer learning.

Theorem 3.15 (Transferability). If the intrinsic metrics of two systems are identical $( g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( 1 ) }$ = $g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( 2 ) } )$ , then the invariant spaces are the same and the metric can be transferred across systems.

Proof. By the assumption $g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( 1 ) } = g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( 2 ) }$ , the two systems’ intrinsic metrics are numerically equal. By the pullback formula $g ^ { ( i ) } = J ^ { ( i ) \top } g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( i ) } J ^ { ( i ) }$ , the two systems’ state-space metrics can be obtained from the same intrinsic metric through their respective Jacobians. Hence the intrinsic metric can be transferred across systems. □

Inverse-free.

Proposition 3.16 (Inverse-free). If J = I (invariants are linear diferences), then

$$
g = g _ { \Delta } + \varepsilon _ { G } I , \quad g ^ { - 1 } = ( \Sigma ^ { - 1 } + \varepsilon _ { G } I ) ^ { - 1 } \approx \Sigma - \varepsilon _ { G } \Sigma ^ { 2 } + O ( \varepsilon _ { G } ^ { 2 } ) .\tag{70}
$$

As $\varepsilon _ { G } \to 0 , g ^ { - 1 } \approx \Sigma$ . The gradient update is $\dot { q } = - g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S \approx - \Sigma \nabla S .$

Proof. When $J = I , g = \Sigma ^ { - 1 } + \varepsilon _ { G } I$ . Using the Woodbury identity or Taylor expansion:

$$
( \Sigma ^ { - 1 } + \varepsilon _ { G } I ) ^ { - 1 } = \Sigma ( I + \varepsilon _ { G } \Sigma ) ^ { - 1 } = \Sigma ( I - \varepsilon _ { G } \Sigma + O ( \varepsilon _ { G } ^ { 2 } ) ) = \Sigma - \varepsilon _ { G } \Sigma ^ { 2 } + O ( \varepsilon _ { G } ^ { 2 } ) .\tag{71}
$$

Inversion is performed in the intrinsic space, avoiding state-space inversion.

Relation to the residual equation. The residual equation is $\boldsymbol { r } = - g \nabla S = - J ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J \nabla S$ The residual uses the covariant metric g because the residual is a force produced by the metric. Using the contravariant metric would yield an incorrect residual direction.

Relation to projections. In Chapter 5, the axiom projection $\Pi _ { A }$ restricts the state to $S _ { a c t i v e }$ . The pulled-back metric after projection is

$$
g | _ { S _ { a c t i v e } } = { ( d \Pi _ { A } ) } ^ { \top } ( J ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J ) { ( d \Pi _ { A } ) } ,\tag{72}
$$

where $d \Pi _ { A }$ is the tangent map of the projection [48]. If $S _ { a c t i v e }$ is a linear subspace, $d \Pi _ { A }$ is a constant orthogonal projection matrix.

Efective dimension.

Proposition 3.17 (Rank upper bound of efective dimension). ran $k ( g ) = r a n k ( J ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J ) \leq$ $\operatorname* { m i n } ( r a n k ( g _ { \Delta } ) , r a n k ( J ) )$ .

Proof. From the rank inequality rank ${ \mathrm { : } } ( A B ) \leq \operatorname* { m i n } ( r a n k ( A ) , r a n k ( B ) )$

Relation to equivariance. The equivariance of the intrinsic metric comes from the group action on the invariant space. If $g _ { \Delta }$ is invariant under the group $G ,$ the pulled-back metric is equivariant on the q space:

$$
g ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } .\tag{73}
$$

For linear group actions, $J ^ { ( k ) } ( q ) = J ( q ) \cdot \rho ( g _ { k } ) ^ { - 1 }$ , and $\mathcal { A } '$ s construction is a constant matrix. For nonlinear group actions, $J ^ { ( k ) } ( \boldsymbol { q } ) = J ( T _ { g _ { k } } \cdot \boldsymbol { q } ) \cdot \rho ( g _ { k } ) ^ { - 1 }$ depends on $q ,$ and we must stack the constraints at sample points:

$$
\mathcal { A } _ { s t a c k } v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) = b _ { s t a c k } , \quad \mathcal { A } _ { s t a c k } = \binom { \mathcal { A } ( q _ { 1 } ) } { \frac { \ d H ^ { 2 } } { \ d H ^ { 2 } ( q _ { N } ) } } .\tag{74}
$$

The joint rank condition does not automatically hold at all $q .$ The reconstruction theory under nonlinear group actions is an open problem in Chapter 11.

## 3.6 Fisher Origin of the Kernel Bandwidth Matrix

## 3.6.1 Origin of the Kernel Bandwidth Matrix

In the pullback formula from Section 3.4

$$
\begin{array} { r } { g ( \boldsymbol { q } ) = \boldsymbol { J } ( \boldsymbol { q } ) ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } \boldsymbol { J } ( \boldsymbol { q } ) + \varepsilon _ { G } \boldsymbol { I } , } \end{array}\tag{75}
$$

$J ( q )$ is determined by the invariant definition, and $\varepsilon _ { G }$ is the regularization constant. The key question is: where does Σ come from?

Σ is not a free parameter. It is uniquely determined by the data distribution. Specifically, under the natural parameterization $\eta = \Sigma ^ { - 1 }$ , the Fisher information matrix is

$$
\begin{array} { r } { F ( \eta ) = \Sigma , } \end{array}\tag{76}
$$

hence

$$
g _ { \Delta } = F ( \eta ) ^ { - 1 } = \Sigma ^ { - 1 } .\tag{77}
$$

This gives the origin of $\Sigma \colon$ it is the Fisher information of the invariant distribution $p ( \Delta | \theta )$ uniquely determined by data, not by the task [23, 24].

## 3.6.2 Definition of the Fisher Information Matrix

Definition 3.18 (Fisher information matrix). Let $p ( \Delta | \theta )$ be a distribution family with parameter $\theta \in \Theta$ , and let $\Delta$ be the observation. The Fisher information matrix is defined as

$$
F ( \theta ) = \mathbb { E } _ { \Delta \sim p ( \Delta | \theta ) } \left[ \frac { \partial \log p ( \Delta | \theta ) } { \partial \theta } \frac { \partial \log p ( \Delta | \theta ) } { \partial \theta } ^ { \top } \right] .\tag{78}
$$

$T h e$ Fisher information matrix is an $M \times M$ symmetric positive definite matrix. It measures the efect of small changes in θ on the distribution $p ( \Delta | \theta )$ . Large Fisher information means the distribution is sensitive to the parameter.

## 3.6.3 Objectivity Theorem

Theorem 3.19 (Task-independence of the Fisher metric). If the following conditions hold:

1. The invariant $\Delta$ is defined by the symmetry group G, without reference to the task;

2. The distribution family $p ( \Delta | \theta )$ is determined by the structure of the invariant space (maximum entropy principle);

3. The parameter $\hat { \theta }$ is given by unsupervised maximum likelihood estimation;

then the Fisher metric $g _ { \Delta } = F ( { \hat { \theta } } ) ^ { - 1 }$ is task-independent.

Proof. Four steps.

Step 1 (invariant task-independence). By condition 1, $\Delta$ depends only on the symmetry group $G .$ . The symmetry group is a physical property of the system, independent of downstream tasks.

Step 2 (distribution family task-independence). By condition 2, the distribution family is determined by the structure of the invariant space. For example, multivariate Gaussian on $\mathbb { R } ^ { M }$ (maximum entropy given second moments), matrix Fisher on $S E ( 3 )$ (natural distribution on Lie groups), Wishart on $S y m ^ { + } ( M )$ (natural conjugate prior on positive definite matrices). These choices are determined by geometric structure, not by task.

Step 3 (parameter estimation task-independence). $\mathrm { B y }$ condition 3, $\hat { \theta }$ is given by unsupervised maximum likelihood estimation:

$$
\hat { \theta } = \arg \operatorname* { m a x } _ { \theta } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \log p ( \Delta _ { i } | \theta ) .\tag{79}
$$

It depends only on data and invariants, not on task labels or task losses.

Step 4 (Fisher task-independence). $F ( { \hat { \theta } } )$ is the Fisher information of $p ( \Delta | \hat { \theta } )$ , uniquely determined by the distribution family and parameter, not involving the task. $g _ { \Delta } = F ( { \hat { \theta } } ) ^ { - 1 }$ is uniquely determined by Fisher.

Conclusion: $g _ { \Delta }$ is task-independent.

Remark 3.20 (Suficient statistic special case). If $\Delta$ is a suficient statistic of the exponential family, then under natural parameterization

$$
C o v ( \Delta ) = F ( \theta ) .\tag{80}
$$

This is a standard result of information geometry $[ { \mathcal { Q } } { \mathcal { 3 } } , { \mathcal { Q } } { \mathcal { 4 } } ] ,$ , not a contribution of this paper. In this case $g _ { \Delta } = C o v ( \Delta ) ^ { - 1 } = F ( \theta ) ^ { - 1 }$ , and the two paths agree.

$I f \Delta$ is not a suficient statistic, the Cram´er–Rao lower bound gives

$$
C o v ( \Delta ) \ge F ( \theta ) ^ { - 1 } ,\tag{81}
$$

hence $g _ { \Delta } ^ { p a r t i a l } = C o v ( \Delta ) ^ { - 1 } \leq F ( \theta )$ . The metric is underestimated. Engineering handling: if the suficient statistic is known, use it; if unknown, use $C o v ( \Delta ) ^ { - 1 }$ as a conservative estimate.

## 3.6.4 Error Decomposition and Condition Number Amplification

Theorem 3.21 (Error decomposition). The error between the learned metric $\hat { g } _ { \Delta }$ and the true metric $g _ { \Delta } ^ { * }$ satisfies

$$
\| \hat { g } _ { \Delta } - g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } \| _ { F } \leq \epsilon _ { s t a t } + \epsilon _ { f a m i l y } + \epsilon _ { i n v } + \epsilon _ { p a r a m } ,\tag{82}
$$

where $\epsilon _ { s t a t }$ is statistical error, $\epsilon _ { f a m i l y }$ is distribution family mismatch, $\epsilon _ { i n v }$ is invariant selection error, and $\epsilon _ { p a r a m }$ is parameterization error.

Proof. By the triangle inequality, the error decomposes into four sources. Statistical error comes from the deviation between the sample covariance $\hat { \Sigma }$ and the true covariance $\Sigma ^ { * }$ . Distribution family mismatch comes from the deviation between the assumed distribution $p _ { \mathrm { a s s u m e d } }$ and the true distribution $p _ { \mathrm { t r u e } }$ . Invariant selection error comes from whether $\Delta$ is a suficient statistic. Parameterization error comes from $g _ { \Delta } \mathrm { ^ { \prime } s }$ parameterization (if state-dependent). □

Theorem 3.22 (Condition number amplification). Let $\Sigma ^ { * }$ be the true covariance, $\hat { \Sigma }$ be the sample covariance from N samples, and $\hat { g } _ { \Delta } = \hat { \Sigma } ^ { - 1 }$ . Then

$$
\frac { \| \hat { g } _ { \Delta } - g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } \| _ { F } } { \| g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } \| _ { F } } \leq \kappa ( \Sigma ^ { * } ) ^ { 2 } \cdot \sqrt { \frac { M } { N } } + O \left( \frac { M } { N } \right) ,\tag{83}
$$

where $\kappa ( \Sigma ^ { * } ) = \lambda _ { \mathrm { m a x } } ( \Sigma ^ { * } ) / \lambda _ { \mathrm { m i n } } ( \Sigma ^ { * } )$ is the condition number of $\Sigma ^ { * }$ .

Proof. Four steps.

Step 1 (sample covariance error). By the law of large numbers and the central limit theorem,

$$
\mathbb { E } \Vert \hat { \Sigma } - \Sigma ^ { * } \Vert _ { F } ^ { 2 } = O \left( \frac { M ^ { 2 } } { N } \right) .\tag{84}
$$

Step 2 (inverse matrix perturbation). By Neumann series expansion,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \hat { \Sigma } ^ { - 1 } - \Sigma ^ { * - 1 } = - \Sigma ^ { * - 1 } ( \hat { \Sigma } - \Sigma ^ { * } ) \Sigma ^ { * - 1 } + O ( \Vert \hat { \Sigma } - \Sigma ^ { * } \Vert ^ { 2 } ) . } \end{array}\tag{85}
$$

Step 3 (take norms). By submultiplicativity of matrix norms,

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| \hat { { \boldsymbol \Sigma } } ^ { - 1 } - { \boldsymbol \Sigma } ^ { * - 1 } \| _ { F } \leq \| { \boldsymbol \Sigma } ^ { * - 1 } \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \cdot \| \hat { { \boldsymbol \Sigma } } - { \boldsymbol \Sigma } ^ { * } \| _ { F } . } \end{array}\tag{86}
$$

Step 4 (normalize). Note that $\| g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } \| _ { F } = \| \Sigma ^ { * - 1 } \| _ { F }$ , and $\lVert g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } \rVert _ { F } \ge \lVert \Sigma ^ { * - 1 } \rVert _ { 2 }$ (Frobenius norm not less than spectral norm). Hence

$$
\frac { \| \hat { \boldsymbol { \Sigma } } ^ { - 1 } - \boldsymbol { \Sigma } ^ { * - 1 } \| _ { F } } { \| \boldsymbol { \Sigma } ^ { * - 1 } \| _ { F } } \leq \| \boldsymbol { \Sigma } ^ { * - 1 } \| _ { 2 } ^ { 2 } \cdot \frac { \| \hat { \boldsymbol { \Sigma } } - \boldsymbol { \Sigma } ^ { * } \| _ { F } } { \| \boldsymbol { \Sigma } ^ { * - 1 } \| _ { F } } \leq \kappa ( \boldsymbol { \Sigma } ^ { * } ) ^ { 2 } \cdot \frac { \| \hat { \boldsymbol { \Sigma } } - \boldsymbol { \Sigma } ^ { * } \| _ { F } } { \| \boldsymbol { \Sigma } ^ { * } \| _ { F } } .\tag{87}
$$

Substituting $\mathbb { E } \Vert \hat { \Sigma } - \Sigma ^ { * } \Vert _ { F } ^ { 2 } = O ( M ^ { 2 } / N )$ yields

$$
\frac { \lVert \hat { g } _ { \Delta } - g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } \rVert _ { F } } { \lVert g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } \rVert _ { F } } \leq \kappa ( \Sigma ^ { * } ) ^ { 2 } \sqrt { \frac { M } { N } } .\tag{88}
$$

Corollary 3.23 (Sample size criterion). If the relative error is required to be $\leq \epsilon _ { t o l }$ , then

$$
N \geq \frac { M \cdot \kappa ( \Sigma ^ { * } ) ^ { 4 } } { \epsilon _ { t o l } ^ { 2 } } .\tag{89}
$$

Proof. From Theorem 3.22, $\kappa ( \Sigma ^ { * } ) ^ { 2 } \sqrt { M / N } \leq \epsilon _ { \mathrm { t o l } } \mathrm { g i v e s } N \geq M \kappa ( \Sigma ^ { * } ) ^ { 4 } / \epsilon _ { \mathrm { t o l } } ^ { 2 } .$

Remark 3.24 (Engineering implication). The sample size criterion (89) has a key observation: the squared condition number $\kappa ( \Sigma ^ { * } ) ^ { 2 }$ is the dominant factor. Even for $M = 6 , \ i f \kappa = 1 0 0$ , one needs $N \geq 6 \times 1 0 ^ { 1 0 }$ samples. Reducing the condition number is more efective than increasing the sample size.

Concrete example (6-DOF robot arm):

<table><tr><td>κ</td><td> $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { { t o l } } } = 0 . 1$ </td><td> $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { { t o l } } } = 0 . 0 1 $ </td></tr><tr><td>1</td><td> $6 \times 1 0 ^ { 2 }$ </td><td> $6 \times 1 0 ^ { 4 }$ </td></tr><tr><td>10</td><td> $6 \times 1 0 ^ { 6 }$ </td><td> $6 \times 1 0 ^ { 8 }$ </td></tr><tr><td>100</td><td> $6 \times 1 0 ^ { 1 0 }$ </td><td> $6 \times 1 0 ^ { 1 2 }$ </td></tr></table>
