Table 7: Engineering criteria for metric reconstruction
<table><tr><td>Question</td><td>Answer</td></tr><tr><td>How many samples are needed?</td><td> $N \geq M \kappa ^ { 4 } / \epsilon _ { \mathrm { t o l } } ^ { 2 }$ </td></tr><tr><td>What dominates the error?</td><td>Squared condition number  $\kappa ^ { 2 }$ </td></tr><tr><td>How to reduce the error?</td><td>Reduce condition number  $>$  increase samples</td></tr><tr><td>When is transfer possible?</td><td>Metric is task-independent and distribution family matches</td></tr><tr><td>How to decouple?</td><td>Metric from Fisher, energy splits into objective and task</td></tr></table>

## 3.7 Chapter Summary

This chapter establishes a complete theory of invariant-induced metrics and intrinsic metric reconstruction. Main results:

Forward construction. The metric is generated by invariants through Jacobian pullback: $g ( q ) \ = \ J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J ( q )$ . The kernel Hessian gives the metric (Theorem 3.4). Covariant and contravariant metrics must be distinguished (Remark 3.5).

Regularization. Rank deficiency is resolved by adding a regularization term $\varepsilon _ { G } I .$ . For orthogonal representations, regularization does not break equivariance; for non-orthogonal representations, it does.

Inverse reconstruction. Given the state-space metric and multiple embeddings, reconstruct the intrinsic metric through the vectorized equation vech $( g ) = \ A v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } )$ . Solvability rank condition (Theorem 3.9): ran $k ( \mathcal { A } ) = M ( M + 1 ) / 2$ . Consistency criterion (Theorem 3.11): $v e c h ( g ) \in C o l ( \mathcal { A } )$ Uniqueness theorem (Theorem 3.14). Underdetermined cases use regularization. Solution methods include least squares, Cholesky parameterization, and manifold optimization.

Fisher origin. The kernel bandwidth matrix Σ is uniquely determined by Fisher information: $g _ { \Delta } = F ( { \hat { \theta } } ) ^ { - 1 }$ . The objectivity theorem (Theorem 3.19) proves the Fisher metric is task-independent. Error decomposition theorem (Theorem 3.21) gives the four-source error analysis. Condition number amplification theorem (Theorem 3.22) shows statistical error is dominated by $\kappa ^ { 2 }$ . Sample size criterion (Corollary 3.23): $N \geq M \kappa ^ { 4 } / \epsilon _ { \mathrm { t o l } } ^ { 2 }$

Engineering applications. OOD distance, transfer learning, inverse-free, relation to projections, efective dimension, relation to equivariance.

Core formulas:

• Pullback formula: $g ( q ) = J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) ) J ( q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I ;$

• Kernel Hessian: $g _ { \Delta } = \Sigma ^ { - 1 }$ ;

• Fisher origin: $g _ { \Delta } = F ( { \hat { \theta } } ) ^ { - 1 }$

• Solvability: rank $; ( \mathcal { A } ) = M ( M + 1 ) / 2 ;$

• Condition number amplification: $\| \delta g _ { \Delta } \| / \| g _ { \Delta } \| \le \kappa ^ { 2 } \sqrt { M / N } .$

• Sample size: $N \geq M \kappa ^ { 4 } / \epsilon _ { \mathrm { t o l } } ^ { 2 }$

## 4 State-Dependent Intrinsic Metric

Chapter 3 established the intrinsic metric reconstruction theory in the constant case. That theory assumes $g _ { \Delta }$ is a constant matrix. This chapter relaxes this assumption and establishes a complete theory of the state-dependent intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta )$

The structure of this chapter is as follows. Section 4.1 argues the necessity of state dependence. Section 4.2 establishes the function setting, defining matrix-valued RKHS and three parameterization methods. Section 4.3 establishes the forward problem. Section 4.4 establishes the inverse problem. Section 4.5 establishes the function-space formulation. Section 4.6 discusses parameterization choices. Section 4.7 establishes identifiability analysis. Section 4.8 establishes existence and uniqueness theorems. Section 4.9 establishes geometric quantities. Section 4.10 establishes OOD theory. Section 4.11 discusses applications. Section 4.12 discusses numerical considerations. Section 4.13 summarizes.

## 4.1 From Constant to Function: Why State Dependence Is Needed

## 4.1.1 Limitations of the Constant Assumption

The intrinsic metric reconstruction theory of Chapter 3 assumes $g _ { \Delta }$ is a constant matrix. This assumption conflicts with two facts.

Statistical fact. The covariance Cov(∆) depends on the distribution of $\Delta .$ . In diferent states, the distributions of invariants difer. If in state $z _ { 1 }$ the invariants concentrate near $\Delta _ { 1 }$ and in state $z _ { 2 }$ they concentrate near $\Delta _ { 2 }$ , then the local covariances difer, and the intrinsic metrics difer. The constant assumption cannot capture this state dependence.

Geometric fact. The invariant space is generally a curved manifold. For example, the quotient space $M / G$ of a symmetry group action is curved. Metrics on curved manifolds must depend on position. If the metric is constant, the manifold is flat, contradicting the curvature of the quotient space.

## 4.1.2 Necessity of State Dependence

Proposition 4.1 (Contradiction of the constant assumption). ${ \cal I } f g _ { \Delta }$ is constant, the Christofel symbols of the invariant space vanish $( \Gamma _ { a b c } ^ { \Delta } = 0 )$ and the Riemann curvature vanishes $( R _ { a b c d } ^ { \Delta } =$ 0). This contradicts the fact that the invariant space is generally curved.

Proof. The derivative of a constant metric is zero. The Christofel symbols $\Gamma _ { a b c } ^ { \Delta }$ are defined by the derivatives of the metric (see Section 4.9). If $\partial _ { d } g _ { \Delta , b c } = 0$ for all $d ,$ then $\mathrm { \bar { T } } _ { a b c } ^ { \Delta } = 0$ . The Riemann curvature tensor $R _ { a b c d } ^ { \Delta }$ is defined by the derivatives of the Christofel symbols. If $\Gamma _ { a b c } ^ { \Delta } = 0$ , then $R _ { a b c d } ^ { \Delta } = 0$ . Hence a constant metric corresponds to a flat space, contradicting the curvature of the quotient space. □

Conclusion: the intrinsic metric must depend on the invariant $\Delta$ . State dependence is not optional; it is a hard requirement of Riemannian geometry.

## 4.1.3 Three Levels of State Dependence

State dependence appears at three levels:

The three are coupled through the chain rule. The state-space metric $g _ { q } ( q ) = J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) ) J ( q ) +$ $\varepsilon _ { G } I$ depends simultaneously on $q$ (through J and $\Delta ( q ) )$ and on $\Delta$ (through $g _ { \Delta } )$

Table 8: Three levels of state dependence
<table><tr><td>Level</td><td>Dependency</td><td>Origin</td></tr><tr><td>Embedding  $J ( q )$ </td><td>Depends on  $q$ </td><td>Jacobian of invariants with respect to state</td></tr><tr><td>Kernel bandwidth  $\Sigma ( \Delta )$ </td><td>Depends on  $\Delta$ </td><td>Covariance varies with position</td></tr><tr><td>Intrinsic metric  $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta )$ </td><td>Depends on  $\Delta$ </td><td>Cholesky function or kernel expansion</td></tr></table>

## 4.2 Function Setting

## 4.2.1 Definition of the State-Dependent Intrinsic Metric

Definition 4.2 (State-dependent intrinsic metric). A state-dependent intrinsic metric is a mapping from the invariant space to the positive definite matrix space:

$$
g _ { \Delta } : \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \to S y m ^ { + } ( M ) , \quad \Delta \mapsto g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) ,\tag{90}
$$

where $S y m ^ { + } ( M )$ is the space of $M \times M$ symmetric positive definite matrices. The mapping satisfies four conditions:

(1) Positive definiteness. For any $\Delta \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M } , g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) \succ 0$ (positive definite).

(2) Smoothness. $g _ { \Delta } \in C ^ { s } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { M } , S y m ^ { + } ( M ) )$ with $s \geq 2$ . Smoothness guarantees the existence of Christofel symbols and Riemann curvature.

(3) Equivariance. For any $g \in G$

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( T _ { g } \cdot \Delta ) = \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } ,\tag{91}
$$

where $\rho : G \to G L ( \mathbb { R } ^ { M } )$ is the representation of the group G on the invariant space.

(4) Boundedness. There exists $R > 0$ such that

$$
\| g _ { \Delta } \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } \leq R ,\tag{92}
$$

where $\| \cdot \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } }$ is the RKHS norm (see Section 4.2.2).

## 4.2.2 Matrix-Valued RKHS

Definition 4.3 (Function space of intrinsic metrics). The intrinsic metric belongs to a matrixvalued reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS):

$$
\mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } = \{ g _ { \Delta } : \mathbb { R } ^ { M }  S y m ( M ) \vert \Vert g _ { \Delta } \Vert _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } < \infty \} ,\tag{93}
$$

where $\kappa _ { \Delta } : \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \to \mathbb { R }$ is a scalar kernel function. The norm of the matrix-valued RKHS is defined as

$$
\| g _ { \Delta } \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } ^ { 2 } = \sum _ { a , b = 1 } ^ { M } \| g _ { \Delta } ^ { a b } \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } ^ { 2 } ,\tag{94}
$$

where $g _ { \Delta } ^ { a b }$ is the (a, b)-th component function of $g _ { \Delta }$ .

Meaning of the matrix-valued RKHS: each component $g _ { \Delta } ^ { a b }$ of $g _ { \Delta }$ is a scalar function belonging to the scalar RKHS $\mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } }$ . The matrix-valued RKHS is the product of $M ^ { 2 }$ scalar RKHS.

## 4.2.3 Parameterization Methods

There are three parameterization methods for the state-dependent intrinsic metric.

Method 1 (Cholesky function, recommended).

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) ^ { \top } ,\tag{95}
$$

where $L _ { \theta } : \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \to \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times M }$ is a lower-triangular matrix function, with diagonal entries made positive via softplus:

$$
L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) _ { i i } = \mathrm { s o f t p l u s } ( \mathrm { M L P } _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) _ { i } ) > 0 ,\tag{96}
$$

where ${ \mathrm { M L P } } _ { \theta }$ is a multi-layer perceptron and θ are the trainable parameters. The softplus function is sof ${ \mathrm { . p l u s } } ( x ) = \log ( 1 + e ^ { x } )$ with range $( 0 , \infty )$

The advantages of this method are automatic positive definiteness, no projection needed, and natural parameterization. The disadvantage is non-convexity (since $L _ { \theta } L _ { \theta } ^ { \top }$ is a quadratic map), potentially with local minima.

Method 2 (matrix-valued kernel expansion).

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \alpha _ { j } \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta , \Delta _ { j } ) + \varepsilon I ,\tag{97}
$$

where $\alpha _ { j } \in \mathrm { S y m } ( M )$ are matrix coeficients, $\{ \Delta _ { j } \} _ { j = 1 } ^ { P }$ are centers, and $\varepsilon > 0$ is the regularization constant.

The advantages are RKHS theoretical support [41, 42]; the disadvantage is that positive definiteness is not automatically guaranteed. The correction is to use Cholesky parameterization $\alpha _ { j } = \beta _ { j } \beta _ { j } ^ { \intercal }$

Method 3 (diagonal + low-rank).

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = \mathrm { d i a g } ( d _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) ) + U _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) U _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) ^ { \top } ,\tag{98}
$$

where $d _ { \theta } : \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \to \mathbb { R } ^ { M }$ is a positive diagonal function, $U _ { \theta } : \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \to \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times r }$ is a low-rank function, and $r \ll M$ is the rank.

The advantage is parameter count $O ( M r )$ , suitable for large M. The disadvantage is that the low-rank assumption may not hold.

## 4.3 Forward Problem

## 4.3.1 Pullback

Definition 4.4 (State-space metric pullback). Given the state-dependent intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta )$ ， the state-space metric is

$$
g _ { q } ( q ) = J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) ) J ( q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I ,\tag{99}
$$

where $J ( q ) = \partial \Delta / \partial q \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times d }$ is the invariant Jacobian and $\varepsilon _ { G } ~ > ~ 0$ is the regularization constant.

## 4.3.2 Pointwise Computation Procedure

For each state $q ,$ the forward computation has four steps:

(1) Compute the invariant diference $\Delta ( q ) = \mathrm { i n v a r i a n t s } ( q ) - \mathrm { i n v a r i a n t s } ( q _ { r e f } )$

(2) Compute the intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) )$ (function evaluation, via Cholesky function or kernel expansion).

(3) Compute the invariant Jacobian $J ( q ) = \partial \Delta / \partial q$ (automatic diferentiation).

(4) Compute the state-space metric $g _ { q } ( q ) = J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) ) J ( q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I .$

## 4.3.3 Equivariance Inheritance

Proposition 4.5 (Equivariance inheritance). ${ \cal I } f g _ { \Delta }$ satisfies the equivariance (91), then $g _ { q }$ is equivariant:

$$
g _ { q } ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g _ { q } ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } .\tag{100}
$$

Proof. By the chain rule, the invariant Jacobian satisfies

$$
J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = J ( q ) \cdot \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } .\tag{101}
$$

Derivation: $\Delta ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = \Delta ( q )$ (invariant definition). Diferentiating with respect to q, the left side diferentiated with respect to $T _ { g } \cdot q$ gives $J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) \cdot \rho ( g )$ (since $T _ { g } \cdot q \ : = \ : \rho ( g ) q )$ , and the right side diferentiated with respect to q gives $J ( q )$ . Hence $J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) \cdot \rho ( g ) = J ( q )$ , i.e., $J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = J ( q ) \cdot \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 }$

Substituting into the pullback formula:

$$
g _ { q } ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) ) J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I\tag{102}
$$

$$
= ( J ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) ) ( J ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I\tag{103}
$$

$$
= \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) ) J ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } + \varepsilon _ { G } I\tag{104}
$$

$$
= \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } \left( J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) ) J ( q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I \right) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 }\tag{105}
$$

$$
= \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g _ { q } ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } ,\tag{106}
$$

where the third step uses $( J \rho ^ { - 1 } ) ^ { \top } = \rho ^ { - \top } J ^ { \top }$ and the fourth step uses $\rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } \varepsilon _ { G } I \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } = \varepsilon _ { G } I$ (for orthogonal representations). □

## 4.4 Inverse Problem

## 4.4.1 Problem Statement

Given:

• State-space metric $g ( q )$ (estimated from data);

• Multiple embeddings $\{ J ^ { ( k ) } \} _ { k = 1 } ^ { K }$ , each mapping to the same M-dimensional invariant space;

• Sample points $\{ q _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N }$

Find: a function $g _ { \Delta } ( \cdot )$ such that for all k and all sample points

$$
g ( q _ { i } ) = ( J ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) ) J ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I .\tag{107}
$$

## 4.4.2 Diference from the Constant Case

Table 9: Diference between the constant and function cases
<table><tr><td>Aspect</td><td>Constant  $g _ { \Delta }$ </td><td>Function  $g _ { \Delta } ( \cdot )$ </td></tr><tr><td>Unknown</td><td> $M ( M + 1 ) / 2$  scalars</td><td>Infinite-dimensional function</td></tr><tr><td>Equation</td><td>Linear</td><td>Functional equation</td></tr><tr><td>Solution</td><td>Linear system</td><td>Function-space optimization</td></tr><tr><td>Solvability</td><td>Rank condition</td><td>Function-space rank condition</td></tr></table>

## 4.4.3 Key Dificulties

Dificulty 1 (infinite dimension). The unknown of $g _ { \Delta } ( \cdot )$ is infinite-dimensional and requires parameterization. The choice of parameterization afects convergence and generalization.

Dificulty 2 (pointwise constraint). The constraint (107) is a pointwise constraint on $g _ { \Delta }$ at $\Delta ( q _ { i } )$ , not on $g _ { \Delta }$ as a whole. This means $g _ { \Delta }$ is only constrained at sample points; behavior between sample points is determined by regularization.

Dificulty 3 (positive definiteness). The positive definiteness constraint $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) \succ 0$ in function space requires parameterization to guarantee. Cholesky parameterization automatically guarantees positive definiteness.

## 4.5 Function-Space Formulation

## 4.5.1 Optimization Problem

Definition 4.6 (State-dependent intrinsic metric reconstruction). The state-dependent intrinsic metric reconstruction is the following optimization problem:

$$
\operatorname* { m i n } _ { g _ { \Delta } \in \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } \sum _ { k = 1 } ^ { K } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \| v e c h ( g ( q _ { i } ) ) - A ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) ) ) \| ^ { 2 } + \lambda \| g _ { \Delta } \| _ { \mathcal H _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } ^ { 2 } ,\tag{108}
$$

where:

$\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } )$ is the vectorization matrix (constructed in Appendix $A ) _ { ; }$ ;

$\lambda > 0$ is the regularization coeficient;

$\| g _ { \Delta } \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } }$ is the RKHS norm.

## 4.5.2 Discretization

Kernel expansion discretization: expand $g _ { \Delta }$ using basis functions $\{ \phi _ { j } \} _ { j = 1 } ^ { P }$

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \alpha _ { j } \phi _ { j } ( \Delta ) + \varepsilon I ,\tag{109}
$$

where $\alpha _ { j } \in \mathrm { S y m } ( M )$ are matrix coeficients and $\varepsilon > 0$ is the regularization constant. Substituting into the optimization problem (108):

$$
\operatorname* { m i n } _ { \{ \alpha _ { j } \} } \sum _ { k , i } \left\| v e c h ( g ( q _ { i } ) ) - \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) v e c h \left( \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \alpha _ { j } \phi _ { j } ( \Delta ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) ) \right) \right\| ^ { 2 } + \lambda \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \| \alpha _ { j } \| _ { F } ^ { 2 } .\tag{110}
$$

This is a linear least-squares problem in $\{ \alpha _ { j } \}$ . Because $\phi _ { j }$ are fixed, vech $ ( \sum _ { j } \alpha _ { j } \phi _ { j } )$ is a linear function of $\{ \alpha _ { j } \}$

## 4.5.3 Basis Function Selection

Table 10: Basis function selection
<table><tr><td>Basis function</td><td>Form</td><td>Suitable for</td></tr><tr><td>Gaussian kernel</td><td> $\phi _ { j } ( \Delta ) = \exp ( - \| \Delta - \Delta _ { j } \| ^ { 2 } / ( 2 \sigma ^ { 2 } ) )$ </td><td>Local variation</td></tr><tr><td>Polynomial</td><td> $\phi _ { j } ( \Delta ) = \Delta ^ { j }$ </td><td>Smooth variation</td></tr><tr><td>Fourier</td><td> $\phi _ { j } ( \Delta ) = \exp ( i \omega _ { j } \cdot \Delta )$ </td><td>Periodic variation</td></tr><tr><td>Neural network</td><td> $\phi _ { j } ( \Delta ) = \mathrm { M L P } _ { j } ( \Delta )$ </td><td>General case</td></tr></table>

For small M $( M \leq 1 0 )$ , use Gaussian kernel expansion. For large $M ,$ use neural networks. The advantage of Gaussian kernel expansion is theoretical clarity (RKHS theory); the disadvantage is that the parameter count grows with $P .$ The advantage of neural networks is controllable parameter count; the disadvantage is weaker theoretical guarantees.

## 4.6 Parameterization Choice

## 4.6.1 Cholesky Function (Recommended)

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) ^ { \top } ,\tag{111}
$$

where $L _ { \theta } ( \Delta )$ is a lower-triangular matrix function, parameterized as a neural network. The diagonal entries of $L _ { \theta }$ are made positive via softplus (96).

Properties: automatic positive definiteness; diagonal entries positive via softplus; no projection needed.

Optimization problem:

$$
\operatorname* { m i n } _ { \theta } \sum _ { k , i } \Big \| v e c h ( g ( q _ { i } ) ) - \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) v e c h ( L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) ) L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) ) ^ { \top } ) \Big \| ^ { 2 } + \lambda \| \theta \| ^ { 2 } .\tag{112}
$$

This problem is non-convex (since $L _ { \theta } L _ { \theta } ^ { \top }$ is a quadratic map), but can be solved by gradient descent or L-BFGS [33].

## 4.6.2 Matrix-Valued Kernel Expansion

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \alpha _ { j } \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta , \Delta _ { j } ) + \varepsilon I .\tag{113}
$$

The advantage is RKHS theoretical support [41, 42]; the disadvantage is that positive definiteness is not automatic. The correction is to use Cholesky parameterization $\alpha _ { j } = \beta _ { j } \beta _ { j } ^ { \intercal }$

## 4.6.3 Hybrid Parameterization

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = \underbrace { g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( 0 ) } } _ { \mathrm { c o n s t a n t ~ p a r t } } + \underbrace { \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \alpha _ { j } \phi _ { j } ( \Delta ) } _ { \mathrm { f u n c t i o n ~ p a r t } } + \varepsilon I ,\tag{114}
$$

where the constant part $g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( 0 ) }$ is estimated from data (Chapter 3 methods) and the function part $\begin{array} { r } { \sum _ { j } \alpha _ { j } \phi _ { j } ( \Delta ) } \end{array}$ is learned from residuals. Hybrid parameterization combines the advantages of constant and function methods.

## 4.7 Identifiability Analysis

## 4.7.1 Pointwise Identifiability

Definition 4.7 (Pointwise identifiability). $A t \Delta _ { 0 } , g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { 0 } )$ is pointwise identifiable if there exists a sample point $q _ { i }$ such that $\Delta ( q _ { i } ) = \Delta _ { 0 }$ and the rank condition of multiple embeddings holds at $\Delta _ { 0 }$

$$
r a n k ( { \cal A } ( \Delta _ { 0 } ) ) = { \cal M } ( M + 1 ) / 2 .\tag{115}
$$

Theorem 4.8 (Pointwise identifiability). If for all $\Delta _ { 0 } \in s u p p ( \Delta )$ , there exists a sample point $q _ { i }$ such that $\Delta ( q _ { i } ) = \Delta _ { 0 }$ , and the rank condition of multiple embeddings holds at $\Delta _ { 0 }$ , then $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { 0 } )$ is pointwise identifiable.

Proof. At each $\Delta _ { 0 }$ , the constraint (107) reduces to the linear system of the constant case:

$$
v e c h ( g ( q _ { i } ) ) = \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { 0 } ) ) .\tag{116}
$$

$\mathrm { B y }$ Theorem 3.9, if ran $k ( \mathcal { A } ( \Delta _ { 0 } ) ) = M ( M { + } 1 ) / 2$ , then vech $\left( g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { 0 } ) \right)$ is uniquely solvable. Hence $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { 0 } )$ is pointwise identifiable. □

## 4.7.2 Function-Space Identifiability

Theorem 4.9 (Function-space identifiability). If the following conditions hold:

1. For all $\Delta \in s u p p ( \Delta )$ , pointwise identifiability holds;

2. $g _ { \Delta } \in \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } }$ (function space);

3. Regularization $\lambda > 0 ;$

then $g _ { \Delta }$ is unique in $\mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } }$

Proof. The objective function of optimization problem (108) is

$$
\mathcal { L } ( g _ { \Delta } ) = \sum _ { k , i } \| v e c h ( g ( q _ { i } ) ) - \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ^ { ( k ) } ( q _ { i } ) ) ) \| ^ { 2 } + \lambda \| g _ { \Delta } \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } ^ { 2 } .\tag{117}
$$

The first term is the data-fitting term; the second term is the regularization term.

By condition 1, $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { 0 } )$ is uniquely determined at each $\Delta _ { 0 }$ . By condition $2 , g _ { \Delta }$ belongs to the RKHS. By condition 3, the regularization term $\lambda \| g _ { \Delta } \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } ^ { 2 }$ is strictly convex in the RKHS.

The regularized least-squares problem in an RKHS has a unique solution [41]. Hence $g _ { \Delta }$ is unique in $\mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } }$ □

## 4.7.3 Sample Complexity

Theorem 4.10 (Sample complexity). To reconstruct $g _ { \Delta } ( \cdot )$ to accuracy $\varepsilon ,$ the required number of sample points N satisfies

$$
N \ge C \cdot \frac { M ( M + 1 ) / 2 \cdot v o l ( s u p p ( \Delta ) ) } { \varepsilon ^ { M / s } } ,\tag{118}
$$

where s is the smoothness of $g _ { \Delta } , C$ is a constant, and $v o l ( s u p p ( \Delta ) )$ is the volume of the support of $\Delta$ .

Proof. By RKHS approximation theory [41]. The function $g _ { \Delta }$ has smoothness s in an Mdimensional space, with approximation error $\stackrel { \cdot } { O } ( N ^ { - s / ( 2 s + M ) } )$ . Inverting gives the required sample size. □

Remark 4.11 (High-dimensional limitation). The bound of Theorem 4.10 rapidly becomes infeasible as M grows.

Exponential curse. The denominator $\varepsilon ^ { M / s }$ decays exponentially with M. When $M = 2 0$ $s = 2 , \varepsilon = 0 . 1 , \varepsilon ^ { M / s } = 0 . 1 ^ { 1 0 } = 1 0 ^ { - 1 0 }$ , requiring sample size on the order of $1 0 ^ { 1 0 }$

Unknown efective smoothness. s is the Sobolev smoothness of $g _ { \Delta }$ . For general matrixvalued functions, s may be much smaller than 2, making the exponent $M / s$ worse.

Support volume. vol(supp(∆)) grows with M, further amplifying the sample requirement. Practical meaning: Theorem 4.10 should be understood as a worst-case upper bound, not the actual sample size needed. In high-dimensional cases $( M > 1 0 )$ , practical paths include:

(1) Reduce the efective dimension. Through invariant selection or dimension reduction, compress M to a manageable range.

(2) Exploit low-rank structure. If g<sub>∆</sub> is approximately low-rank, use Method 3 (diagonal + low-rank) parameterization, reducing the parameter count from $O ( M ^ { 2 } )$ to $O ( M r )$

(3) Kernel expansion truncation. Use $P \ll N$ basis functions, reducing the infinite-dimensional problem to a P-dimensional linear least-squares problem.

(4) Accept local reconstruction. Do not aim at global $g _ { \Delta } ( \cdot )$ ; reconstruct only near the training distribution. The OOD bound (Theorem $4 . 1 5 )$ still applies.

Conclusion: Theorem $\it 4 . 1 0$ is instructive in low dimensions $( M \le 5 ) _ { ; }$ ; in high dimensions it should be regarded as a theoretical boundary, and practical methods must rely on structural assumptions.

## 4.8 Existence and Uniqueness of Solutions

## 4.8.1 Existence

Theorem 4.12 (Existence). If the following conditions hold:

1. $\mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } }$ is a complete RKHS;

2. $\kappa _ { \Delta }$ is a positive definite kernel;

3. $\lambda > 0 _ { i }$

then the optimization problem (108) has a unique solution.

Proof. The regularized least-squares problem in an RKHS has a unique solution [41]. Completeness guarantees limits are within the space; positive definiteness guarantees the inner product is positive definite; $\lambda > 0$ guarantees strict convexity. □

## 4.8.2 Uniqueness

Theorem 4.13 (Uniqueness). Under the conditions of Theorem $4 . 1 2 ,$ the solution $g _ { \Delta } ^ { * }$ is unique.

Proof. The objective function $\mathcal { L } ( \boldsymbol { g } _ { \Delta } )$ is strictly convex in $\mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } }$ (the data-fitting term is convex, the regularization term is strictly convex). A strictly convex function on a convex set has a unique minimum. □

Remark 4.14 (Tightness limitation of the convergence rate). An early version of this paper included a convergence theorem: $\lVert g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } - g _ { \Delta } ^ { t r u e } \rVert _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } }  0$ with rate $N ^ { - s / ( 2 \bar { s } + M ) }$ . The proof relies on standard results of RKHS regularized least squares $\it { 1 4 1 }$ and is mathematically valid.

However, the convergence rate is dificult to validate stably in low-dimensional systems. When $M = 1 0 , s = 2$ , the exponent is approximately 0.14, extremely slow. For low-dimensional systems $( M = 1 , s = 2 )$ , the theoretical exponent is 0.4, but the experimentally fitted slope is usually much lower. Reasons include insuficient training, error saturation, efective Sobolev smoothness difering from the assumption, and residual training noise.

This theorem is kept as theoretical derivation, not as experimental evidence. The experimental chapter does not report the convergence rate experiment. For small M $( \leq 3 )$ and suficient training, one may attempt to observe the convergence trend; otherwise, one should rely on the OOD extrapolation bound (Theorem $4 . 1 5 )$ as the theoretical guarantee.

## 4.9 Geometric Quantities

## 4.9.1 Christofel Symbols

The Christofel symbols of the state space are

$$
\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } ( q ) = { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } g _ { q } ^ { i l } \left( \partial _ { j } g _ { q , l k } + \partial _ { k } g _ { q , l j } - \partial _ { l } g _ { q , j k } \right) ,\tag{119}
$$

where $\partial _ { j } = \partial / \partial q ^ { j }$ and $g _ { q } ^ { i l }$ are the contravariant components of $g _ { q }$ . The term $\partial _ { j } g _ { q }$ contains three parts:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \partial _ { j } g _ { q } = ( \partial _ { j } J ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J + J ^ { \top } ( \partial _ { j } g _ { \Delta } ) J + J ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \partial _ { j } J ) . } \end{array}\tag{120}
$$

The new term is $\partial _ { j } g _ { \Delta } = ( \partial g _ { \Delta } / \partial \Delta ) \cdot ( \partial \Delta / \partial q ^ { j } ) = ( \partial g _ { \Delta } / \partial \Delta ) \cdot J _ { j }$ , where $J _ { j }$ is the $j \mathrm { - t h }$ row of $J .$

Meaning: the state dependence of the intrinsic metric enters the Christofel symbols through $\partial g _ { \Delta } / \partial \Delta$ . If $g _ { \Delta }$ is constant, $\partial _ { j } g _ { \Delta } = 0$ , and the Christofel symbols depend only on the derivatives of J.

## 4.9.2 Invariant-Space Curvature

The Christofel symbols of the invariant space are

$$
\Gamma _ { a b c } ^ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } g _ { \Delta } ^ { a d } \left( \partial _ { b } g _ { \Delta , d c } + \partial _ { c } g _ { \Delta , d b } - \partial _ { d } g _ { \Delta , b c } \right) ,\tag{121}
$$

where $\partial _ { a } = \partial / \partial \Delta ^ { a }$ and $g _ { \Delta } ^ { a d }$ are the contravariant components of $g _ { \Delta }$

The Riemann curvature tensor of the invariant space is

$$
R _ { a b c d } ^ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = \partial _ { c } \Gamma _ { a b d } ^ { \Delta } - \partial _ { d } \Gamma _ { a b c } ^ { \Delta } + \Gamma _ { a e c } ^ { \Delta } \Gamma _ { e b d } ^ { \Delta } - \Gamma _ { a e d } ^ { \Delta } \Gamma _ { e b c } ^ { \Delta } .\tag{122}
$$

Meaning: the curvature of the invariant space is determined by the derivatives of $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta )$ . If $g _ { \Delta }$ is constant, the curvature is zero. Nonzero curvature means the invariant space is curved.

## 4.9.3 Curvature and Extrapolation Dificulty

Appendix C gives a heuristic observation: in regions of high curvature, extrapolation error tends to be larger. This observation is based on the Jacobi field equation but has no rigorous proof. The precise relationship between curvature and extrapolation error is an open problem in Chapter 11.

## 4.10 OOD Theory

## 4.10.1 RKHS Extrapolation Bound

Theorem 4.15 (OOD extrapolation bound). Let $g _ { \Delta } \in \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } }$ with $\| g _ { \Delta } \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } \le R$ . Then for an OOD point $q _ { * } .$

$$
\varepsilon _ { G } ( q _ { * } ) \leq \varepsilon _ { G } ( q _ { I D } ) + R \cdot \| J ( q _ { * } ) \| ^ { 2 } \cdot \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { i n v } ) } ,\tag{123}
$$

where $\Delta _ { i n v } = \Delta ( q _ { * } ) - \Delta ( q _ { I D } )$ and $\kappa _ { \Delta }$ is the kernel function.

Proof. By the boundedness of the RKHS norm and the properties of the kernel function. The value of $g _ { \Delta }$ at an OOD point can be expressed as

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { * } ) = \langle g _ { \Delta } , \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \cdot , \Delta _ { * } ) \rangle _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } .\tag{124}
$$

By the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality,

$$
| g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { * } ) - g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { I D } ) | \leq \| g _ { \Delta } \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } \cdot \| \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \cdot , \Delta _ { * } ) - \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \cdot , \Delta _ { I D } ) \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } .\tag{125}
$$

The second term, by the kernel property,

$$
\| \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \cdot , \Delta _ { * } ) - \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \cdot , \Delta _ { I D } ) \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } ^ { 2 } = 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { * } , \Delta _ { I D } ) .\tag{126}
$$

Substituting gives $| g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { * } ) - g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { I D } ) | \le R \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { * } , \Delta _ { I D } ) }$ . Pulling back to the state-space metric $g _ { q } = J ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J + \varepsilon _ { G } I .$ , the error is multiplied by $\| J ( q _ { * } ) \| ^ { 2 }$ , giving (123). □

Remark 4.16 (Two forms of the bound). The bound (123) has two forms.

(1) q<sub>∗</sub>-dependent bound. The right side varies with $q _ { * }$ . It is usable in OOD monitoring, computing a specific bound for each $q _ { * }$

(2) Constant bound. If J is bounded on the domain, i.e., $\| J ( q ) \| \leq J _ { \operatorname* { m a x } }$ , then

$$
\varepsilon _ { G } ( q _ { * } ) \leq \varepsilon _ { G } ( q _ { I D } ) + R \cdot J _ { \operatorname* { m a x } } ^ { 2 } \cdot \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { i n v } ) } .\tag{127}
$$

This is a constant-coeficient bound, but more conservative than the q<sub>∗</sub>-dependent bound.

If J is bounded on the OOD region $( e . g .$ , the embedding is a bounded function), use the constant bound; otherwise use the q<sub>∗</sub>-dependent bound, computed pointwise.

## 4.10.2 Geodesic OOD Distance

In the state-dependent case, the Euclidean approximation is inaccurate. The exact OOD distance is the geodesic distance:

$$
d _ { O O D } ( \Delta _ { * } , \Delta _ { I D } ) = \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \gamma } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \sqrt { g _ { \Delta } ( \gamma ( t ) ) ( \dot { \gamma } ( t ) , \dot { \gamma } ( t ) ) } d t ,\tag{128}
$$

where $\gamma : [ 0 , 1 ] \to \mathbb { R } ^ { M }$ is a smooth curve from $\Delta _ { * }$ to $\Delta _ { I D }$ , with $\gamma ( 0 ) = \Delta _ { * } , \gamma ( 1 ) = \Delta _ { I D }$

The geodesic equation (Euler–Lagrange equation) is

$$
\ddot { \gamma } ^ { a } + \Gamma _ { a b c } ^ { \Delta } \dot { \gamma } ^ { b } \dot { \gamma } ^ { c } = 0 .\tag{129}
$$

Approximation: if $\Delta .$ <sub>∗</sub> is close to $\Delta _ { I D }$ , the Mahalanobis distance approximation is valid:

$$
d _ { O O D } \approx \sqrt { ( \Delta _ { * } - \Delta _ { I D } ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { I D } ) ( \Delta _ { * } - \Delta _ { I D } ) } .\tag{130}
$$

The error is $O ( \| R _ { \Delta } \| \cdot \| \delta \| ^ { 3 } )$ , where $R _ { \Delta }$ is the Riemann curvature tensor of the invariant space and δ is the distance between the two points [22].

## 4.11 Applications

## 4.11.1 Transfer Learning

Theorem 4.17 (Function transfer). Ifthe intrinsic metric functions oftwo systems are identical $( g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( 1 ) } ( \cdot ) = g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( 2 ) } ( \cdot ) )$ , the metric can be transferred across systems.

Proof. By the assumption, the two systems’ intrinsic metric functions are numerically equal. $\mathrm { B y }$ the pullback formula $g _ { q } ^ { ( i ) } = J ^ { ( i ) \top } g _ { \Delta } ^ { ( i ) } ( \Delta ^ { ( i ) } ) J ^ { ( i ) }$ , the two systems’ state-space metrics can be obtained from the same intrinsic metric function through their respective Jacobians. Hence the intrinsic metric function can be transferred across systems. □

Application: learn $g _ { \Delta } ( \cdot )$ (function) on the source task; use directly on the target task. The target state-space metric is $g _ { q } ^ { ( 2 ) } ( q ) = J ^ { ( 2 ) } ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ^ { ( 2 ) } ( q ) ) J ^ { ( 2 ) } ( q )$

## 4.11.2 Inverse-Free

Proposition 4.18 (Inverse-free). $I f \ J = I$ , then

$$
g _ { q } ( q ) = g _ { \Delta } ( q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I , \quad g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } ( q ) = ( \Sigma ^ { - 1 } + \varepsilon _ { G } I ) ^ { - 1 } \approx \Sigma - \varepsilon _ { G } \Sigma ^ { 2 } + O ( \varepsilon _ { G } ^ { 2 } ) .\tag{131}
$$

As $\varepsilon _ { G } \to 0 , g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } \approx \Sigma$

Proof. When $J = I , g _ { q } = g _ { \Delta } + \varepsilon _ { G } I = \Sigma ^ { - 1 } + \varepsilon _ { G } I$ . Using the Taylor expansion

$$
( \Sigma ^ { - 1 } + \varepsilon _ { G } I ) ^ { - 1 } = \Sigma ( I + \varepsilon _ { G } \Sigma ) ^ { - 1 } = \Sigma ( I - \varepsilon _ { G } \Sigma + O ( \varepsilon _ { G } ^ { 2 } ) ) = \Sigma - \varepsilon _ { G } \Sigma ^ { 2 } + O ( \varepsilon _ { G } ^ { 2 } ) .\tag{132}
$$

## 4.11.3 State-Dependent Metric Learning

Framework: learn $g _ { \Delta } ( \cdot )$ from data, rather than learning $g _ { q } ( \cdot )$ directly. Advantages: coordinateindependent, transferable, with RKHS extrapolation bound.

## 4.12 Numerical Considerations

## 4.12.1 Computational Complexity

## 4.12.2 Numerical Stability

Problem: $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta )$ may be ill-conditioned (large condition number).

Solution: (1) Cholesky parameterization automatically guarantees positive definiteness; (2) Diagonal entries have lower bound $L _ { i i } \ge \varepsilon _ { L } > 0 ;$ (3) Condition number monitoring.

## 4.13 Chapter Summary

This chapter establishes a complete theory of the state-dependent intrinsic metric. Main results: Setting. $g _ { \Delta } : \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \to \operatorname { S y m } ^ { + } ( M )$ is a function, belonging to a matrix-valued RKHS. Parameterization methods include Cholesky function (recommended), matrix-valued kernel expansion, and diagonal + low-rank.

Forward. $g _ { q } ( q ) = J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) ) J ( q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I ,$ . Equivariance inheritance (Proposition ??).

Inverse. Function-space optimization with RKHS regularization. Kernel expansion discretizes to a linear least-squares problem.

Identifiability. Pointwise identifiability (Theorem 4.8) plus function-space uniqueness (Theorem 4.9). The sample complexity theorem 4.10 is infeasible in high dimensions (Remark 4.11).

Existence and uniqueness. Under standard RKHS conditions, the solution exists and is unique (Theorems 4.12 and 4.13).

Geometry. $\Gamma _ { \Delta } \neq 0 , R _ { \Delta } \neq 0 .$

OOD. RKHS extrapolation bound (Theorem 4.15) and geodesic distance.

Applications. Transfer learning, inverse-free, state-dependent metric learning.

Core formulas:

• State-dependent metric: $g _ { \Delta } : \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \to \operatorname { S y m } ^ { + } ( M )$

• Pullback: $g _ { q } ( q ) = J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) ) J ( q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I ;$

• Cholesky parameterization: $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) ^ { \top }$

• OOD extrapolation bound: $\varepsilon _ { G } ( q _ { * } ) \leq \varepsilon _ { G } ( q _ { I D } ) + R \| J ( q _ { * } ) \| ^ { 2 } \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { i n v } ) }$

## 5 Three-Layer Projections

Chapters 2–4 established the geometric foundations and metric construction of SKG. This chapter establishes the three-layer projection theory, handling three types of constraints in SKG inference.

Projections in SKG serve three roles. First, the axiom projection $\Pi _ { A }$ restricts the state to the admissible state space, handling hard constraints. Second, the symplectic-step projection $P _ { \perp }$ corrects the symplectic step, restoring the GENERIC degeneracy condition. Third, the conservation projection $\Pi _ { E }$ preserves energy, eliminating energy drift in numerical integration.

The structure of this chapter is as follows. Section 5.1 explains the necessity of three-layer projections. Section 5.2 establishes the axiom projection. Section 5.3 establishes the symplecticstep projection. Section 5.4 establishes the conservation projection. Section 5.5 discusses the efect of projections on OOD bounds. Section 5.6 summarizes.

## 5.1 Why Three-Layer Projections Are Needed

The SKG inference flow is

$$
\nabla _ { \dot { z } } \dot { z } = P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H - g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S - \alpha \dot { z } .\tag{133}
$$

This flow describes free evolution in the unconstrained case. In actual systems, the state must satisfy three types of constraints.

First type: axiom constraints. Hard constraints that the system must satisfy, such as joint angle ranges of a robotic arm, bond length restrictions in molecular simulation, and nonholonomic constraints of robots. These constraints define the admissible state space $S _ { a c t i v e } \subset$ M. If not satisfied, the state has no physical meaning.

Second type: symplectic-step degeneracy condition. When $S = S ( p )$ , the GENERIC degeneracy condition $L \nabla S = 0$ is violated (Section 2.1). Without correction, the symplectic step produces entropy, violating energy conservation.

Third type: conservation laws. The energy E must be preserved in numerical integration. Without correction, numerical errors accumulate, causing energy drift and long-time simulation failure [19, 20].

The three types of constraints correspond to three-layer projections. Each projection acts on a diferent object, handling a diferent constraint.

Table 12: Structure of the three-layer projections
<table><tr><td>Projection</td><td>Symbol Object</td><td></td><td>Constraint type</td></tr><tr><td>Axiom projection</td><td> $\Pi _ { A }$ </td><td>State z</td><td>Hard constraint</td></tr><tr><td>Symplectic-step projection</td><td> $P _ { \perp }$ </td><td>Symplectic step  $\omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H$ </td><td>Degeneracy condition</td></tr><tr><td>Conservation projection</td><td> $\Pi _ { E }$ </td><td>State z</td><td>Energy conservation</td></tr></table>

Joint action of the three projections: for state $z ,$ first apply the axiom projection $\Pi _ { A }$ to restrict the state to $S _ { a c t i v e }$ , then apply the symplectic-step projection $P _ { \perp }$ to correct the symplectic step, and finally apply the conservation projection $\Pi _ { E }$ to preserve energy. The order is determined by the hierarchy of constraints: axiom constraints take priority (hard), symplectic-step degeneracy second (structural), energy conservation last (numerical).

## 5.2 Axiom Projection: Handling Hard Constraints

## 5.2.1 Admissible State Space

Definition 5.1 (Admissible state space). Let the system have K equality constraints

$$
g _ { i } ( z ) = 0 , \quad i = 1 , \ldots , K ,\tag{134}
$$

where $g _ { i } : M \to \mathbb { R }$ are smooth functions. The admissible state space is defined as

$$
S _ { a c t i v e } = \bigcap _ { i = 1 } ^ { K } S _ { i } , \quad S _ { i } = \{ z \in M \mid g _ { i } ( z ) = 0 \} .\tag{135}
$$

Each $S _ { i }$ is a codimension-1 submanifold of M. $S _ { a c t i v e }$ is a codimension-K submanifold of M $( i f$ the constraints are independent).

Meaning of equality constraints: the system state must exactly satisfy these constraints. For example, the link lengths of a double pendulum are fixed $( g _ { i } ( z ) = \| q _ { i } - q _ { i - 1 } \| ^ { 2 } - L _ { i } ^ { 2 } = 0 )$ and bond lengths in molecular simulation are fixed. These are hard constraints that cannot be violated.

## 5.2.2 Definition of the Axiom Projection

Definition 5.2 (Axiom projection). The axiom projection $\Pi _ { A } : M \to S _ { a c t i v e }$ is defined as

$$
\Pi _ { A } ( z ) = \arg \operatorname* { m i n } _ { z ^ { \prime } \in S _ { a c t i v e } } d _ { g } ( z , z ^ { \prime } ) ,\tag{136}
$$

where $d _ { g } \big ( z , z ^ { \prime } \big )$ is the geodesic distance induced by the metric $g .$

Meaning: the axiom projection maps a constraint-violating state z to the nearest point in the admissible state space $S _ { a c t i v e }$ . The projection preserves physical meaning.

## 5.2.3 Lagrange Multiplier Method

Directly solving (136) is a constrained optimization problem. Use the Lagrange multiplier method [33]. Define the Lagrangian

$$
\mathcal { L } ( z ^ { \prime } , \lambda ) = \frac { 1 } { 2 } d _ { g } ( z , z ^ { \prime } ) ^ { 2 } + \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { K } \lambda _ { i } g _ { i } ( z ^ { \prime } ) ,\tag{137}
$$

where $\lambda = ( \lambda _ { 1 } , \ldots , \lambda _ { K } )$ are Lagrange multipliers.

First-order optimality conditions:

$$
z ^ { \prime } - z + \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { K } \lambda _ { i } \nabla g _ { i } ( z ^ { \prime } ) = 0 ,\tag{138}
$$

$$
g _ { i } ( z ^ { \prime } ) = 0 , \quad i = 1 , \ldots , K .\tag{139}
$$

Equation (138) is the stationarity condition. Equation (139) is the constraint condition.

This system has $d + K$ unknowns (d components of $z ^ { \prime }$ plus K components of λ) and $d +$ K equations (d stationarity equations plus K constraint equations). If the $\nabla g _ { i }$ are linearly independent (independent constraints), the system has a unique solution.

## 5.2.4 Geometric Interpretation

Geometric interpretation: $S _ { a c t i v e }$ is a submanifold embedded in M. The axiom projection maps a point z to the nearest point in $S _ { a c t i v e }$ . The projection direction is along the normal space of $S _ { a c t i v e }$ :

$$
N _ { z } S _ { a c t i v e } = \operatorname { s p a n } \{ \nabla g _ { 1 } ( z ) , \dots , \nabla g _ { K } ( z ) \} .\tag{140}
$$

The projected state $z ^ { \prime } = \Pi _ { A } ( z )$ satisfies $z - z ^ { \prime } \in N _ { z ^ { \prime } } S _ { a c t i v e } ,$ i.e., $z - z ^ { \prime }$ is a normal vector to $S _ { a c t i v e }$ at $z ^ { \prime } .$

## 5.2.5 Tangent Map and Pullback Metric

Definition 5.3 (Tangent map). Let $\Pi _ { A } : M  S _ { a c t i v e }$ be the axiom projection. The tangent map $d \Pi _ { A } : T _ { z } M  T _ { \Pi _ { A } ( z ) } S _ { a c t i v e }$ is defined as

$$
( d \Pi _ { A } ) ( v ) = \frac { d } { d t } \bigg | _ { t = 0 } \Pi _ { A } ( z + t v ) , \quad v \in T _ { z } M .\tag{141}
$$

The tangent map projects tangent vectors of the state space to tangent vectors of the admissible subspace. If $S _ { a c t i v e }$ is a linear subspace, then $d \Pi _ { A }$ is a constant orthogonal projection matrix.

Pullback metric:

$$
g | _ { S _ { a c t i v e } } = ( d \Pi _ { A } ) ^ { \top } ( J ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J ) ( d \Pi _ { A } ) .\tag{142}
$$

This gives the metric on $S _ { a c t i v e }$ , obtained by pulling back the state-space metric through the tangent map.

## 5.3 Symplectic-Step Projection: Resolving the Degeneracy Conflict

## 5.3.1 Violation of the Degeneracy Condition

Section 2.1 noted: when $S = S ( p ) , \nabla S = ( 0 , \partial _ { p } S )$ , and the standard symplectic structure satisfies

$$
\omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla S = ( \partial _ { p } S , 0 ) ^ { \top } \neq 0 .\tag{143}
$$

The first degeneracy condition $L \nabla S = 0$ is violated. This means the symplectic step $\omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H$ produces entropy, violating energy conservation.

## 5.3.2 Definition of the Symplectic-Step Projection

Definition 5.4 (Symplectic-step projection). The symplectic-step projection $P _ { \perp }$ is defined as

$$
P _ { \perp } = \left\{ { \begin{array} { l l } { I - \frac { \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } } { \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } } , } & { \nabla S \neq 0 , } \\ { I , } & { \nabla S = 0 . } \end{array} } \right.\tag{144}
$$

where $\| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } = \nabla S ^ { \top } \nabla S$ and I is the $d \times d$ identity matrix.

Meaning: $P _ { \perp }$ is the orthogonal projection onto the orthogonal complement of $\nabla { S }$ . It cuts of the component along ∇S, preserving the other directions.

## 5.3.3 Properties of the Projection

Proposition 5.5 (Properties of the symplectic-step projection). The symplectic-step projection $P _ { \perp }$ satisfies:

(1) Symmetry. $P _ { \perp } ^ { \top } = P _ { \perp }$

(2) Idempotence. $P _ { \mathrm { \perp } } ^ { 2 } = P _ { \perp }$

(3) Orthogonality. $P _ { \mathrm { | } } \nabla S = 0$

(4) Rank. rank $( P _ { \perp } ) = d - 1 ~ ( w h e n ~ \nabla S \ne 0 )$

Proof. Verify each.

(1) Symmetry:

$$
P _ { \perp } ^ { \top } = \left( I - \frac { \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } } { \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } } \right) ^ { \top } = I - \frac { \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } } { \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } } = P _ { \perp } .\tag{145}
$$

(2) Idempotence:

$$
P _ { \perp } ^ { 2 } = \left( I - \frac { \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } } { \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } } \right) ^ { 2 } = I - 2 \frac { \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } } { \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } } + \frac { \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } } { \| \nabla S \| ^ { 4 } }\tag{146}
$$

$$
= I - 2 { \frac { \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } } { \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } } } + { \frac { \nabla S \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } \nabla S ^ { \top } } { \| \nabla S \| ^ { 4 } } } = I - { \frac { \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } } { \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } } } = P _ { \bot } .\tag{147}
$$

(3) Orthogonality:

$$
P _ { \perp } \nabla S = \nabla S - \frac { \nabla S ( \nabla S ^ { \top } \nabla S ) } { \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } } = \nabla S - \nabla S = 0 .\tag{148}
$$

(4) Rank: $P _ { \perp }$ projects onto the orthogonal complement of $\nabla S$ The dimension of the orthogonal complement is $d - 1$ . Hence rank $( P _ { \perp } ) = d - 1$ □

## 5.3.4 Restoration of the Degeneracy Condition

Theorem 5.6 (Restoration of the degeneracy condition). Using the symplectic-step projection to correct the symplectic step: $\omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H \to P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H$ . Then the first GENERIC degeneracy condition is restored:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { ( P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \top } \nabla S = 0 . } \end{array}\tag{149}
$$

Proof. By the symmetry and orthogonality of $P _ { \perp }$

$$
\begin{array} { r } { ( P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \top } \nabla S = ( \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \top } P _ { \perp } ^ { \top } \nabla S = ( \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \top } P _ { \perp } \nabla S = 0 . } \end{array}\tag{150}
$$

The first equality uses $( A B ) ^ { \top } = B ^ { \top } A ^ { \top }$ ; the second uses $P _ { \perp } ^ { \top } = P _ { \perp }$ (property 1); the third uses $P _ { \perp } \nabla S = 0$ (property 3). □

Meaning: the corrected symplectic step does not produce entropy, and energy conservation is restored.

## 5.3.5 Singularity at $\nabla S = 0$

When $\nabla S = 0 , \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 } = 0$ , and the denominator of $P _ { \perp }$ in Definition (144) is zero; the projection is undefined. In this case, define $P _ { \perp } = I$

This definition is reasonable because when $\nabla S = 0 , P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H = \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H$ . The degeneracy condition requires $L \nabla S = ( \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \intercal } \nabla S = 0$ . Since $\nabla { S } = 0$ , this condition is automatically satisfied. Hence $P _ { \bot } = I$ is the natural definition.

Physical meaning of $\nabla S = 0 :$ entropy reaches a local extremum. In mechanical systems, when $p = 0$ (turning point), $\partial _ { p } S = 0 , \nabla S = 0$ . The symplectic step naturally satisfies the degeneracy condition.

## 5.3.6 Symplectic-Step Projection and Residual Independence

Key observation: although the symplectic-step projection is introduced to resolve the degeneracy condition, it has no efect on the residual.

The residual equation is

$$
r = \ddot { z } + \Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } ) - P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H + \alpha \dot { z } .\tag{151}
$$

When the model is exact, $r = - g ( z ) \nabla S ( z )$ . The residual only involves the dissipative part $g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ , independent of the symplectic step $P _ { \bot } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H$ . Even if the symplectic-step projection changes, the residual is unchanged. This property is detailed in Chapter 6.

## 5.4 Conservation Projection: Preserving Conserved Quantities

## 5.4.1 Necessity of Energy Conservation

The energy E is a conserved quantity of the SKG system (when $\alpha = 0 )$ . But numerical integration introduces errors, causing energy drift. In long-time simulation, energy drift accumulates, causing results to fail [19, 20].

Solution: after each numerical integration step, use the conservation projection to project the state back onto the constant-energy surface.

## 5.4.2 Definition of the Conservation Projection

Definition 5.7 (Conservation projection). Let $E : M \to \mathbb { R }$ be a conserved quantity and $E _ { 0 } =$ $E ( z _ { 0 } )$ be the initial energy. The conservation projection $\Pi _ { E } \colon M \to M$ is defined as

$$
\Pi _ { E } ( z ) = z - \frac { E ( z ) - E _ { 0 } } { \Vert \nabla E ( z ) \Vert ^ { 2 } } \cdot \nabla E ( z ) .\tag{152}
$$

Meaning: adjust the state along the $\nabla E$ direction so that the energy returns to $E _ { 0 }$ . The adjustment is $\begin{array} { r } { \Delta \dot { z } = - \frac { E ( z ) - E _ { 0 } } { \| \nabla E ( z ) \| ^ { 2 } } \nabla E ( \check { z } ) } \end{array}$

## 5.4.3 Properties of the Conservation Projection

Proposition 5.8 (Properties of the conservation projection). The conservation projection $\Pi _ { E }$ satisfies:

(1) Energy preservation. $E ( \Pi _ { E } ( z ) ) = E _ { 0 } + O ( \| \Delta z \| ^ { 2 } )$ (first-order approximation).

(2) Projection direction. $\Pi _ { E } ( z ) - z { \it \ i s }$ along $\nabla E ( z )$

(3) Idempotence. $\Pi _ { E } ( \Pi _ { E } ( z ) ) = \Pi _ { E } ( z ) + O ( \lVert \Delta z \rVert ^ { 2 } )$ (first-order approximation).

Proof. (1) Energy preservation. Let $z ^ { \prime } = \Pi _ { E } ( z )$ . By definition,

$$
\boldsymbol { z } ^ { \prime } - \boldsymbol { z } = - \frac { E ( \boldsymbol { z } ) - E _ { 0 } } { \Vert \nabla E ( \boldsymbol { z } ) \Vert ^ { 2 } } \nabla E ( \boldsymbol { z } ) .\tag{153}
$$

Taylor-expand E at z:

$$
E ( z ^ { \prime } ) = E ( z ) + \nabla E ( z ) ^ { \top } ( z ^ { \prime } - z ) + O ( \| z ^ { \prime } - z \| ^ { 2 } ) .\tag{154}
$$

Substituting $z ^ { \prime } - z \colon$

$$
E ( z ^ { \prime } ) = E ( z ) - \frac { E ( z ) - E _ { 0 } } { \| \nabla E ( z ) \| ^ { 2 } } \nabla E ( z ) ^ { \top } \nabla E ( z ) + O ( \| z ^ { \prime } - z \| ^ { 2 } ) = E ( z ) - ( E ( z ) - E _ { 0 } ) + O ( \| \Delta z \| ^ { 2 } ) = E _ { 0 } + O ( \| \Delta z \| ^ { 2 } ) .\tag{155}
$$

Energy is preserved to first order.

(2) Projection direction. By definition, $z ^ { \prime } - z$ is a scalar multiple of $\nabla E ( z )$ , hence along $\nabla E ( z )$

(3) Idempotence. If z is already on the constant-energy surface $\left( E ( z ) = E _ { 0 } \right)$ , then $\Pi _ { E } ( z ) = z$ If z is not on the constant-energy surface, after one projection $E ( \Pi _ { E } ( z ) ) \approx E _ { 0 }$ , and a second projection barely changes the state. Hence $\Pi _ { E } ( \Pi _ { E } ( z ) ) \approx \Pi _ { E } ( z )$ □

## 5.4.4 Conservation Projection and Symplectic Integrators

The conservation projection is used in conjunction with symplectic integrators. Symplectic integrators preserve the symplectic structure but do not strictly preserve energy (there is an $O ( h ^ { p } )$ energy drift, where h is the step size and p is the integrator order). The conservation projection eliminates energy drift, stabilizing long-time simulation.

Procedure:

1. Use a symplectic integrator (e.g., St¨ormer–Verlet) to advance one step: $z _ { n + 1 } ^ { r a w } = \Phi _ { h } ( z _ { n } )$

2. Apply the conservation projection: $z _ { n + 1 } = \Pi _ { E } ( z _ { n + 1 } ^ { r a w } )$ ;

3. Output $z _ { n + 1 }$

This procedure preserves energy to machine precision while preserving the symplectic structure (the efect of the conservation projection on the symplectic structure is of higher order).

## 5.5 Efect of Projections on OOD Bounds

## 5.5.1 Projection Reduces Extrapolation Distance

Theorem 5.9 (Projection reduces extrapolation distance). Let $S _ { a c t i v e } \subset M$ be the admissible state space and $z _ { * } , z _ { I D } \in S _ { a c t i v e }$ . Then

$$
d _ { g } ^ { S _ { a c t i v e } } ( z _ { * } , z _ { I D } ) \leq d _ { g } ^ { M } ( z _ { * } , z _ { I D } ) ,\tag{156}
$$

where $d _ { g } ^ { S _ { a c t i v e } }$ is the geodesic distance on $S _ { a c t i v e }$ and $d _ { g } ^ { M }$ is the geodesic distance on $M$

Proof. Geodesics on $S _ { a c t i v e }$ are a subset of geodesics on M (since $S _ { a c t i v e }$ is a submanifold of $M ;$ geodesics on $S _ { a c t i v e }$ are also geodesics on M, but not vice versa). Hence the shortest geodesic on $S _ { a c t i v e }$ is not shorter than the shortest geodesic on $M \mathbf { \cdot }$

$$
d _ { g } ^ { S _ { a c t i v e } } ( z _ { * } , z _ { I D } ) = \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \gamma \subset S _ { a c t i v e } } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \sqrt { g ( \dot { \gamma } , \dot { \gamma } ) } d t \geq \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \gamma \subset M } \int _ { 0 } ^ { 1 } \sqrt { g ( \dot { \gamma } , \dot { \gamma } ) } d t = d _ { g } ^ { M } ( z _ { * } , z _ { I D } ) .\tag{157}
$$

The first equality uses the geodesic distance definition on $S _ { a c t i v e } ;$ the second inequality uses $S _ { a c t i v e } \subset M$ □

## 5.5.2 Tightening the OOD Bound

Apply Theorem 5.9 to the OOD extrapolation bound (123). After the OOD point $q _ { * }$ is projected to $S _ { a c t i v e }$ , the extrapolation distance decreases and the OOD bound tightens:

$$
\varepsilon _ { G } ( q _ { * } ) \leq \varepsilon _ { G } ( q _ { I D } ) + R \cdot \| J ( \Pi _ { A } ( q _ { * } ) ) \| ^ { 2 } \cdot \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { i n v } ^ { p r o j } ) } ,\tag{158}
$$

where $\Delta _ { i n v } ^ { p r o j } = \Delta ( \Pi _ { A } ( q _ { * } ) ) - \Delta ( q _ { I D } )$

Projection reduces extrapolation distance, thereby tightening the OOD bound. This is a practical value of projections.

## 5.5.3 Joint Action of the Three Projections

The joint action of the three projections is as follows.

Axiom projection $\Pi _ { A }$ . Restricts the state to $S _ { a c t i v e }$ , reduces extrapolation distance, tightens the OOD bound.

Symplectic-step projection $P _ { \perp }$ . Corrects the symplectic step, restores the degeneracy condition. Has no direct efect on the OOD bound (the residual is independent of the symplectic step) but guarantees energy conservation of the inference.

Conservation projection $\Pi _ { E }$ . Preserves energy, eliminates numerical drift. Has no direct efect on the OOD bound but guarantees the stability of long-time inference.

The three projections are independent, acting on diferent objects: the axiom projection on the state, the symplectic-step projection on the symplectic step, the conservation projection on the energy.

## 5.6 Chapter Summary

This chapter establishes the three-layer projection theory of SKG, handling axiom, symplecticstep, and conservation constraints.

Axiom projection $\Pi _ { A }$ . Projects constraint-violating states onto the admissible state space $S _ { a c t i v e }$ . Solved via Lagrange multiplier method. Tangent map $d \Pi _ { A }$ gives the pullback metric $g | _ { S _ { a c t i v e } } = ( d \Pi _ { A } ) ^ { \top } ( J ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J ) ( d \Pi _ { A } )$

Symplectic-step projection $P _ { \perp }$ . Corrects the symplectic step, restores the GENERIC degeneracy condition. Defined as $P _ { \perp } = I - \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } / \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 }$ (when $\nabla S \neq 0 ) , P _ { \perp } = I$ (when

$\boldsymbol { \nabla } S = 0 )$ . Properties: symmetric, idempotent, orthogonal, rank $d - 1$ . Degeneracy condition restored (Theorem 5.6). The symplectic-step projection has no efect on the residual.

Conservation projection $\Pi _ { E }$ . Preserves energy. Defined as $\begin{array} { r } { \Pi _ { E } ( z ) = z - \frac { E ( z ) - E _ { 0 } } { \| \nabla E ( z ) \| ^ { 2 } } \nabla E ( z ) } \end{array}$ Properties: energy preservation to first order, projection direction, idempotence (first order). Used in conjunction with symplectic integrators.

Efect of projections on OOD bounds. Projection reduces extrapolation distance (Theorem 5.9) and tightens the OOD bound.

Core formulas:

• Axiom projection: $\begin{array} { r } { \Pi _ { A } ( z ) = \arg \operatorname* { m i n } _ { z ^ { \prime } \in S _ { a c t i v e } } d _ { g } ( z , z ^ { \prime } ) } \end{array}$

• Symplectic-step projection: $P _ { \perp } = I - \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } / \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 }$

• Degeneracy restoration: $( P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \top } \nabla S = 0 ;$

• Conservation projection: $\begin{array} { r } { \Pi _ { E } ( z ) = z - \frac { E ( z ) - E _ { 0 } } { \Vert \nabla E ( z ) \Vert ^ { 2 } } \nabla E ( z ) ; } \end{array}$

• Projection reduces distance: $d _ { g } ^ { S _ { a c t i v e } } \leq d _ { g } ^ { M }$

## 6 Residual Theory and Identifiability

Chapters 3–5 established the forward theory of SKG: given metric and energy, inference is geometric evolution. This chapter establishes residual theory, studying the problem of inverting the metric from trajectory data.

The residual is central to data calibration in SKG. It defines the residual as the diference between the observed trajectory and the model-predicted trajectory, and inverts the metric through the residual equation. This chapter answers three questions. First, which metric components are observable (identifiable)? Second, which components are structurally unidentifiable? Third, how does noise propagate?

The structure of this chapter is as follows. Section 6.1 establishes the residual equation with explicit expansion of the geometric force. Section 6.2 establishes the one-dimensional observable formula. Section 6.3 establishes the four-dimensional rank condition. Section 6.4 analyzes noise propagation. Section 6.5 proves the structural unidentifiability of $g _ { q q }$ . Section 6.6 establishes the potential–metric coupling equation. Section 6.7 gives the weighted estimation method. Section 6.8 summarizes.

## 6.1 Residual Equation and Geometric Force

## 6.1.1 Definition of the Residual

Definition 6.1 (Residual). Let the second-order SKG flow be

$$
\nabla _ { \dot { z } } \dot { z } = P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H - g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S - \alpha \dot { z } .\tag{159}
$$

Expanding the covariant derivative $\nabla _ { \dot { z } } \dot { z } = \ddot { z } + \Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } )$ , where $\Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } )$ is the geometric force. Moving the right side to the left, define the residual

$$
r = \ddot { z } + \Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } ) - P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H + \alpha \dot { z } + g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S .\tag{160}
$$

Meaning: if the model is exact, $r = 0$ . In practice, ¨z is estimated from trajectory data, g is calibrated from data, and ω, H, S are known. The residual measures the deviation between the observed trajectory and the model prediction.

## 6.1.2 Simplification of the Residual Equation

In equation (160), the knownness of the terms difers. ω, H, S are known (given a priori). g is to be calibrated. ¨z is estimated from trajectory data. Γ is determined by $g . \ P _ { \perp }$ is determined by S.

Moving the known terms to the right and the to-be-calibrated terms to the left:

$$
r = \ddot { z } + \Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } ) - P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H + \alpha \dot { z } .\tag{161}
$$

When the model is exact,

$$
r = - g ( z ) \nabla S ( z ) .\tag{162}
$$

This is the standard form of the residual equation.

## 6.1.3 Exact Cancellation of the Symplectic Part

Theorem 6.2 (Symplectic part cancels in the residual). In the residual equation (161), the symplectic step $P _ { \bot } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H$ is orthogonal to ∇S and does not contribute to the residual.

Proof. By the definition of the symplectic-step projection (Definition 5.4), $P _ { \bot } \nabla S = 0$ . Hence

$$
\begin{array} { r } { ( P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \top } \nabla S = ( \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \top } P _ { \perp } ^ { \top } \nabla S = ( \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { \top } P _ { \perp } \nabla S = 0 . } \end{array}\tag{163}
$$

The symplectic step is orthogonal to $\nabla { S } .$ The right side of the residual equation (162) is $- g \nabla S$ , involving only the ∇S direction. Hence the symplectic step does not contribute to the residual. □

Meaning: the residual involves only the dissipative part $g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ . Although the symplecticstep projection was introduced to correct the degeneracy condition, it has no efect on the residual. This simplifies the residual equation, constraining only the components of $g$ along ∇S.

## 6.1.4 Role of the Geometric Force

Unlike the symplectic step, the geometric force $\Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } )$ does not cancel. It is determined by the derivatives of the metric $g \colon$

$$
\Gamma ^ { i } ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } ) = \Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } \dot { z } ^ { j } \dot { z } ^ { k } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } g ^ { i l } ( \partial _ { j } g _ { l k } + \partial _ { k } g _ { l j } - \partial _ { l } g _ { j k } ) \dot { z } ^ { j } \dot { z } ^ { k } .\tag{164}
$$

The role of the geometric force in the residual is as follows.

Constant metric case (J = I, Σ constant). $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } = 0 .$ , geometric force is zero. The residual equation reduces to

$$
r = \ddot { z } - P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H + \alpha \dot { z } .\tag{165}
$$

The residual only involves ${ \ddot { z } } ,$ the symplectic step, and Rayleigh damping. The metric $g$ does not enter the residual (except through the $g ^ { - 1 }$ term in ∇S). The one-dimensional observable formula (Section 6.2) is derived in this degenerate case.

State-dependent metric case $( J ( q )$ non-constant or $\Sigma ( q )$ state-dependent). $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } \neq 0 .$ geometric force is nonzero. The residual equation contains the geometric force term. The metric g enters the residual through two pathways: directly through g<sup>−</sup> $^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ , indirectly through $\Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } )$ .

## 6.1.5 Physical Meaning of the Residual

Physical meaning of the residual r: the diference between the observed acceleration and the model-predicted acceleration. When the model is exact, the residual equals the dissipative force $- g \nabla S$ . Three sources of the residual:

Observation noise. z¨ is estimated from trajectory data via numerical diferentiation and is noisy.

Model error. $g$ is calibrated from data with estimation error. The prior forms of $\omega _ { \mathrm { { i } } }$ H, S may be incorrect.

Numerical error. Truncation error of numerical integration.

Separating the three sources is the central problem of residual theory.

## 6.2 One-Dimensional Observable

## 6.2.1 Simplified Assumptions

Consider the one-dimensional case with $m = 1$ degrees of freedom. Simplified conditions: $k = 1$ (single direction), $\alpha = 0$ (no Rayleigh damping), $S = S ( p ) = p ^ { 2 } / 2$ (entropy depends only on momentum, quadratic form).

In the one-dimensional case, the state is $z = ( q , p ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 2 }$ , where $q$ is position and $p$ is momentum. The symplectic structure is $\omega ^ { \sharp } = { \binom { 0 } { - 1 } } \ 1 \big )$ . The entropy gradient is $\nabla S = ( 0 , p ) ^ { \top }$

The symplectic step $P _ { \bot } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H$ is orthogonal to $\nabla { S }$ (Theorem 6.2) and does not contribute to the residual. The residual equation reduces to

$$
r = \ddot { z } + \Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } ) + g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S .\tag{166}
$$

Taking the degenerate case with zero geometric force (J = I, Σ constant), the residual equation further simplifies to

$$
\boldsymbol { r } = \ddot { z } + { g } ^ { - 1 } \nabla { S } .\tag{167}
$$

## 6.2.2 Observable Formula

Proposition 6.3 (One-dimensional observable formula). In the one-dimensional case, with simplified conditions $m = 1 , k = 1 , \alpha = 0 , S = S ( p ) = p ^ { 2 } / 2$ , and zero geometric force, the metric component $g _ { 2 }$ (the pp block) satisfies

$$
g _ { 2 } ^ { o b s } ( q _ { i } ) = - \frac { r _ { i } ^ { ( 2 ) } } { p _ { i } } = - \frac { \ddot { p } _ { i } + q _ { i } } { p _ { i } } ,\tag{168}
$$

where $r _ { i } ^ { ( 2 ) }$ is the second component of the residual (momentum direction), $p _ { i }$ is the momentum at the i-th sample point, and ${ \ddot { p } } _ { i }$ is the second derivative of momentum.

Proof. In the one-dimensional case, the metric $g$ is a $2 \times 2$ matrix:

$$
g = \left( { \begin{array} { c c } { g _ { 1 } } & { g _ { 1 2 } } \\ { g _ { 1 2 } } & { g _ { 2 } } \end{array} } \right) ,\tag{169}
$$

where $g _ { 1 }$ is the qq block, $g _ { 1 2 }$ is the qp block, and $g _ { 2 }$ is the $p p$ block. The contravariant metric $g ^ { - 1 }$ satisfies $g g ^ { - 1 } = I$

The entropy gradient is $\nabla S = ( 0 , p ) ^ { \top }$ . The second component of the residual equation (167):

$$
r ^ { ( 2 ) } = \ddot { p } + ( g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S ) ^ { ( 2 ) } .\tag{170}
$$

Computation of $( g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S ) ^ { ( 2 ) }$ : by $\begin{array} { r } { g ^ { - 1 } = \frac { 1 } { \operatorname* { d e t } ( g ) } \left( \begin{array} { c c } { g _ { 2 } } & { - g _ { 1 2 } } \\ { - g _ { 1 2 } } & { g _ { 1 } } \end{array} \right) } \end{array}$ ，

$$
( g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S ) ^ { ( 2 ) } = { \frac { 1 } { \operatorname* { d e t } ( g ) } } ( - g _ { 1 2 } , g _ { 1 } ) \cdot ( 0 , p ) ^ { \top } = { \frac { g _ { 1 } p } { \operatorname* { d e t } ( g ) } } .\tag{171}
$$

On the other hand, by the residual equation (162), $r = - g \nabla S$ . The second component:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { r ^ { ( 2 ) } = - ( g \nabla S ) ^ { ( 2 ) } = - ( g _ { 1 2 } , g _ { 2 } ) \cdot ( 0 , p ) ^ { \top } = - g _ { 2 } p . } \end{array}\tag{172}
$$

Hence $g _ { 2 } = - r ^ { ( 2 ) } / p .$

On the other hand, by the model-exact case, the residual directly equals − ${ \cdot g } \nabla S ,$ so $r ^ { ( 2 ) } = $ $- g _ { 2 } p$ . Also $\Ddot { p } + q = \Ddot { p } - ( P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { ( 2 ) }$ . When the Hamiltonian is $H = { \textstyle \frac { 1 } { 2 } } ( p ^ { 2 } + q ^ { 2 } )$ (harmonic oscillator), $\omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H = ( p , - q ) ^ { \top }$ . After the symplectic-step projection, $( { \cal P } _ { \bot } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H ) ^ { ( 2 ) } = - q$ (for the one-dimensional harmonic oscillator). Hence $r ^ { ( 2 ) } = \ddot { p } + q$ . Substituting $g _ { 2 } = - r ^ { ( 2 ) } / p$ gives

$$
g _ { 2 } ^ { o b s } ( q _ { i } ) = - \frac { \ddot { p } _ { i } + q _ { i } } { p _ { i } } .\tag{173}
$$

## 6.2.3 Meaning of the Formula

Meaning of the one-dimensional observable formula (168): under the simplified conditions, $g _ { 2 }$ (the $p p$ block) can be inverted from trajectory data. The numerator ${ \ddot { p } } _ { i } + q _ { i }$ is the sum of the observed acceleration and the predicted acceleration; the denominator $p _ { i }$ is the momentum. The ratio gives $g _ { 2 }$

The applicability of the formula is strict: one-dimensional, single direction, no damping, $S = p ^ { 2 } / 2$ , zero geometric force. Extension to higher dimensions requires further analysis.

## 6.2.4 Singularity

The formula (168) is singular when $p _ { i } = 0$ (zero denominator). Physical meaning of $p _ { i } = 0 :$ turning point. At the turning point, the momentum is zero, and the formula fails.

Engineering handling: weighting scheme

$$
w _ { i } = \frac { p _ { i } ^ { 2 } } { p _ { i } ^ { 2 } + \sigma ^ { 2 } } ,\tag{174}
$$

where $\sigma$ is the noise level. When $p _ { i }$ is large, the weight is close to 1; when $p _ { i }$ is small, the weight is close to 0. This weighting scheme suppresses noise amplification near the turning point.

## 6.3 Four-Dimensional Rank Condition

## 6.3.1 Four-Dimensional Case

Consider the four-dimensional case with $n = 2$ degrees of freedom. State $\boldsymbol { z } = ( q _ { 1 } , q _ { 2 } , p _ { 1 } , p _ { 2 } ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 4 }$

The metric $g$ is a $4 \times 4$ symmetric matrix with $4 \times 5 / 2 = 1 0$ independent components. But by Proposition 6.6 (Section 6.5), the $q q$ block $( 2 \times 2$ symmetric, 3 independent components) is structurally unidentifiable. The identifiable components are the $q p$ block $( 2 \times 2$ , 4 independent components) and the $p p$ block $( 2 \times 2$ symmetric, 3 independent components), totaling 7.

## 6.3.2 Linear System

Proposition 6.4 (Four-dimensional rank condition). In the four-dimensional case $( n = 2 )$ with $S = S ( p )$ and zero geometric force, the residual equation can be written in linear form

$$
r _ { i } = - A _ { i } w , \quad A _ { i } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 4 \times 7 } ,\tag{175}
$$

where $w \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 7 }$ is the vector of identifiable metric components and $A _ { i }$ is a matrix constructed from the state and momentum. The system has a unique solution if and only if

$$
r a n k ( [ A _ { 1 } ; \ldots ; A _ { N } ] ) = 7 .\tag{176}
$$

Proof. When $S = S ( p ) , \nabla S = ( 0 , 0 , \partial _ { p _ { 1 } } S , \partial _ { p _ { 2 } } S ) ^ { \top }$ . The metric acts as

$$
g \nabla S = \left( { \begin{array} { c c } { g _ { q q } } & { g _ { q p } } \\ { g _ { q p } ^ { \top } } & { g _ { p p } } \end{array} } \right) \left( { \begin{array} { c } { 0 } \\ { \partial _ { p } S } \end{array} } \right) = \left( { \begin{array} { c } { g _ { q p } \partial _ { p } S } \\ { g _ { p p } \partial _ { p } S } \end{array} } \right) .\tag{177}
$$

$g _ { q q }$ does not appear, hence structurally unidentifiable.

Identifiable components: $g _ { \boldsymbol { q } p } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 2 \times 2 } ~ ( 4 ) , ~ g _ { p p } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 2 \times 2 }$ symmetric (3), totaling 7.

The second component of the residual equation:

$$
\boldsymbol { r } ^ { ( 2 ) } = - g _ { p p } \partial _ { p } S - g _ { q p } ^ { \top } \nabla _ { q } S .\tag{178}
$$

When $\nabla _ { q } S = 0 \ ( S = S ( p ) ) , r ^ { ( 2 ) } = - g _ { p p } \partial _ { p } S$ . But in general, $\nabla _ { q } S$ may be nonzero (if S depends on $q )$ .

The linear form (175): for each sample point i, the relationship between the residual and the identifiable components is linear. Stacking N sample points gives 4N equations. Unique solvability holds if the stacked matrix has full rank (rank 7). □

## 6.3.3 Meaning of the Rank Condition

Meaning of the rank condition (176): the sample points must provide suficient diversity to uniquely determine the 7 identifiable components. If the sample points concentrate in a subspace, the stacked matrix may be rank-deficient, and some components may be unidentifiable.

Engineering handling: if rank-deficient, increase sample diversity or add regularization. Diagnosis: compute the singular values of the stacked matrix; if the smallest singular value is close to zero, the condition number is large and the solution is unstable.

## 6.4 Noise Propagation

## 6.4.1 Noise Model

Assume the acceleration observations are noisy. The observed value of $\ddot { p } _ { i }$ is

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \ddot { p } _ { i } ^ { o b s } = \ddot { p } _ { i } ^ { t r u e } + \eta _ { i } , } \end{array}\tag{179}
$$

where $\eta _ { i } \sim N ( 0 , \sigma ^ { 2 } )$ is Gaussian noise, and $\sigma$ is the noise standard deviation.

## 6.4.2 Noise Propagation Formula

Proposition 6.5 (Noise propagation). Under the one-dimensional observable formula (168), the noise causes an error in $g _ { 2 } ^ { o b s }$ :

$$
g _ { 2 } ^ { o b s } ( q _ { i } ) = g _ { 2 } ^ { t r u e } ( q _ { i } ) - \frac { \eta _ { i } } { p _ { i } } .\tag{180}
$$

The error standard deviation is

$$
\sigma _ { g _ { 2 } } ( q _ { i } ) = \frac { \sigma } { | p _ { i } | } .\tag{181}
$$

Proof. By the formula (168),

$$
g _ { 2 } ^ { o b s } ( q _ { i } ) = - \frac { \ddot { p } _ { i } ^ { t r u e } + \eta _ { i } + q _ { i } } { p _ { i } } = - \frac { \ddot { p } _ { i } ^ { t r u e } + q _ { i } } { p _ { i } } - \frac { \eta _ { i } } { p _ { i } } = g _ { 2 } ^ { t r u e } ( q _ { i } ) - \frac { \eta _ { i } } { p _ { i } } .\tag{182}
$$

Taking the standard deviation:

$$
\sigma _ { g _ { 2 } } = \sqrt { \mathrm { V a r } ( g _ { 2 } ^ { o b s } ) } = \sqrt { \mathrm { V a r } \left( - \frac { \eta _ { i } } { p _ { i } } \right) } = \frac { \sigma } { | p _ { i } | } .\tag{183}
$$

## 6.4.3 Noise Amplification and Weighting Scheme

Meaning of formula (181): the noise standard deviation is inversely proportional to $| p _ { i } |$ . When $| p _ { i } |$ is small, noise amplification is severe. When $p _ { i } = 0$ (turning point), noise is infinitely amplified.

Weighting scheme: weight by $w _ { i } = | p _ { i } | ^ { 2 } / ( | p _ { i } | ^ { 2 } + \sigma ^ { 2 } )$ . When $| p _ { i } |$ is large, the weight is close to 1; when |p<sub>i</sub>| is small, the weight is close to 0. This scheme follows directly from the weighting matrix of least squares, suppressing noise amplification near the turning point.

Numerical validation (T5 experiment): with $\sigma = 0 . 1$ , the theoretical standard deviation $\sigma / | p |$ agrees with the measured standard deviation, with a maximum relative error of 1.48%.

## 6.5 Structural Unidentifiability of $g _ { q q }$

## 6.5.1 Origin of the Problem

The residual equation (162) is $r = - g \nabla S$ . When $\begin{array} { r } { S = S ( p ) , \nabla S = ( 0 , M ^ { - 1 } p ) } \end{array}$ . The metric acts as

$$
g \nabla S = \binom { g _ { q q } } { g _ { q p } ^ { \intercal } } \begin{array} { c } { { g _ { q p } } } \\ { { g _ { p p } } } \end{array} \bigg ( \begin{array} { c } { { 0 } } \\ { { M ^ { - 1 } p } } \end{array} \bigg ) = \binom { g _ { q p } M ^ { - 1 } p } { g _ { p p } M ^ { - 1 } p } .\tag{184}
$$

$g _ { q q }$ does not appear in the residual at all.

Proposition 6.6 (Structural unidentifiability of $g _ { q q } )$ . Let $S = S ( p )$ . Then $g _ { q q }$ does not appear in the residual equation at all. $g _ { q q }$ is structurally unidentifiable.

Proof. By (184), $g \nabla S$ does not depend on g . The residual equation $g _ { q q }$ $r = - g \nabla S$ involves only $g \nabla S$ . Hence $g _ { q q }$ does not appear in the residual. Any $g _ { q q }$ gives the same residual. $g _ { q q }$ is unidentifiable. □

## 6.5.2 Counting the Identifiable Components

The identifiable components are $g _ { \boldsymbol { q } p } \left( n ^ { 2 } \right)$ components) and $g _ { p p } \ ( n ( n + 1 ) / 2$ components), totaling

$$
n ^ { 2 } + \frac { n ( n + 1 ) } { 2 } = \frac { 3 n ^ { 2 } + n } { 2 } .\tag{185}
$$

For $n = 2$ (four-dimensional case), the number of identifiable components is ${ \frac { 3 { \cdot } 4 { + } 2 } { 2 } } = 7$ , agreeing with Section 6.3.
