For $n = 3$ (six-dimensional case), the number of identifiable components is ${ \frac { 3 { \cdot } 9 { + } 3 } { 2 } } = 1 5$ . The total number of metric components is $6 \times 7 / 2 = 2 1$ , and the unidentifiable components $( g _ { q q } )$ are $3 \times 4 / 2 = 6$

## 6.5.3 Engineering Handling

$g _ { q q }$ is structurally unidentifiable and must be fixed explicitly. Two handling methods.

Method 1 (fix to identity matrix). Set $g _ { q q } = I$ . The metric form is

$$
g _ { \theta } = \left( { \begin{array} { l l } { I } & { g _ { q p } } \\ { g _ { q p } ^ { \top } } & { g _ { p p } } \end{array} } \right) .\tag{186}
$$

This method is simple but may not reflect the true geometry.

Method 2 (constrained pullback metric). Add the constraint $J _ { q } ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J _ { q } = I$ during reconstruction, where $J _ { q }$ is the $q$ block of J. This method preserves the geometric meaning of the intrinsic metric but is complex to implement.

We recommend Method 1 as default and Method 2 as a high-precision option.

## 6.6 Potential–Metric Coupling

## 6.6.1 Efect of Potential Error

The potential V is estimated from data with error $\varepsilon _ { V } = V - \hat { V }$ . This error afects the residual, and in turn afects the metric estimate.

Proposition 6.7 (Potential–metric coupling). Let the potential error be $\boldsymbol { \varepsilon } _ { V } = \boldsymbol { V } - \hat { \boldsymbol { V } }$ and the metric error be $\varepsilon _ { g } = g - \hat { g }$ . Then

$$
\varepsilon _ { p p } M ^ { - 1 } p = - \partial _ { q } \varepsilon _ { V } , \quad \varepsilon _ { q p } M ^ { - 1 } p = 0 .\tag{187}
$$

The potential error pollutes the metric estimate through $\varepsilon _ { p p }$

Proof. The residual equation is $r = - g \nabla S$ . When $\boldsymbol { S } = \boldsymbol { S } ( p ) , \boldsymbol { \nabla } \boldsymbol { S } = ( 0 , M ^ { - 1 } p )$ . Perturbation analysis:

$$
( r + \delta r ) = - ( g + \delta g ) \nabla S + O ( \delta ^ { 2 } ) .\tag{188}
$$

The potential error $\varepsilon _ { V }$ enters the symplectic force through $\nabla V$ , afecting $\delta r$ . First-order perturbation:

$$
\delta r = - \varepsilon _ { p p } M ^ { - 1 } p \cdot \partial _ { q } \varepsilon _ { V } .\tag{189}
$$

This relation gives (187).

## 6.6.2 Downweighting Scheme

The potential error pollutes the metric estimate through $\varepsilon _ { p p }$ . Handling: when weighting the residual, account for the potential error:

$$
w _ { i } ^ { c o u p l i n g } = \frac { | p _ { i } | ^ { 2 } } { | p _ { i } | ^ { 2 } + \sigma ^ { 2 } + \| \partial _ { q } \varepsilon _ { V } \| ^ { 2 } } .\tag{190}
$$

This extends the original weighting scheme (174) with the potential error term. When the potential error is large, the weight decreases.

## 6.7 Weighted Estimation Method

## 6.7.1 Weighted Least Squares

The weighted least-squares estimate of the residual equation:

$$
\boldsymbol { \hat { g } } = \arg \operatorname* { m i n } _ { \boldsymbol { g } } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } w _ { i } \| \boldsymbol { r } _ { i } + \boldsymbol { g } ( \boldsymbol { z } _ { i } ) \nabla { \cal S } ( \boldsymbol { z } _ { i } ) \| ^ { 2 } + \lambda \mathcal { R } ( \boldsymbol { g } ) ,\tag{191}
$$

where $w _ { i }$ is the weight (equation (174) or (190)), $\mathcal { R } ( g )$ is the regularization term, and $\lambda > 0$ is the regularization coeficient.

Design principle of the weights: sample points with high observation quality get large weights, and those with low quality get small weights. Turning points $( p _ { i }$ small) have low observation quality and small weights.

## 6.7.2 Regularization Term

The regularization term $\mathcal { R } ( g )$ preserves the geometric meaning of $g .$ Three regularizations.

Metric regularization. $\mathcal { R } ( g ) = \| g - g _ { 0 } \| _ { F } ^ { 2 }$ , where $g _ { 0 }$ is the prior metric. This regularization pulls toward the prior.

Intrinsic metric regularization. $\mathcal { R } ( g ) = \| \Sigma - \Sigma _ { 0 } \| _ { F } ^ { 2 }$ , where $\Sigma _ { 0 }$ is the Fisher prior (Section 3.6). This regularization preserves Fisher objectivity.

Low-rank regularization. $\mathcal { R } ( g ) = \lVert g \rVert$ 火 (nuclear norm), inducing a low-rank metric.

## 6.8 Chapter Summary

This chapter establishes the residual theory and identifiability analysis of SKG.

Residual equation. The residual $r = \ddot { z } + \Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } ) - P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H + \alpha \dot { z }$ . When the model is exact, $r = - g \nabla S$ . The symplectic part cancels exactly in the residual (Theorem 6.2). The geometric force does not cancel; it is produced by the state dependence of the metric.

One-dimensional observable. $g _ { 2 } ^ { o b s } ( q _ { i } ) = - ( \ddot { p } _ { i } + q _ { i } ) / p _ { i } \ \mathrm { ( P }$ roposition 6.3). Applicability is strict: one-dimensional, single direction, no damping, $S = p ^ { 2 } / 2$ , zero geometric force.

Four-dimensional rank condition. $r _ { i } = - A _ { i } w$ , uniquely solvable if ran $k ( [ A _ { 1 } ; \dots ; A _ { N } ] ) =$ $7$ (Proposition 6.4).

Noise propagation. $\sigma _ { g _ { 2 } } ( q _ { i } ) = \sigma / | p _ { i } |$ (Proposition 6.5). Noise amplifies at turning points; suppressed by the weighting scheme (174).

$g _ { q q }$ unidentifiable. When $S = S ( p ) , g _ { q q }$ does not appear in the residual, is structurally unidentifiable (Proposition 6.6). The number of identifiable components is $( 3 n ^ { 2 } + n ) / 2$ . Engineering handling: fix $g _ { q q } = I .$

Potential–metric coupling. Potential error pollutes the metric estimate through $\varepsilon _ { p p }$ (Proposition 6.7). Downweighting scheme (190).

Core formulas:

• Residual: $r = \ddot { z } + \Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } ) - P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H + \alpha \dot { z } ;$

• One-dimensional observable: $g _ { 2 } ^ { o b s } = - ( \ddot { p } + q ) / p ;$

• Four-dimensional rank: rank $\begin{array} { r } { \langle \left[ \boldsymbol { A } _ { 1 } ; \ldots ; \boldsymbol { A } _ { N } \right] \rangle = 7 \langle \boldsymbol { \qquad } } \end{array}$

• Noise propagation: $\sigma _ { g _ { 2 } } = \sigma / | p | ;$

$g _ { q q }$ unidentifiable: $( g \nabla S ) _ { q q } = 0$

• Potential coupling: $\varepsilon _ { p p } M ^ { - 1 } p = - \partial _ { q } \varepsilon _ { V }$

## 7 Kernel Parameterization and OOD Error Decomposition

Chapter 4 established the function-space theory of the state-dependent intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta } ( \cdot )$ and Chapter 6 established the residual theory for calibrating the metric from trajectory data. This chapter discusses the specific form of metric parameterization and gives a decomposition of the OOD error.

Kernel parameterization is the metric parameterization choice in the SKG framework. Compared with neural network parameterization, kernel parameterization has four structural advantages: representability, analytic regularization, generalization bounds, and optimization landscape. This chapter focuses on generalization bounds and OOD error decomposition.

The structure of this chapter is as follows. Section 7.1 establishes the motivation and form of kernel parameterization. Section 7.2 gives the kernel approximation rate. Section 7.3 compares kernel parameterization with neural network parameterization. Section 7.4 establishes the OOD error decomposition. Section 7.5 summarizes.

## 7.1 Motivation and Form of Kernel Parameterization

## 7.1.1 Two Choices for Metric Parameterization

The intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta } ( \cdot )$ has two natural parameterization choices.

Kernel parameterization. Represent $g _ { \Delta }$ as a function in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS):

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \alpha _ { j } \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta , \Delta _ { j } ) + \varepsilon I ,\tag{192}
$$

where $\kappa _ { \Delta } : \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \times \mathbb { R } ^ { M } \to \mathbb { R }$ is a scalar kernel function, $\{ \Delta _ { j } \} _ { j = 1 } ^ { P }$ are centers, $\alpha _ { j } \in \mathrm { S y m } ( M )$ are matrix coeficients, and $\varepsilon > 0$ is a regularization constant.

Neural network parameterization. Represent $g _ { \Delta }$ using a multi-layer perceptron (MLP):

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = \mathrm { M L P } _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) ,\tag{193}
$$

where ${ \mathrm { M L P } } _ { \theta }$ is a multi-layer perceptron with parameters $\theta ,$ outputting an $M \times M$ symmetric positive definite matrix.

Both parameterizations can represent arbitrary continuous functions (universal approximation). The diference lies in the structural prior.

## 7.1.2 Advantages of Kernel Parameterization

Kernel parameterization has four structural advantages in the SKG framework.

Advantage 1 (representability). The representability of the kernel family has a rigorous dimension lower bound. For $n = 2 .$ , the kernel family $\mathcal { K } _ { D , 1 }$ exactly covers Sym $^ + ( 2 )$ . For $n \geq 3$ the dimension lower bound is $r ( 2 n - r + 1 ) \geq n ( n - 1 )$

Advantage 2 (analytic regularization). Under kernel parameterization, three types of regularization (commutativity regularization, curvature regularization, analytic anchor regularization) have explicit forms. These regularizations can be embedded directly in the training loss.

Advantage 3 (generalization bounds). The Rademacher complexity of kernel parameterization has a rigorous upper bound. Under the same Lipschitz constraint, the generalization bound of kernel metrics is tighter than that of MLP metrics.

Advantage 4 (optimization landscape). Under kernel parameterization, the loss function (when $r > r ^ { * } )$ satisfies the strict saddle property. Gradient descent with random perturbation converges to the global optimum almost everywhere.

This chapter focuses on Advantages 3 and 4. Advantages 1 and 2 are discussed in Section 3.7 and subsequent sections.

## 7.1.3 Specific Forms of Kernel Parameterization

Kernel parameterization has two implementations.

Form 1 (matrix-valued kernel expansion).

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \alpha _ { j } \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta , \Delta _ { j } ) + \varepsilon I ,\tag{194}
$$

where $\alpha _ { j } \in \mathrm { S y m } ( M )$ are matrix coeficients. Positive definiteness is not automatically guaranteed; use Cholesky parameterization $\alpha _ { j } = \beta _ { j } \beta _ { j } ^ { \intercal }$

Form 2 (Cholesky kernel expansion).

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) ^ { \top } ,\tag{195}
$$

where $L _ { \theta }$ is a lower-triangular matrix function, parameterized as a kernel expansion

$$
L _ { \theta } ( \Delta ) = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \beta _ { j } \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta , \Delta _ { j } ) + \varepsilon I ,\tag{196}
$$

where $\beta _ { j }$ are lower-triangular matrix coeficients. This form automatically guarantees positive definiteness.

We adopt Form 2 because it automatically guarantees positive definiteness and is compatible with the kernel expansion.

## 7.2 Kernel Approximation Rate

## 7.2.1 Definition of the Approximation Rate

Definition 7.1 (Kernel approximation rate). Let $g _ { \Delta } ^ { * }$ be the true intrinsic metric and $\hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { ( P ) }$ be the estimate using a kernel expansion with P centers. The kernel approximation rate is defined as

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { \Delta \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M } } \| g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } ( \Delta ) - \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { ( P ) } ( \Delta ) \| \le C \cdot P ^ { - s / M } ,\tag{197}
$$

where s is the smoothness of $g _ { \Delta } ^ { * }$ and $C$ is a constant.

Meaning: the approximation error of the kernel expansion decays as $P ^ { - s / M }$ with the number of centers P. Larger s (smoother function) gives faster decay. Larger M (higher-dimensional space) gives slower decay (curse of dimensionality).

## 7.2.2 Proof of the Approximation Rate

Theorem 7.2 (Kernel approximation rate). Let $g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } \in \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } }$ with $\| g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } } \leq R$ . Let the kernel $\kappa _ { \Delta }$ satisfy:

1. Translation invariance: $\kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta , \Delta ^ { \prime } ) = \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta - \Delta ^ { \prime } )$ ;

2. Smoothness: $\kappa _ { \Delta } \in C ^ { s } ( \mathbb { R } ^ { M } ) , s \geq 2$

3. Fourier transform decay: $\hat { \kappa } _ { \Delta } ( \omega ) \leq C ( 1 + \| \omega \| ) ^ { - s }$

Then for any $\varepsilon > 0$ , there exist P centers such that

$$
\operatorname* { s u p } _ { \Delta \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M } } \| g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } ( \Delta ) - \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { ( P ) } ( \Delta ) \| \le C ^ { \prime } \cdot P ^ { - s / M } + \varepsilon ,\tag{198}
$$

where $C ^ { \prime }$ is a constant.

Proof. By RKHS approximation theory [41].

Step 1 (Fourier representation of the kernel). By translation invariance and Fourier decay,

$$
\kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta - \Delta ^ { \prime } ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { M } } e ^ { i \omega \cdot ( \Delta - \Delta ^ { \prime } ) } \hat { \kappa } _ { \Delta } ( \omega ) d \omega .\tag{199}
$$

Step 2 (Fourier representation of the kernel expansion). Using a kernel expansion with $P$ centers:

$$
\hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { ( P ) } ( \Delta ) = \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \alpha _ { j } \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta - \Delta _ { j } ) .\tag{200}
$$

Its Fourier representation is

$$
\hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { ( P ) } ( \Delta ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { M } } e ^ { i \omega \cdot \Delta } \left( \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \alpha _ { j } e ^ { - i \omega \cdot \Delta _ { j } } \right) \hat { \kappa } _ { \Delta } ( \omega ) d \omega .\tag{201}
$$

Step 3 (approximation error). Let the Fourier representation of $g _ { \Delta } ^ { * }$ be $\hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { * } ( \omega )$ . The approximation error is

$$
g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } ( \Delta ) - \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { ( P ) } ( \Delta ) = \int _ { \mathbb { R } ^ { M } } e ^ { i \omega \cdot \Delta } \left( \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { * } ( \omega ) - \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { P } \alpha _ { j } e ^ { - i \omega \cdot \Delta _ { j } } \hat { \kappa } _ { \Delta } ( \omega ) \right) d \omega .\tag{202}
$$

Step 4 (choice of centers and coeficients). Choose centers $\{ \Delta _ { j } \}$ and coeficients $\{ \alpha _ { j } \}$ to minimize the integral in the low-frequency range. By the Fourier decay, the high-frequency contribution is ${ \cal O } ( P ^ { - s / M } )$ . The low-frequency contribution is determined by the coverage of centers.

Step 5 (curse of dimensionality). In an M-dimensional space, P centers have covering radius $P ^ { - 1 / \dot { M } }$ . The Fourier decay gives an approximation rate of $P ^ { - s / M }$ □

Remark 7.3 (Meaning of the approximation rate). The approximation rate $P ^ { - s / M }$ indicates two phenomena.

(1) Smoothness acceleration. Larger s (smoother $g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } )$ gives faster approximation. ${ \cal I } f g _ { \Delta } ^ { \ast }$ is $C ^ { \infty }$ , the rate can be arbitrarily fast (for any $s )$

(2) Curse of dimensionality. Larger M gives slower approximation. For $M = 1 0 , s = 2 ,$ the rate is $P ^ { - 0 . 2 }$ . One needs $P \sim 1 0 ^ { 5 }$ centers to achieve $1 0 ^ { - 1 }$ accuracy. This is the curse of dimensionality.

Engineering meaning: kernel expansion requires many centers in high dimensions. In practice, for $M > 1 0$ , use low-rank approximation or neural network parameterization.

## 7.3 Kernel vs Neural Network Parameterization

## 7.3.1 Rademacher Complexity

Definition 7.4 (Rademacher complexity). Let $\mathcal { F }$ be a function family and $\{ z _ { i } \} _ { i = 1 } ^ { N }$ be sample points. The Rademacher complexity is defined as

$$
\mathcal { R } _ { N } ( \mathcal { F } ) = \mathbb { E } _ { \sigma } \left[ \operatorname* { s u p } _ { f \in \mathcal { F } } \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \sigma _ { i } f ( z _ { i } ) \right] ,\tag{203}
$$

where $\sigma _ { i } \in \{ - 1 , + 1 \}$ are independent Rademacher random variables and $\mathbb { E } _ { \sigma }$ is the expectation over $\boldsymbol { \sigma } = ( \sigma _ { 1 } , \dots , \sigma _ { N } )$ .

Rademacher complexity measures the “richness” of the function family $\mathcal { F }$ on the sample points. Larger complexity means a more flexible family and a larger generalization error bound.

## 7.3.2 Generalization Bound Gap

Theorem 7.5 (Generalization bound gap). Let the kernel metric family $\boldsymbol { \mathcal { K } } _ { D , r }$ and the MLP metric family ${ { \ K } _ { M L P } }$ be under the same Lipschitz constraint. Then

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { R } _ { N } ( { \cal K } _ { D , r } ) \leq \mathcal { R } _ { N } ( { \cal K } _ { M L P } ) - \Delta ( N , r ) , } \end{array}\tag{204}
$$

where $\Delta ( N , r ) > 0$ is the gap, depending on the sample size N and rank r.

Proof. Step 1 (parameterization of the kernel metric). The kernel metric $\boldsymbol { \mathcal { K } } _ { D , r }$ is parameterized by a diagonal parameter D (M parameters) and a low-rank parameter $U \ \left( M r \right.$ parameters):

$$
g _ { \Delta } = D ^ { - 1 } + U D ^ { - 1 } U ^ { \top } .\tag{205}
$$

Independent degrees of freedom: $O ( M r )$ (accounting for gauge symmetry).

Step 2 (parameterization of the MLP metric). The MLP metric ${ \kappa _ { \mathrm { M L P } } }$ is parameterized by a multi-layer perceptron. The full matrix output has $O ( M ^ { 2 } )$ parameters.

Step 3 (Rademacher complexity). Under the same Lipschitz constraint, the Rademacher complexity is controlled by the number of parameters:

$$
\mathcal { R } _ { N } ( \mathcal { F } ) \leq C \cdot \frac { \mathrm { L i p } ( \mathcal { F } ) \cdot \sqrt { \mathrm { d i m } ( \mathcal { F } ) } } { \sqrt { N } } ,\tag{206}
$$

where $\operatorname { L i p } ( { \mathcal { F } } )$ is the Lipschitz constant and dim(F) is the number of parameters.

Step 4 (gap). The parameter count of the kernel metric $O ( M r )$ is smaller than that of the MLP $O ( M ^ { 2 } )$ . Hence

$$
\mathcal { R } _ { N } ( K _ { D , r } ) \leq C \cdot \frac { L \sqrt { M r } } { \sqrt { N } } \leq C \cdot \frac { L \sqrt { M ^ { 2 } } } { \sqrt { N } } - \Delta ( N , r ) = \mathcal { R } _ { N } ( K _ { \mathrm { M L P } } ) - \Delta ( N , r ) ,\tag{207}
$$

where $\begin{array} { r } { \Delta ( N , r ) = C \cdot \frac { L ( \sqrt { M ^ { 2 } } - \sqrt { M r } ) } { \sqrt { N } } > 0 \ ( \mathrm { w h e n } \ r < M ) } \end{array}$

Remark 7.6 (Source of the gap). The generalization gap $\Delta ( N , r )$ comes from the diference in parameter count. The kernel metric has $O ( M r )$ parameters, smaller than the $M L P _ { \ \cal S } ^ { \prime } \ O ( M ^ { 2 } )$ (when $r \ll M )$ . Under the same Lipschitz constraint, families with fewer parameters generalize better.

Boundary case. If the MLP’s parameter count is restricted to ${ \cal O } ( M r ) ( e . g .$ , using a low-rank MLP), the generalization gap disappears. In this case, the kernel parameterization’s advantage is only its analyticity—the explicit forms of the three regularizations $\mathcal { R } _ { 1 } , \mathcal { R } _ { 2 } , \mathcal { R } _ { 3 }$

## 7.3.3 Computational Complexity Comparison

Table 13: Computational complexity comparison
<table><tr><td>Operation</td><td>Kernel parameterization MLP parameterization</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Parameter count</td><td> $O ( M r )$ </td><td> $O ( M ^ { 2 } )$ </td></tr><tr><td>Forward computation</td><td> $O ( M ^ { 2 } )$ </td><td> $O ( M ^ { 2 } )$ </td></tr><tr><td>Backpropagation</td><td> $O ( M ^ { 2 } )$ </td><td> $O ( M ^ { 3 } )$ </td></tr><tr><td> $\mathcal { R } _ { 1 }$  computation</td><td> $O ( M r ^ { 2 } )$ </td><td> $O ( M ^ { 3 } )$ </td></tr><tr><td>Regularization</td><td>Analytic form</td><td>Requires autodiff</td></tr></table>

Kernel parameterization outperforms MLP in backpropagation and regularization computation.

## 7.3.4 Optimization Landscape

Theorem 7.7 (Strict saddle property). Let the true metric $M ^ { * } ( \Delta ) = C ( \Delta ) ^ { - 1 }$ have a minimalrank decomposition $r ^ { * }$ in the kernel family. If $r > r ^ { * }$ , then the loss function

$$
\mathcal { L } ( D , U ) = \mathbb { E } _ { \Delta \in \mathcal { D } } \left\| D ( \Delta ) + U ( \Delta ) ^ { \top } U ( \Delta ) - M ^ { * } ( \Delta ) \right\| _ { F } ^ { 2 }\tag{208}
$$

satisfies the strict saddle property: all saddle points have negative Hessian eigenvalues.

Proof. The saddle property of the matrix factorization loss was established by Ge–Jin–Zheng [40].

Step 1 (Riemannian geometric analysis). Define a natural Riemannian metric on the kernel family $\boldsymbol { \mathcal { K } } _ { D , r }$

Step 2 (Hessian at saddle points). The Hessian at a saddle point has negative curvature in the “scaling direction”.

Step 3 (escape when $r > r ^ { * } )$ . When $r > r ^ { * }$ , extra degrees of freedom allow escape from saddle points. Hence all saddle points have negative Hessian eigenvalues. □

Remark 7.8 (Meaning of the strict saddle property). Meaning: all non-globally-optimal stationary points are saddle points (with negative-curvature directions), not local minima. Gradient descent with random perturbation can escape all saddle points and converges to the global optimum almost everywhere.

Boundary case $( r = r ^ { * } )$ . The strict saddle property is not guaranteed. Benign initialization or second-order methods are needed. This is directly related to the $n = 2 , r = 1$ case: $r = 1 = r ^ { * }$ lies on the boundary.

## 7.4 OOD Error Decomposition

## 7.4.1 Sources of OOD Error

OOD error is the model error at out-of-distribution points. OOD error has three sources.

Source 1 (fitting error). The model does not fully fit the true function in the training distribution. This error decreases with training set size N and model complexity.

Source 2 (approximation error). The model’s hypothesis space cannot represent the true function. This error decreases with model complexity.

Source 3 (extrapolation error). When OOD points are far from the training distribution, the model’s extrapolation ability degrades. This error increases with OOD distance.

## 7.4.2 OOD Error Decomposition Theorem

Theorem 7.9 (OOD error decomposition). Let $g _ { \Delta } ^ { * }$ be the true intrinsic metric and $\hat { g } _ { \Delta }$ be the learned intrinsic metric. Then the error at OOD point $\Delta _ { * }$ satisfies

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { * } ) - g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } ( \Delta _ { * } ) \| \le \underbrace { \epsilon _ { f i t } } _ { f i t t i n g \ e r r o r } + \underbrace { \epsilon _ { a p p r o x } } _ { a p p r o x i m a t i o n \ e r r o r } + \underbrace { \epsilon _ { e x t r a p } } _ { e x t r a p o l a t i o n \ e r r o r } , } \end{array}\tag{209}
$$

where each term is defined below.

Proof. By the triangle inequality,

$$
\| \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { * } ) - g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } ( \Delta _ { * } ) \| \leq \| \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { * } ) - \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { \infty } ( \Delta _ { * } ) \| + \| \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { \infty } ( \Delta _ { * } ) - g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } ( \Delta _ { * } ) \| ,\tag{210}
$$

where $\hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { \infty }$ is the optimal function in the hypothesis space (with infinitely many samples).

The first term $\| \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { * } ) - \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { \infty } ( \Delta _ { * } ) \|$ is the fitting error, originating from finite samples. The second term $\| \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ^ { \infty } ( \Delta _ { * } ) - g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } ( \Delta _ { * } ) \|$ is the approximation error, originating from the hypothesis space. The second term further decomposes into approximation error within the training distribution and OOD extrapolation error. □

## 7.4.3 Fitting Error

The fitting error $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { f i t } }$ originates from finite samples. Within the training distribution, by statistical learning theory,

$$
\epsilon _ { \mathrm { f i t } } \leq C \cdot \mathcal { R } _ { N } ( \mathcal { K } ) \leq C ^ { \prime } \cdot \frac { L \sqrt { M r } } { \sqrt { N } } ,\tag{211}
$$

where $\mathcal { R } _ { N } ( \mathcal { K } )$ is the Rademacher complexity, L is the Lipschitz constant, and N is the sample size.

The fitting error decays as $N ^ { - 1 / 2 }$ . Sample size criterion (Corollary 3.23):

$$
N \geq \frac { M \cdot \kappa ( \Sigma ^ { * } ) ^ { 4 } } { \epsilon _ { \mathrm { f i t } } ^ { 2 } } .\tag{212}
$$

## 7.4.4 Approximation Error

The approximation error $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r o x } }$ originates from the hypothesis space restriction. The approximation error of the kernel expansion is

$$
\epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r o x } } \leq C \cdot P ^ { - s / M } ,\tag{213}
$$

where $P$ is the number of kernel expansion centers and s is the smoothness of the true function.

The approximation error decays as $P ^ { - s / M }$

## 7.4.5 Extrapolation Error

The extrapolation error $\boldsymbol { \epsilon } _ { \mathrm { e x t r a p } }$ originates from OOD points being far from the training distribution. By the RKHS extrapolation bound (Theorem 4.15),

$$
\epsilon _ { \mathrm { e x t r a p } } \leq R \cdot \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } ) } ,\tag{214}
$$

where $R = \| g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } }$ is the RKHS norm, $\kappa _ { \Delta }$ is the kernel function, and $\Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } = \Delta _ { * } - \Delta _ { I D }$

The extrapolation error increases with OOD distance. When $\kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } )  0$ (OOD point far from the training distribution), the extrapolation error tends to $R \sqrt { 2 }$

## 7.4.6 Complete OOD Error Decomposition

Combining the three errors:

$$
\epsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( \Delta _ { * } ) \leq \underbrace { C ^ { \prime } \cdot \frac { L \sqrt { M r } } { \sqrt { N } } } _ { \epsilon _ { \mathrm { f i t } } } + \underbrace { C \cdot P ^ { - s / M } } _ { \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r o x } } } + \underbrace { R \cdot \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } ) } } _ { \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e x t r a p } } } .\tag{215}
$$

This decomposition gives a quantitative description of the three sources of OOD error.

Remark 7.10 (Error trade-ofs). There are trade-ofs among the three errors.

Model complexity. Increasing model complexity (increasing r or P) reduces approximation error but increases fitting error (larger Rademacher complexity).

Sample size. Increasing sample size N reduces fitting error but does not afect approximation error or extrapolation error.

Kernel choice. The choice of kernel $\kappa _ { \Delta }$ afects extrapolation error and approximation error. Smooth kernels (e.g., Gaussian) have small approximation error but large extrapolation error. Rough kernels have small extrapolation error but large approximation error.

Engineering meaning: the error decomposition gives criteria for choosing hyperparameters $( r , P , \kappa _ { \Delta } , N )$ . The optimal choice balances the three errors.

## 7.4.7 Experimental Validation of the OOD Error

The E9 experiment in Chapter 10 validates the RKHS extrapolation bound (Theorem 4.15). Result: average tightness ratio $6 \times 1 0 ^ { - 4 }$ , the bound holds but is overly loose (about 1700 times larger than the actual error). The looseness comes from over-conservative estimates of the RKHS norm R and the kernel function $\kappa _ { \Delta }$

Engineering improvement: using more accurate R estimates (e.g., cross-validation) and more accurate $\kappa _ { \Delta }$ estimates (e.g., kernel ridge regression) can tighten the bound.

## 7.5 Chapter Summary

This chapter establishes kernel parameterization theory and OOD error decomposition.

Kernel parameterization. Represent the intrinsic metric as a function in an RKHS. Two forms: matrix-valued kernel expansion and Cholesky kernel expansion. The Cholesky form automatically guarantees positive definiteness.

Kernel approximation rate. The approximation error of the kernel expansion is ${ \cal O } ( P ^ { - s / M } )$ (Theorem 7.2). Larger s gives faster decay; larger M gives slower decay (curse of dimensionality).

Kernel vs MLP. Kernel parameterization has a rigorous upper bound on Rademacher complexity; its generalization bound is tighter than $\mathrm { M L P ^ { \prime } s }$ (Theorem 7.5). The gap $\Delta ( N , r ) > 0$ when $r < M$ . When $r > r ^ { * }$ , the loss function satisfies the strict saddle property (Theorem 7.7), and gradient descent converges to the global optimum almost everywhere.

OOD error decomposition. The OOD error decomposes into fitting, approximation, and extrapolation errors (Theorem 7.9). There are trade-ofs among the three. Experimental vali dation: E9 validates the RKHS extrapolation bound, which holds but is overly loose (tightness ratio $6 \times 1 0 ^ { - 4 } )$ .

Core formulas:

• Kernel expansion: $\begin{array} { r } { g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ) = \sum _ { j } \alpha _ { j } \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta , \Delta _ { j } ) + \varepsilon I ; } \end{array}$

• Kernel approximation rate: $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r o x } } \leq C \cdot P ^ { - s / M } ;$

• Generalization gap: $\mathcal { R } _ { N } ( { \mathcal { K } } _ { D , r } ) \leq \mathcal { R } _ { N } ( { \mathcal { K } } _ { \mathrm { M L P } } ) - \Delta ( N , r ) ;$

• OOD error decomposition: ϵ<sub>OOD</sub> $\leq \epsilon _ { \mathrm { f i t } } + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r o x } } + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e x t r a p } } .$

## 8 Structural OOD Advantages

Chapter 7 established the generalization bounds of kernel parameterization and the OOD error decomposition. This chapter establishes the structural OOD advantages, proving strict OOD gains in group directions.

The core question of this chapter is: does the metric parameterization of the SKG framework (kernel metric), compared with unstructured parameterization (MLP metric), have a structural advantage in the OOD error along group directions? The answer: under orthogonal representations, the kernel metric’s OOD error does not exceed its ID error, while the MLP metric has a Lipschitz lower bound. The two form a structural contrast.

The structure of this chapter is as follows. Section 8.1 gives the SKG OOD bound. Section 8.2 defines the symplectic contribution ratio. Section 8.3 establishes the strict OOD gain theorem in group directions, including implicit assumptions, equivariance prerequisites, the three-step proof, and specific forms for each group. Section 8.4 discusses the triviality of the lower bound. Section 8.5 summarizes.

## 8.1 SKG OOD Bound

## 8.1.1 Recall of the OOD Error Decomposition

Chapter 7 established the OOD error decomposition (Theorem 7.9):

$$
\epsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( \Delta _ { * } ) \leq \epsilon _ { \mathrm { f i t } } + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r o x } } + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e x t r a p } } ,\tag{216}
$$

where $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { { f i t } } }$ is the fitting error, $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r o x } }$ is the approximation error, and $\boldsymbol { \epsilon } _ { \mathrm { e x t r a p } }$ is the extrapolation error.

Substituting the specific expressions:

$$
\epsilon _ { \mathrm { f i t } } \leq C _ { 1 } \cdot \frac { L \sqrt { M r } } { \sqrt { N } } ,\tag{217}
$$

$$
\epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r o x } } \leq C _ { 2 } \cdot P ^ { - s / M } ,\tag{218}
$$

$$
\epsilon _ { \mathrm { e x t r a p } } \leq R \cdot \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } ) } .\tag{219}
$$

## 8.1.2 SKG OOD Bound

Combining the three errors, the SKG OOD bound is

$$
\epsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ^ { \mathrm { S K G } } ( \Delta _ { * } ) \leq C _ { 1 } D ^ { - s / d } + C _ { 2 } \sigma A ( \Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } ) \sqrt { D / N } + R \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } ) } ,\tag{220}
$$

where:

• D: efective dimension (related to the number of kernel expansion centers $P )$ ;

$\sigma \mathrm { : }$ noise standard deviation;

$A ( \Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } )$ : amplification factor of the extrapolation distance;

$R = \| g _ { \Delta } ^ { * } \| _ { \mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } } }$ : RKHS norm;

$\kappa \Delta \colon$ kernel function.

Equation (220) is the OOD error upper bound of the SKG framework. The three terms correspond to approximation, fitting, and extrapolation errors.

## 8.1.3 Relation to the Extrapolation Distance

In equation (220), the extrapolation distance $\Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } = \Delta _ { * } - \Delta _ { I D }$ appears in two terms. As $\Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } }$ increases:

$A ( \Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } )$ increases, so the fitting error increases;

$\kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } )$ decreases (moving away from the training distribution), so the extrapolation error increases.

The OOD error increases with the extrapolation distance.

## 8.2 Symplectic Contribution Ratio

## 8.2.1 Definition

Definition 8.1 (Symplectic contribution ratio). The symplectic contribution ratio $\rho _ { H }$ is defined as

$$
\rho _ { H } = \frac { \| \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H \| } { \| \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H \| + \| g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S \| } ,\tag{221}
$$

where $\| \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H \|$ is the magnitude of the symplectic force and $\| g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S \|$ is the magnitude of the dissipative force.

The symplectic contribution ratio takes values in [0, 1]. $\rho _ { H } = 0$ means pure dissipation (zero symplectic force); $\rho _ { H } = 1$ means pure conservative (zero dissipative force).

## 8.2.2 Meaning

The symplectic contribution ratio measures the relative weight of the conservative and dissipative parts in the SKG system. When $\rho _ { H }$ is large, the conservative part dominates, and the OOD error comes mainly from the dissipative part (which is known). When $\rho _ { H }$ is small, the dissipative part dominates, and the OOD error comes mainly from the learning error of the dissipative part.

The symplectic contribution ratio is a diagnostic quantity in the SKG framework. It has no theoretical guarantee but can be used for engineering judgment: in systems with large $\rho _ { H }$ , the SKG OOD advantage is more significant.

## 8.3 Strict OOD Gain in Group Directions

## 8.3.1 Implicit Assumptions

The results of this section depend on the following four implicit assumptions.

Assumption 8.2 (Linear group action). The group action $T _ { g }$ is linear on the state space:

$$
T _ { g } \cdot q = \rho ( g ) q ,\tag{222}
$$

where $\rho : G \to G L ( \mathbb { R } ^ { d } )$ is the representation of the group G on the state space.

Assumption 8.3 (Orthogonal representation). The representation ρ of the group G is orthogonal:

$$
\rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } = I , \quad \forall g \in G .\tag{223}
$$

Equivalently, $\rho ( g )$ is an orthogonal matrix, $\rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } = \rho ( g ) ^ { \top }$

Orthogonal representations cover the rotation group $S O ( d )$ , the rigid-body motion group $S E ( 3 )$ , and the translation group $\mathbb { R } ^ { d } [ 2 6 , 2 7 , 2 8 ]$ . The regularization design for non-orthogonal representations (such as the scale group $\mathbb { R } ^ { + } )$ is an open problem in Chapter 11.

Assumption 8.4 (Constant intrinsic metric). The intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta }$ is a constant matrix (independent $o f \Delta )$

Assumption 8.5 (MLP inverse Lipschitz condition). There exists $L _ { M } > 0$ such that the MLP metric g<sub>M</sub> satisfies

$$
\lVert g _ { M } ( q _ { * } ) - g _ { M } ( q _ { 0 } ) \rVert \geq L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , q _ { 0 } ) ,\tag{224}
$$

for all $q _ { * } , q _ { 0 } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$

Remark 8.6 (The inverse Lipschitz condition is not automatic). The inverse Lipschitz condition is not an automatic property of MLPs. For a general MLP, the smallest singular value of the Jacobian may approach zero. MLP classes satisfying the inverse Lipschitz condition include:

(1) Bi-Lipschitz networks: both the Lipschitz and inverse Lipschitz constants are bounded;

(2) Networks with a lower bound on the smallest singular value of the Jacobian: the smallest singular value of the Jacobian $i s \geq L _ { M }$ at all points;

(3) Strongly monotone networks: the network satisfies $\langle g _ { M } ( q _ { * } ) - g _ { M } ( q _ { 0 } ) , q _ { * } - q _ { 0 } \rangle \geq L _ { M } \Vert q _ { * } -$ $q _ { 0 } \| ^ { 2 }$

In the OOD region, the Jacobian of an $M L P$ may degenerate, and the inverse Lipschitz condition may fail.

## 8.3.2 Equivariance Prerequisites

Assumption 8.7 (True metric equivariant). The true metric $g ^ { * }$ satisfies the equivariance:

$$
g ^ { * } ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g ^ { * } ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } , \quad \forall g \in G .\tag{225}
$$

Assumption 8.8 (Invariant correctly encoded). The kernel metric $g _ { K }$ is correctly encoded by invariants:

$$
g _ { K } ( q ) = { J ( q ) } ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } { J ( q ) } + \varepsilon _ { G } I .\tag{226}
$$

This section assumes the representation $\rho$ of the group G is orthogonal (Assumption 8.3).

Assumption 8.9 (OOD comes from the group action). The OOD point $q _ { * }$ is obtained from a training-distribution point $q _ { 0 }$ via the group action:

$$
q _ { * } = T _ { g } \cdot q _ { 0 } , \quad q _ { 0 } \in D _ { t r a i n } .\tag{227}
$$

## 8.3.3 Three-Step Proof

Lemma 1 (equivariance of the invariant metric).

Lemma 8.10 (Equivariance of the kernel metric). Under Assumptions 8.3 and 8.8, the kernel metric $g _ { K }$ satisfies

$$
g _ { K } ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g _ { K } ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } .\tag{228}
$$

Proof. By the chain rule and the invariant definition, the invariant Jacobian satisfies

$$
J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = J ( q ) \cdot \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } .\tag{229}
$$

Derivation: $\Delta ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = \Delta ( q )$ (invariant definition). Diferentiating with respect to $q ,$ the left side diferentiated with respect to $T _ { g } \cdot q$ gives $J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) \cdot \rho ( g )$ (since $T _ { g } \cdot q \ : = \ : \rho ( g ) q )$ , and the right side diferentiated with respect to q gives $J ( q )$ . Hence $J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) \cdot \rho ( g ) = J ( q )$ , i.e., $J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = J ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 }$

Substituting into the kernel metric definition (226):

$$
g _ { K } ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = { J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) } ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I\tag{230}
$$

$$
= ( J ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( J ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I\tag{231}
$$

$$
= \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } + \varepsilon _ { G } I\tag{232}
$$

$$
= \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } \left( J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J ( q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I \right) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 }\tag{233}
$$

$$
= \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g _ { K } ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } ,\tag{234}
$$

where the third step uses $( A B ) ^ { \top } = B ^ { \top } A ^ { \top }$ and the fourth step uses $\rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } \varepsilon _ { G } I \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } = \varepsilon _ { G } I$ (orthogonal representation). □

Lemma 2 (transmission from equivariance to OOD error).

Lemma 8.11 (Equivariance transmission). Under Assumptions 8.7 and $\ 8 . 9 ,$

$$
\lVert g _ { K } ( q _ { * } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) \rVert \leq \lVert \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } \rVert ^ { 2 } \cdot \lVert g _ { K } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { 0 } ) \rVert .\tag{235}
$$

Proof. By Lemma 8.10 and Assumption 8.7,

$$
g _ { K } ( q _ { * } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) = g _ { K } ( T _ { g } \cdot q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( T _ { g } \cdot q _ { 0 } )\tag{236}
$$

$$
= \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g _ { K } ( q _ { 0 } ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } - \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g ^ { * } ( q _ { 0 } ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 }\tag{237}
$$

$$
= \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } ( g _ { K } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { 0 } ) ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } .\tag{238}
$$

Taking norms:

$$
\| g _ { K } ( q _ { * } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) \| = \| \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } ( g _ { K } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { 0 } ) ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } \| \leq \| \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } \| ^ { 2 } \cdot \| g _ { K } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { 0 } ) \| .\tag{239}
$$

The last step uses submultiplicativity of matrix norms.

Corollary 8.12 (OOD bound under orthogonal representation). Under orthogonal representation (Assumption 8.3), $\| \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } \| = 1$ , hence

$$
\lVert g _ { K } ( q _ { * } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) \rVert \leq \lVert g _ { K } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { 0 } ) \rVert .\tag{240}
$$

That is, $\varepsilon _ { O O D } ( K ) \leq \varepsilon _ { I D } ( K )$

The OOD bound under orthogonal representation states: the kernel metric’s OOD error does not exceed its ID error. Equivariance converts OOD error into ID error.

Lemma 3 (Lipschitz lower bound of the MLP metric).

Lemma 8.13 (Lipschitz lower bound of the MLP metric). Under Assumption 8.5, for OOD point $q _ { * }$ , define

$$
C : = \varepsilon _ { I D } ( M ) + C _ { t r u e } ,\tag{241}
$$

where:

$\varepsilon _ { I D } ( M ) = \| g _ { M } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { 0 } ) \|$ : the MLP metric ID error;

$C _ { t r u e } : = \| g ^ { * } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) \|$ : the variation of the true metric under the group action.

Then

$$
\varepsilon _ { O O D } ( M ) = \| g _ { M } ( q _ { * } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) \| \geq L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) - C .\tag{242}
$$

Proof. By the triangle inequality,

$$
\| g _ { M } ( q _ { * } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) \| \ge \| g _ { M } ( q _ { * } ) - g _ { M } ( q _ { 0 } ) \| - \| g _ { M } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) \| .\tag{243}
$$

Applying the triangle inequality again to the second term:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| g _ { M } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) \| \leq \| g _ { M } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { 0 } ) \| + \| g ^ { * } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) \| = { \varepsilon } _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( M ) + C _ { \mathrm { t r u e } } = C . } \end{array}\tag{244}
$$

The first term, by the inverse Lipschitz condition (Assumption 8.5):

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \| g _ { M } ( q _ { * } ) - g _ { M } ( q _ { 0 } ) \| \ge L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , q _ { 0 } ) \ge L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) , } \end{array}\tag{245}
$$

where $\begin{array} { r } { d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) = \operatorname* { m i n } _ { q \in D _ { t r a i n } } d ( q _ { * } , q ) \leq d ( q _ { * } , q _ { 0 } ) } \end{array}$

Substituting (244) and (245) into (243):

$$
\| g _ { M } ( q _ { * } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) \| \geq L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) - C .\tag{246}
$$

Corollary 8.14 (Upper bound on C). If the true metric $g ^ { * }$ satisfies the Lipschitz condition:

$$
\lVert g ^ { * } ( q _ { 1 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { 2 } ) \rVert \leq L ^ { * } \cdot d ( q _ { 1 } , q _ { 2 } ) ,\tag{247}
$$

then

$$
C _ { t r u e } = \| g ^ { * } ( q _ { 0 } ) - g ^ { * } ( q _ { * } ) \| \leq L ^ { * } \cdot d ( q _ { 0 } , q _ { * } ) \leq L ^ { * } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) ,\tag{248}
$$

hence

$$
C \leq \varepsilon _ { I D } ( M ) + L ^ { * } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) .\tag{249}
$$

Main theorem.

Theorem 8.15 (OOD gain in group directions). Under Assumptions 8.2–8.5,

$$
\varepsilon _ { O O D } ( K ) \leq \varepsilon _ { I D } ( K ) ,\tag{250}
$$

$$
\varepsilon _ { O O D } ( M ) \geq L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) - C .\tag{251}
$$

If

$$
L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) > C + \varepsilon _ { I D } ( K ) ,\tag{252}
$$

then

$$
\varepsilon _ { O O D } ( M ) - \varepsilon _ { O O D } ( K ) \geq L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) - C - \varepsilon _ { I D } ( K ) > 0 .\tag{253}
$$

Proof. By Corollary 8.12, $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( K ) \leq \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K )$

By Lemma 8.13, ε<sub>OOD</sub>(M) ≥ L<sub>M</sub> · d(q<sub>∗</sub>, D<sub>train</sub>) − C.

By condition (252),

$$
\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( M ) - \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( K ) \geq ( L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) - C ) - \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K ) = L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) - C - \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K ) .\tag{254}
$$

By condition (252), $L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) - C - \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K ) > 0$ . Hence the gain is strictly positive.

Corollary 8.16 (Suficient condition for gain). Substituting the upper bound on C (249) into condition (252):

$$
L _ { M } \cdot d > \varepsilon _ { I D } ( M ) + L ^ { \ast } \cdot d + \varepsilon _ { I D } ( K ) ,\tag{255}
$$

that is,

$$
( L _ { M } - L ^ { * } ) \cdot d > \varepsilon _ { I D } ( M ) + \varepsilon _ { I D } ( K ) ,\tag{256}
$$

where $d = d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } )$

Suficient condition for gain: the MLP inverse Lipschitz constant $L _ { M }$ is larger than the true metric Lipschitz constant $L ^ { * }$ , and the gap $( L _ { M } - L ^ { * } )$ · d is suficient to cover the ID errors $\varepsilon _ { I D } ( M ) + \varepsilon _ { I D } ( K )$

Remark 8.17 (Assumption dependence of the theorem). Theorem 8.15 is an assumptiondependent result, relying on the inverse Lipschitz condition (Assumption 8.5). If the MLP does not satisfy this condition, the theorem’s conclusion is not guaranteed.

It also relies on the orthogonal representation assumption (Assumption 8.3). Under nonorthogonal representations, the regularization term $\varepsilon _ { G } I$ breaks equivariance, and the theorem’s conclusion is not guaranteed.

## 8.3.4 Specific Forms for Each Group

Translation group $G = \mathbb { R } ^ { d }$ . The group action is $q \mapsto q + t$ . Invariants take diference form $\Delta = q _ { i } - q _ { j }$ . The representation $\rho ( g ) = I$ (trivial representation) is orthogonal. Theorem 8.15 applies.

Rotation group $G = S O ( d )$ . Invariant $\Delta = \| q \|$ , invariant Jacobian $J = \boldsymbol { q } ^ { \intercal } / | | \boldsymbol { q } | |$ . The representation $\rho ( g ) = R$ (rotation matrix) is orthogonal $( R ^ { \top } R = I )$ . Theorem 8.15 applies.

Combined group $G = S E ( 3 )$ . The representation $\rho ( g )$ is the orthogonal extension of the rotation part. $\rho ( g ) = { \binom { R } { 0 } } \ 1 \rfloor$ . The overall representation is not orthogonal (due to the translation part), but the rotation part is orthogonal. Theorem 8.15 applies to the rotation part.

Non-orthogonal representation. The scale group $G = \mathbb { R } ^ { + }$ has representation $\rho ( \lambda ) = \lambda$ which is non-orthogonal. Theorem 8.15 does not apply. The regularization design for nonorthogonal representations is an open problem.

## 8.4 Triviality of the Lower Bound

## 8.4.1 Triviality of the Lower Bound

The lower bound part of Theorem 8.15

$$
\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( M ) \geq L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) - C\tag{257}
$$

may give a trivial bound in practice. When $L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) \leq C$ , the lower bound is $\leq 0$ . In this case, the lower bound provides no information (since the error is non-negative).

## 8.4.2 Experimental Validation

The E5 experiment in Chapter 10 validates the form of the bound (257) on 1000 OOD points (0 violations). But the proportion of meaningful lower bounds is zero, i.e., $L _ { M } \cdot d - C < 0$ for all OOD points.

Reasons include:

$C = \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( M ) \approx 0 . 2 6 3$ is large (metric error is relatively large);

$d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } )$ is small in paired-rotation OOD (rotation does not change the distance to the origin);

$L _ { M }$ (the inverse Lipschitz constant of the MLP metric) is not naturally larger than 1.

## 8.4.3 Meaning of the Lower Bound

The substantive contribution of Theorem 8.15 is the upper bound part $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( K ) \leq \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K )$ The lower bound part holds formally but is trivial in this experimental setting, providing no quantitative constraint.

The meaning of the lower bound is that it gives a suficient condition for the group-direction OOD gain (Corollary 8.16). This condition can be satisfied in specific cases $( L _ { M }$ large, d large, ε<sub>ID</sub> small). When the condition is satisfied, the gain is strictly positive.

## 8.5 Chapter Summary

This chapter establishes the structural OOD advantage theory.

SKG OOD bound. $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ^ { \mathrm { S K G } } \leq C _ { 1 } D ^ { - s / d } + C _ { 2 } \sigma A ( \Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } ) \sqrt { D / N } + R \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { \mathrm { i n v } } ) } .$

Symplectic contribution ratio. $\rho _ { H } = \| \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H \| / ( \| \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H \| + \| g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S \| )$ . A diagnostic quantity, no theoretical guarantee.

Strict OOD gain in group directions. Under four implicit assumptions (linear group action, orthogonal representation, constant intrinsic metric, inverse Lipschitz condition):

$\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( K ) \leq \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K )$ (the kernel metric OOD error does not exceed the ID error);

$\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( M ) \geq L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) - C$ (the MLP metric has a Lipschitz lower bound);

• If $L _ { M } \cdot d > C + \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K )$ , the gain is strictly positive (Theorem 8.15).

Specific forms for each group. Translation group, rotation group, and combined group under orthogonal representations apply; scale group under non-orthogonal representation does not apply.

Triviality of the lower bound. The lower bound part is trivial in experiments (meaningful proportion is zero). The substantive contribution is the upper bound part. The meaning of the lower bound is to give a suficient condition for the gain (Corollary 8.16).

Core formulas:

• SKG OOD bound: $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ^ { \mathrm { S K G } } \leq C _ { 1 } D ^ { - s / d } + C _ { 2 } \sigma A \sqrt { D / N } + R \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } }$

• Kernel metric equivariance: $g _ { K } ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g _ { K } ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 }$

• Orthogonal representation OOD bound: $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( K ) \leq \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K )$

• MLP Lipschitz lower bound: $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( M ) \geq L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) - C ;$

• Gain condition: $L _ { M } \cdot d > C + \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K )$

## 9 Learning Flow and Induction Decoupling

Chapters 2–8 established the SKG inference flow: given a metric $g _ { q }$ and energy S, inference is pure geometric evolution. This chapter establishes the learning flow: given the structure of the inference flow, determine the task-relevant part of the energy S.

The core question of this chapter is: in the SKG framework, which parameters need to be learned? Which parameters are objectively determined? The answer: the metric $g _ { q }$ is objectively determined by Fisher information (Section 3.6) and is not learned by gradient descent. The Fisher metric $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ in parameter space is estimated once from task data, also not learned by gradient descent. The learning-decoupling theorem proves that the sole object of induction is the parameters ϕ of the task energy $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$

The structure of this chapter is as follows. Section 9.1 starts from the inference flow and derives the learning flow. Section 9.2 distinguishes metric determination from energy determination. Section 9.3 establishes the learning-decoupling theorem. Section 9.4 establishes the symmetric structure of the inference flow and the learning flow. Section 9.5 establishes the natural gradient form of the learning flow. Section 9.6 gives the two-stage training procedure. Section 9.7 compares with standard machine learning. Section 9.8 discusses analogies with Bayesian inference and statistical physics. Section 9.9 gives engineering criteria. Section 9.10 summarizes.

## 9.1 From Inference Flow to Learning Flow

## 9.1.1 Structure of the Inference Flow

The second-order SKG inference flow is

$$
\nabla _ { \dot { z } } \dot { z } = P _ { \dot { \perp } } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H - g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S - \alpha \dot { z } .\tag{258}
$$

The first-order (overdamped) SKG inference flow is

$$
\dot { q } = - g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } ( q ) \nabla S ( q ) ,\tag{259}
$$

where $g _ { q }$ is the state-space metric and S is the energy functional.

The structure of the inference flow contains three elements:

• State space $q \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d } ;$

• Metric $g _ { q }$ (covariant metric, used to define distances);

• Energy $S : \mathbb { R } ^ { d }  \mathbb { R }$ (scalar function).

Given $g _ { q }$ and S, inference is pure geometric evolution, with no parameter learning.

## 9.1.2 Formulation of the Learning Problem

In actual systems, $g _ { q }$ and $S$ are not completely known. The question is: which parameters need to be learned from data?

In the SKG framework, $g _ { q }$ is obtained by pulling back invariants:

$$
g _ { q } ( q ) = J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta ( q ) ) J ( q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I ,\tag{260}
$$

where $J ( q ) = \partial \Delta / \partial q$ is determined by the invariant definition and $g _ { \Delta }$ is the intrinsic metric on the invariant space.

The energy S decomposes into an objective part and a task part:

$$
S ( q ) = S _ { \mathrm { o b j } } ( q ) + S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } ( q ) ,\tag{261}
$$

where $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } }$ is given by physical laws (known) and $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ is learned from task data (to be estimated).

The learning problem reduces to: how are $g _ { \Delta }$ and $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ determined?

## 9.2 Metric Determination vs Energy Determination

## 9.2.1 Metric Determination: Unsupervised Preprocessing

Section 3.6 established the Fisher origin of the kernel bandwidth matrix. The intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta }$ is uniquely determined by Fisher information:

$$
\hat { g } _ { \Delta } = F ( \hat { \theta } ) ^ { - 1 } , \quad \hat { \theta } = \mathrm { M L E } ( \{ \Delta _ { i } \} ) ,\tag{262}
$$

where $\hat { \theta }$ is the maximum likelihood estimate of the parameters of the invariant distribution $p ( \Delta | \theta )$

Characteristics of metric determination:

• Unsupervised. No task labels are needed, only invariant data $\{ \Delta _ { i } \}$ ;

• Task-independent. By the objectivity theorem (Theorem 3.19), $g _ { \Delta }$ is task-independent;

• Ofline. Can be determined before the task begins;

• One-shot estimation. Not learned by gradient descent, but estimated once by MLE.

Metric determination is not task induction; it is task-independent preprocessing.

## 9.2.2 Energy Determination: Supervised Induction

In the energy $S = S _ { \mathrm { { o b j } } } + S _ { \mathrm { { t a s k } } } , S _ { \mathrm { { o b j } } }$ is known and $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ is to be estimated.

Parameterize $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } = S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } ( q ; \phi )$ , where $\phi$ are the trainable parameters. Determine $\phi$ by residual fitting.

The residual equation is (Section 6.1)

$$
r ( t ) = \dot { q } ( t ) + g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } ( q ( t ) ) \nabla _ { q } S _ { \mathrm { o b j } } ( q ( t ) ) .\tag{263}
$$

Then

$$
r ( t ) = - g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } ( q ( t ) ) \nabla _ { q } S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } ( q ( t ) ; \phi ) .\tag{264}
$$

Solving gives

$$
\nabla _ { q } S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } ( q ( t ) ; \phi ) = - g _ { q } ( q ( t ) ) r ( t ) .\tag{265}
$$

Characteristics of energy determination:

• Supervised. Requires task trajectory data $\{ \boldsymbol { q } ( t ) \}$ ;

• Task-relevant. Diferent tasks yield diferent $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ ;

• Online. Each task is learned separately;

• Learned by gradient descent. Use the natural gradient flow to learn $\phi .$

Energy determination is true induction.

## 9.2.3 Comparison

Table 14: Metric determination vs energy determination
<table><tr><td>Aspect</td><td>Metric determination</td><td>Energy determination</td></tr><tr><td>Object</td><td>g∆</td><td>Parameters  $\phi$  of  $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ </td></tr><tr><td>Data</td><td>Domain data</td><td>Task data</td></tr><tr><td>Supervision</td><td>Unsupervised</td><td>Supervised</td></tr><tr><td>Task-relevant</td><td>No</td><td>Yes</td></tr><tr><td>Time</td><td>Offline</td><td>Online</td></tr><tr><td>Method</td><td>MLE + Fisher</td><td>Natural gradient flow</td></tr></table>

The separation of metric determination and energy determination is a core feature of the SKG framework.

## 9.3 Learning-Decoupling Theorem

## 9.3.1 Theorem Statement

Theorem 9.1 (Learning decoupling). Suppose the following conditions hold:

1. The metric $g _ { q }$ is determined by unsupervised domain data, independent of task data;

2. The energy $S = S _ { o b j } + S _ { t a s k }$ , where $S _ { o b j }$ is given by physical laws;

3. The inference flow is $\dot { q } = - g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$

Then the sole object of induction is the parameters $\phi$ of $S _ { t a s k }$

Proof. Four steps.

Step 1 (metric is not learned). By condition 1, $g _ { q }$ is determined by unsupervised domain data, not learned from task data. $g _ { q }$ is known.

Step 2 (objective energy is not learned). By condition 2, $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } }$ is given by physical laws and is known.

Step 3 (inference flow is fully determined). By condition 3, the inference flow $\dot { q } =$ $- g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ is fully determined by $g _ { q }$ and S. Given $g _ { q }$ and $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } }$ , the only unknown part of the inference flow is $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$

Step 4 (unique learning object). $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ is parameterized by $\phi .$ . Hence the only thing to learn is ϕ.

Conclusion: the sole object of induction is the parameters $\phi$ of $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$

## 9.3.2 Meaning of the Theorem

Meaning: in the SKG framework, the learning problem reduces from “learning all parameters” to “learning the parameters of the task energy”.

Comparison with standard machine learning:

• Standard ML: learn all network parameters (metric, energy, features, etc.);

• SKG: learn the parameters $\phi$ of the task energy.

The SKG learning problem is greatly simplified.

## 9.3.3 Conditions of the Theorem

The conditions of the learning-decoupling theorem are strict. If any condition fails, decoupling does not hold.

Violation of condition 1. If the metric $g _ { q }$ is learned from task data, then the metric is task-relevant and decoupling fails. Avoidance: obtain domain data from task-independent sources, or set the metric directly from domain knowledge.

Violation of condition 2. If the boundary between $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } }$ and $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ is blurred, decoupling fails. The boundary is determined by physical laws; it is a physical modeling problem, not a mathematical one.

Violation of condition 3. If the inference flow is not $\dot { q } = - g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ (e.g., second-order flow), decoupling requires modification. The learning flow for second-order flow is discussed in subsequent sections.

## 9.4 Symmetry of Inference Flow and Learning Flow

## 9.4.1 Symmetric Structure

The inference flow and learning flow share the same geometric structure.

Table 15: Symmetric structure of inference flow and learning flow
<table><tr><td>Aspect</td><td>Inference flow</td><td>Learning flow</td></tr><tr><td>Space</td><td>State space q</td><td>Parameter space  $\phi$ </td></tr><tr><td>Metric</td><td> $g _ { q }$  (objective, task-independent):</td><td> $F _ { S } ( \phi )$  (task-relevant)</td></tr><tr><td>Driving force</td><td> $\nabla { S }$  (energy gradient)</td><td> $\nabla \mathcal { L }$  (loss gradient)</td></tr><tr><td>Dynamics</td><td> $\dot { q } = - g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ </td><td> $\dot { \phi } = - F _ { S } ^ { - 1 } \nabla \mathcal { L }$ </td></tr><tr><td>Learning object None</td><td></td><td> $\phi$  (parameters of  $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } )$ </td></tr><tr><td>Purpose</td><td>Reach target</td><td>Determine task energy</td></tr></table>

Symmetry: state space corresponds to parameter space, metric corresponds to Fisher information, energy gradient corresponds to loss gradient, geometric evolution corresponds to natural gradient descent.

## 9.4.2 Task Relevance of $F _ { S } ( \phi )$

Two kinds of Fisher metrics must be distinguished.

$g _ { \Delta }$ (Fisher of the metric space). Determined by the Fisher information of the invariant distribution $p ( \Delta | \theta )$ :

$$
\begin{array} { r } { g _ { \Delta } = F ( \hat { \theta } ) ^ { - 1 } , \quad F ( \hat { \theta } ) = \mathbb { E } _ { \Delta } [ \nabla _ { \theta } \log p \cdot \nabla _ { \theta } \log p ^ { \top } ] . } \end{array}\tag{266}
$$

$g _ { \Delta }$ is task-independent (Theorem 3.19), not learned by gradient descent.

$F _ { S } ( \phi )$ (Fisher of the parameter space). Determined by the Fisher information of the loss function ${ \mathcal { L } } ( \phi )$

$$
F _ { S } ( \phi ) = \mathbb { E } _ { q \sim p ( q | \phi ) } [ \nabla _ { \phi } \log p ( q | \phi ) \cdot \nabla _ { \phi } \log p ( q | \phi ) ^ { \top } ] .\tag{267}
$$

$F _ { S } ( \phi )$ depends on the loss function ${ \mathcal { L } } ( \phi )$ , which depends on task data. Hence $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ is taskrelevant.

Remark 9.2 (Distinguishing “not learned” from “task-independent”). Neither $g _ { \Delta }$ nor $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ is learned by gradient descent. But their task relevance difers.

<table><tr><td>Object</td><td>Learned?</td><td>Task-relevant?</td></tr><tr><td> $g _ { \Delta }$ </td><td>No</td><td>No</td></tr><tr><td> $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ </td><td>No</td><td>Yes</td></tr></table>

g<sub>∆</sub> is estimated once by MLE (not by gradient descent); $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ is estimated once from task data (not by gradient descent, or with a few iterations). Neither is learned by gradient descent, but $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ is task-relevant.

Distinguishing “not learned” from “task-independent” is key. The learning-decoupling theorem only concerns the distinction of “learning,” not that of “task relevance.”

## 9.5 Natural Gradient Form of the Learning Flow

## 9.5.1 Loss Function

Parameterize $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } = S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } ( q ; \phi )$ , where $\phi \in \mathbb { R } ^ { P }$ are the trainable parameters and P is the number of parameters.

The loss function is the residual fitting error:

$$
\mathcal { L } ( \phi ) = \sum _ { t } \| r ( t ) + g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } ( q ( t ) ) \nabla _ { q } S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } ( q ( t ) ; \phi ) \| ^ { 2 } ,\tag{268}
$$

where $r ( t )$ is the residual (computed from (263)) and $q ( t )$ is trajectory data.

## 9.5.2 Pullback Structure of the Parameter-Space Metric

The parameter-space metric admits a pullback representation that mirrors the pullback construction of Chapter 3. This is the second instance of the pullback principle introduced in Section 1.6.

Let $z ( \theta )$ be the model hidden state and

$$
J _ { 2 } = \frac { \partial z } { \partial \theta } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { D \times P }\tag{269}
$$

the state-to-parameter Jacobian, where D is the hidden-state dimension. Let $g _ { z }$ be the statespace metric on the hidden state z.

Definition 9.3 (Parameter-space pullback metric). The parameter-space pullback metric is

$$
g _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) = J _ { 2 } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) ^ { \top } g _ { z } ( z ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) ) J _ { 2 } ( \boldsymbol { \theta } ) + \varepsilon _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } \boldsymbol { I } ,\tag{270}
$$

where $\varepsilon _ { \theta } > 0$ is a regularization constant ensuring positive definiteness when rank $( J _ { 2 } ) < P$

The pullback structure is identical to that of Chapter 3. The invariant-space metric $g _ { \Delta }$ pulls back to the state-space metric $g _ { q } = J _ { 1 } ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J _ { 1 } + \varepsilon _ { G } I$ through $J _ { 1 } = \partial \Delta / \partial q$ . The state-space metric $g _ { z }$ pulls back to the parameter-space metric $g _ { \theta } = J _ { 2 } ^ { \top } g _ { z } J _ { 2 } + \varepsilon _ { \theta } I$ through $J _ { 2 } = \partial z / \partial \theta$ Both constructions share the same mechanism.

## 9.5.3 Relation Between the Pullback Metric and the Fisher Information

The relationship between the pullback metric $g _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } }$ and the Fisher information $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ requires careful treatment. They coincide only under specific conditions.

Theorem 9.4 (Pullback metric equals Fisher information). Suppose the following three conditions hold:

1. Suficient statistic. The hidden state z is a suficient statistic for the task: $p ( y \mid x , \theta ) =$ $p ( \boldsymbol { y } \mid \boldsymbol { z } ( \boldsymbol { x } , \boldsymbol { \theta } ) )$ ;

2. Negative log-likelihood loss. The loss function is the negative log-likelihood: ${ \mathcal { L } } ( \phi ) =$ $\textstyle - \sum _ { i } \log p ( y _ { i } \mid z ( x _ { i } ; \theta ) )$ ;

3. Objective state-space metric. The state-space metric $g _ { z }$ equals the Fisher information of the state distribution: $g _ { z } = F _ { z } ( z )$

Then the parameter-space pullback metric equals the Fisher information matrix:

$$
g _ { \theta } ( \theta ) = F _ { S } ( \phi ) .\tag{271}
$$

Proof. By the chain rule for Fisher information. Under conditions 1 and 2, the Fisher information of the parameter θ is

$$
\begin{array} { r } { F _ { S } ( \phi ) = \mathbb { E } _ { y \mid z } \left[ \nabla _ { \theta } \log p ( y \mid z ( x ; \theta ) ) \cdot \nabla _ { \theta } \log p ( y \mid z ( x ; \theta ) ) ^ { \top } \right] . } \end{array}\tag{272}
$$

Apply the chain rule $\nabla _ { \theta } \log p ( y \mid z ) = J _ { 2 } ^ { \top } \nabla _ { z } \log p ( y \mid z )$ , where $J _ { 2 } = \partial z / \partial \theta$ . Then

$$
\begin{array} { r } { F _ { S } ( \phi ) = \mathbb { E } _ { y | z } \left[ J _ { 2 } ^ { \top } \nabla _ { z } \log p \cdot \nabla _ { z } \log p ^ { \top } J _ { 2 } \right] = J _ { 2 } ^ { \top } \mathbb { E } _ { y | z } \left[ \nabla _ { z } \log p \cdot \nabla _ { z } \log p ^ { \top } \right] J _ { 2 } = J _ { 2 } ^ { \top } F _ { z } ( z ) J _ { 2 } . } \end{array}\tag{273}
$$

By condition 3, $F _ { z } ( z ) = g _ { z }$ . Hence $F _ { S } ( \phi ) = J _ { 2 } ^ { \top } g _ { z } J _ { 2 } = g _ { \theta } ( \theta )$ (up to the regularization term, which is negligible for suficiently small $\varepsilon _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } } )$ □

Remark 9.5 (Two distinct cases). The pullback metric g<sub>θ</sub> and the Fisher information $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ must be distinguished.

• Suficient statistic case. When conditions 1–3 of Theorem 9.4 hold, $g _ { \theta } = F _ { S } ( \phi )$ . The learning flow is a natural gradient flow with the pulled-back metric. This is the geometric case.

• Non-suficient case. When any of conditions 1–3 fails, $g _ { \theta } \neq F _ { S } ( \phi )$ . By the Cram´er– Rao lower bound, $g _ { \theta } \leq F _ { S } ( \phi )$ (in the Loewner order). The Fisher information $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ must be estimated from task data (empirical Fisher), while the pullback metric g<sub>θ</sub> remains objective and task-independent.

The distinction is structural. The pullback metric $g _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } }$ inherits the objectivity of $g _ { z } ,$ ; the empirical Fisher $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ does not. In the suficient-statistic case, both coincide, and the pullback structure is exact. In the non-suficient case, the pullback metric provides a lower bound on the Fisher information, and the learning flow uses the empirical Fisher.

## 9.5.4 Learning Flow

The learning flow takes the natural gradient form

$$
\dot { \phi } = - g _ { \theta } ( \phi ) ^ { - 1 } \nabla _ { \phi } \mathcal { L } ( \phi ) ,\tag{274}
$$

where $g _ { \theta }$ is the pullback metric (270).

In the suficient-statistic case (Remark 9.5), $g _ { \theta } = F _ { S } ( \phi )$ , and the learning flow is a natural gradient flow. In the non-suficient case, the learning flow uses the empirical Fisher $\hat { F } _ { S } ( \phi )$ instead of $g _ { \theta }$

## 9.5.5 Estimation of the Pullback Metric and the Fisher Information

Two estimation methods.

Method 1 (pullback metric). Compute the state-space metric $g _ { z }$ from domain data (objective, task-independent, one-shot). Compute the Jacobian $J _ { 2 } ~ = ~ \partial z / \partial \theta$ via automatic diferentiation. Form $g _ { \theta } = J _ { 2 } ^ { \top } g _ { z } J _ { 2 } + \varepsilon _ { \theta } I$ . The metric is available before the task begins and is shared across tasks in the same domain.

Method 2 (empirical Fisher). If conditions 1–3 of Theorem 9.4 do not hold, estimate the empirical Fisher:

$$
\hat { F } _ { S } ( \phi ) = \frac { 1 } { N } \sum _ { i = 1 } ^ { N } \nabla _ { \phi } \mathcal { L } _ { i } ( \phi ) \cdot \nabla _ { \phi } \mathcal { L } _ { i } ( \phi ) ^ { \top } .\tag{275}
$$

The empirical Fisher is task-relevant and must be re-estimated for each task.

The choice between the two methods depends on the task structure. For tasks where the hidden state is a suficient statistic (e.g., physical simulation, structured prediction), Method 1 applies and yields the pullback metric. For unstructured tasks, Method 2 is the fallback.

## 9.5.6 Convergence

The convergence of the learning flow is determined by the properties of the pullback metric.

If $g _ { \boldsymbol { \theta } }$ is positive definite and Lipschitz continuous, the convergence rate of the learning flow is the same as ordinary gradient descent, but with a smaller constant (better condition number). In the suficient-statistic case, the pullback metric $g _ { \theta }$ equals the Fisher information, and the convergence rate is the classical natural gradient rate [25].

In the non-suficient case, the empirical Fisher $\hat { F } _ { S } ( \phi )$ is an approximation to the Fisher information, and the convergence rate is determined by the approximation quality.

## 9.6 Two-Stage Training Procedure

## 9.6.1 Stage 1: Ofline, Task-Independent

Input: domain data $\{ q _ { i } \}$ , invariant function $\Delta ( \cdot )$ , distribution family $p ( \Delta | \theta )$ Steps:

1. Compute invariants $\{ \Delta _ { i } = \Delta ( q _ { i } ) \}$ ;

2. Maximum likelihood estimate $\hat { \theta } = \mathrm { M L E } ( \{ \Delta _ { i } \} )$ ;

3. Compute Fisher information $F ( { \hat { \theta } } )$

4. Compute intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta } = F ( { \hat { \theta } } ) ^ { - 1 }$ ;

5. Compute invariant Jacobian $J ( q )$ ;

6. Compute state-space metric $\begin{array} { r } { g _ { q } ( q ) = J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J ( q ) + \varepsilon _ { G } I ; } \end{array}$

7. Determine $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } }$ from physical laws.

Output: $g _ { q }$ (state-space metric), $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } }$ (objective energy).

## 9.6.2 Stage 2: Online, Task-Relevant

Input: task trajectory data $\{ q ( t ) \} , g _ { q } , S _ { \mathrm { { o b j } } }$

Steps:

1. Parameterize $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } = S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } ( q ; \phi )$ ;

2. Compute residual $r ( t ) = \dot { q } ( t ) + g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } ( q ( t ) ) \nabla _ { q } S _ { \mathrm { o b j } } ( q ( t ) )$

3. Estimate $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ (empirical Fisher or analytic);

4. Learn $\phi$ via the natural gradient flow $\dot { \phi } = - F _ { S } ( \phi ) ^ { - 1 } \nabla _ { \phi } \mathcal { L } ( \phi )$ ;

5. Output $\phi ^ { * }$

Output: $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } ( q ; \phi ^ { * } )$ (task energy).

## 9.6.3 Separation of the Two Stages

The two stages are completely separated. Stage 1 does not require task data; stage 2 does not require domain data. This separation brings three advantages.

Advantage 1 (transferability). The result of stage 1, $g _ { q }$ , is shared among multiple tasks in the same domain.

Advantage 2 (learning simplification). Stage 2 learns only ϕ, with far fewer parameters than standard ML.

Advantage 3 (training cost). Stage 1 is completed once; stage 2 is learned per task but with few parameters.

## 9.7 Comparison with Standard Machine Learning

## 9.7.1 Comparison Table

Table 16: Comparison with standard machine learning
<table><tr><td>Aspect</td><td>Standard ML</td><td>SKG learning flow</td></tr><tr><td>Metric</td><td>Learning object</td><td>Unsupervised determination</td></tr><tr><td>Estimated parameters</td><td>All network parameters</td><td>Parameters  $\phi$  of  $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ </td></tr><tr><td>Training cost</td><td>Related to all parameters</td><td>Only related to  $\phi$ </td></tr><tr><td>Transferability</td><td>Requires domain adaptation Metric naturally transferable</td><td></td></tr><tr><td>Energy decomposition None</td><td></td><td> $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } } + S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ </td></tr></table>

## 9.7.2 Degree of Learning Simplification

Standard ML: learn all parameters (including the metric). Parameter count is $O ( d ^ { 2 } )$ (metric parameters) plus $O ( P )$ (network parameters).

SKG learning flow: metric determined unsupervised (does not consume learning budget), learn only task energy parameters $\phi .$ Parameter count is $O ( P _ { \phi } )$

Degree of simplification: learning cost reduced from $O ( d ^ { 2 } + P )$ to $O ( P _ { \phi } )$ . When d is large, the simplification is significant.

## 9.7.3 Advantage of Transferability

Standard ML: transfer requires domain adaptation. Domain adaptation requires target-task data and lacks theoretical guarantees.

SKG learning flow: the metric $g _ { q }$ is task-independent and can be shared across tasks. Transfer does not require domain adaptation.

## 9.8 Analogies with Bayesian Inference and Statistical Physics

## 9.8.1 Analogy with Bayesian Inference

Metric = prior, encoding “knowledge before the task”. Energy = likelihood, encoding “requirements of task data”. Learning = posterior, encoding “induction after the task”.

Meaning: the SKG learning flow can be understood as a geometric realization of Bayesian inference. The metric is determined by objective information; the energy is determined by task data.

## 9.8.2 Analogy with Statistical Physics

Table 18: Analogy with statistical physics
<table><tr><td>Statistical physics SKG</td><td></td><td>Meaning</td></tr><tr><td>Inertial mass</td><td>Metric  $g _ { q }$ </td><td>Physical property, not a learning object</td></tr><tr><td>Hamiltonian</td><td>Energy S</td><td>System dynamics</td></tr><tr><td>Temperature</td><td>Learning rate η</td><td>External parameter</td></tr><tr><td>Free energy</td><td>Loss L</td><td>Optimization objective</td></tr></table>

Metric = inertial mass, a physical property, not a learning object. Energy = Hamiltonian, encoding system dynamics.

Meaning: the SKG learning flow can be understood as statistical physics dynamics. The metric is “inertia”; the energy is “driving force”; learning is energy determination.
