## 9.10 Chapter Summary

This chapter establishes the learning flow and induction-decoupling theory.

From inference flow to learning flow. The inference flow $\dot { q } = - g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ contains three elements: state space $q ,$ metric $g _ { q }$ , energy S.

Metric determination vs energy determination. The metric $g _ { q }$ is objectively determined by Fisher information (unsupervised, task-independent, ofline). The energy $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ is determined by task data (supervised, task-relevant, online).

Learning-decoupling theorem. Under three conditions—metric determined unsupervised, energy decomposition, and inference flow determined—the sole object of induction is the parameters $\phi$ of $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ (Theorem 9.1).

Symmetry of inference flow and learning flow. The two flows share the same geometric structure. State space corresponds to parameter space; metric corresponds to Fisher information; energy gradient corresponds to loss gradient.

Natural gradient form. The learning flow is $\dot { \phi } = - F _ { S } ( \phi ) ^ { - 1 } \nabla _ { \phi } \mathcal { L } ( \phi )$ , where $F _ { S } ( \phi )$ is the parameter-space Fisher information.

Two-stage training. Stage 1 (ofline, task-independent) determines $g _ { q }$ and $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } }$ . Stage 2 (online, task-relevant) learns the parameters $\phi$ of $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$

Comparison with standard ML. The SKG learning flow has far fewer parameters than standard ML, naturally transferable, and low training cost.

Analogies with Bayesian inference and statistical physics. Metric = prior, energy = likelihood. Metric = inertial mass, energy = Hamiltonian.

Core formulas:

• Inference flow: $\dot { q } = - g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } \nabla S ;$

• Learning flow: $\dot { \phi } = - F _ { S } ( \phi ) ^ { - 1 } \nabla _ { \phi } \mathcal { L } ( \phi )$

• Energy decomposition: $S = S _ { \mathrm { { o b j } } } + S _ { \mathrm { { t a s k } } }$

• Residual: $r ( t ) = \dot { q } ( t ) + g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } ( q ( t ) ) \nabla _ { q } S _ { \mathrm { o b j } } ( q ( t ) ) ;$

• Task energy gradient: $\nabla _ { q } S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } ( q ( t ) ; \phi ) = - g _ { q } ( q ( t ) ) r ( t )$

• Parameter-space Fisher: $F _ { S } ( \phi ) = \mathbb { E } _ { q \sim p ( q | \phi ) } [ \nabla _ { \phi } \log p \cdot \nabla _ { \phi } \log p ^ { \top } ]$

## 10 Experimental Validation

This chapter reports the experimental validation of the paper’s core conclusions. All experiments follow the same principle: the quantities being validated are measured independently and are not generated from theoretical formulas. All experimental code, data generation scripts, and training configurations are publicly available.

The structure of this chapter is as follows. Section 10.1 describes the experimental setup. Section 10.2 reports the unit tests (T1–T6). Section 10.3 reports the core experiments (E1–E10). Section 10.4 summarizes. Section 10.5 gives the honesty declaration.

## 10.1 Experimental Setup

## 10.1.1 Test Systems

Two test systems are used. The selection criteria are: simple system, known laws, controllable dissipative metric, and allowing closed-form or semi-closed-form analysis.

One-dimensional damped oscillator. The system equation is

$$
\ddot { q } + g _ { 2 } ( q ) \dot { q } + q = 0 , \quad g _ { 2 } ( q ) = 1 + 0 . 5 q ^ { 2 } ,\tag{276}
$$

where $q \in \mathbb { R }$ is the position, $g _ { 2 } ( q )$ is the dissipative metric $( p p$ block), and the symplectic force − $^ { - } q$ is known. The dissipative metric $g _ { 2 } ( q )$ is unknown and calibrated from trajectory data.

Reason for choosing this system: one-dimensional, unconstrained, symplectic force known, dissipative metric in closed form, convenient for analysis.

Two-dimensional rotation-invariant system. The system equation is

$$
\ddot { q } i + q _ { i } + \sum _ { j = 1 } ^ { 2 } G _ { i j } ( q ) \dot { q } _ { j } = 0 , \quad G ( q ) = g _ { \Delta } ( \| q \| ) \cdot \frac { q q ^ { \top } } { \| q \| ^ { 2 } } + 0 . 5 I ,\tag{277}
$$

where $q = ( q _ { 1 } , q _ { 2 } ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 2 }$ is the position, $G ( q )$ is the $2 \times 2$ dissipative matrix field, and $g _ { \Delta } : \mathbb { R } ^ { + }$ → $\mathbb { R } ^ { + }$ is the intrinsic metric. The true intrinsic metric is

$$
g _ { \Delta } ( r ) = 1 + 0 . 3 r ^ { 2 } + 0 . 1 \sin ( 2 r ) ,\tag{278}
$$

where $r = \| q \|$ . This function is state-dependent, smooth, and non-trivial; it is unknown to the experimenter.

Reason for choosing this system: two-dimensional, rotation-invariant, state-dependent intrinsic metric, allowing validation of the state-dependent reconstruction theory (Chapter 4).

## 10.1.2 Data Generation

Data are generated by numerical integration of the SKG flow. The integrator is RK45 (Runge– Kutta–Fehlberg 4(5) order), with relative tolerance rtol = 10<sup>−10</sup> and absolute tolerance atol = $1 0 ^ { - 1 2 }$ . High-precision integration ensures numerical errors are far smaller than model errors.

Training and OOD domains are separated:

Table 20: Data partition  
Item Setting   
Training domain (1D) $q \in [ 0 . 5 , 2 . 0 ] , N = 2 0$ trajectories, 500 steps each   
OOD domain (1D) $q \in [ 2 . 0 , 3 . 0 ] , p \in [ - 1 , 1 ]$ , 200 test points   
Training domain (2D) $\lVert q \rVert \in [ 0 . 5 , 1 . 5 ] , \theta \in [ 0 , \pi / 6 ]$   
OOD domain (2D) $\theta \in [ \pi / 2 , 3 \pi / 4 ]$ (pure rotation), $\| q \| \in [ 1 . 5 , 2 . 5 ]$ (radius extrapolation)

Training and testing are strictly separated. Observations are inverted from the residual equation; the intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta }$ is not given directly. The model must learn from trajectory data.

## 10.1.3 Software and Hardware

Software. Python 3.10, NumPy, SciPy, PyTorch [1]. Optimizer: Adam, initial learning rate $1 0 ^ { - 3 }$ , cosine annealing to $1 0 ^ { - 5 }$

Random seeds. All experiments use 5 random seeds (seed numbers 0–4). Reported results are the mean and standard deviation over 5 seeds.

Hardware. Single GPU (NVIDIA A100), total runtime approximately 10 minutes.

## 10.1.4 Experimental Principles

All experiments follow the same principles:

Principle 1 (independent measurement). The quantities being validated are measured from data or independent models, not generated from theoretical formulas. For example, when validating the OOD extrapolation bound, ε<sub>ID</sub> is given by the fitting MSE of kernel ridge regression on ID data, R is computed from the standard RKHS norm calculation, and ε<sub>OOD</sub> is given by the true MSE at OOD test points. The three quantities are measured independently.

Principle 2 (parameter alignment). The parameter counts of compared experiments difer by $< 5 \%$ . For example, in the E1 experiment, the SKG parameter count is 1153 and the black-box parameter count is 1129, a diference of 2.1%.

Principle 3 (statistical testing). Multi-seed paired t-tests. The t-statistic and p-value are reported. Significance level $\alpha = 0 . 0 1$

Principle 4 (honest reporting). Failure results are not hidden. The triviality of the lower bound in E5 and the looseness of the bound in E9 are reported as they are.

## 10.2 Unit Tests (Rigorous Conclusions)

Unit tests validate the rigorous conclusions of the paper. These conclusions have mathematical proofs; the unit tests verify that the numerical implementation is correct. All unit test errors should be at machine precision $( < 1 0 ^ { - 9 } )$

Key findings.

Finding 1 (K’s OOD/ID ratio is near 1). K’s OOD error is consistently 1.05–1.15 times its ID error. The more training, the closer the ratio is to 1. This is a direct validation of the upper bound $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( K ) \leq \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K )$ of Theorem 8.15.

Finding 2 (M’s OOD error has a lower bound). M’s ID error continuously decreases (from $5 . 4 3 \times 1 0 ^ { - 4 }$ to $4 . 2 5 \times 1 0 ^ { - 5 } )$ , but its OOD error is nearly unchanged (always in 6.1– $6 . 8 \times 1 0 ^ { - 2 } )$ . This is a direct validation of the lower bound $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( M ) \geq L _ { M } { \cdot } d { - } C$ of Theorem 8.15.

Finding 3 (the gap grows with training steps). The $\mathrm { M } / \mathrm { K }$ ratio is 101 at 1000 steps, 279 at 3000 steps, and 1589 at 10000 steps. The more training, the larger the extrapolation ability gap between K and M.

Quantitative validation of the lower bound. On 1000 OOD points, the bound $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( M ) \geq$   
$L _ { M } \cdot d - C$ holds for all seeds (0 violations). But the proportion of meaningful lower bounds is   
zero, $\mathrm { i . e . , } L _ { M } \cdot d - C < 0$ for all OOD points. Reasons include $C = \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( M ) \approx 0 . 2 6 3$ being large,   
$d ( q _ { * } , D _ { \mathrm { t r a i n } } )$ being small in paired-rotation OOD, and $L _ { M }$ not being naturally larger than 1. Judgment.

Conclusion. Theorem 8.15 under fair comparison (same training steps plus OOD/ID ratio metric), the upper bound part is strongly validated; the lower bound part holds formally but is trivial. PASS (upper bound strongly validated).

## 10.3.10 E10: Learning-Flow Decoupling Validation (Theorem 9.1)

Validation target. Theorem 9.1: when the metric $g _ { q }$ is objectively determined by Fisher information, the sole object of induction is the parameters ϕ of the task energy $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$

Setup. One-dimensional damped oscillator, $\ddot { q } + g _ { 2 } ( q ) \dot { q } + q = 0$ with $g _ { 2 } ( q ) = 1 + 0 . 5 q ^ { 2 }$   
Training domain $q \in [ 0 . 5 , 2 . 0 ]$ , 20 trajectories of 500 steps each. OOD domain $q \in [ 2 . 0 , 3 . 0 ]$   
$p \in [ - 1 , 1 ]$ , 200 test points. Data generated by RK45 integration, rtol = 10<sup>−10</sup>, $\mathrm { a t o l } = 1 0 ^ { - 1 2 }$ Two paradigms are compared:

• SKG learning flow. The symplectic force − $^ { - } q$ is known. The metric $g _ { 2 } ( q )$ is estimated by kernel ridge regression from the residual equation (without using the true parameter). The only learned object is the parameter $\phi$ of the task energy $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } } = \phi \cdot q ^ { 2 } / 2$ (1 parameter).

• Standard MLP. No physical prior is used. A 2-layer MLP (32 hidden units) learns the entire $\ddot { q }$ function from scratch (1185 parameters).

Both are trained with Adam for 2000 epochs on the same data, optimizer, and step count. Results (5 seeds). Per-seed OOD MSE:

<table><tr><td>Method</td><td>Parameter count</td><td>OOD MSE  $( \mathrm { m e a n } \pm \mathrm { s t d } )$ </td></tr><tr><td>SKG learning flow</td><td></td><td> $\mathbf { 1 . 4 8 6 4 \pm 0 . 3 3 0 9 }$ </td></tr><tr><td>Standard MLP</td><td>1185</td><td> $2 . 1 0 4 5 \pm 0 . 3 5 4 2$ </td></tr><tr><td>Ratio</td><td>1/1185</td><td>0.706</td></tr></table>

Summary statistics:  
Paired t-test: t = −6.581, p = 0.0028.

Key findings.

Finding 1 (directional conclusion). The SKG learning flow OOD MSE is significantly lower than the standard MLP $( p = 0 . 0 0 2 8 < 0 . 0 1 )$ . The directional prediction of Theorem 9.1 is validated: when the metric is objectively determined by Fisher information, learning simplifies to determining a small number of task-energy parameters.

Finding 2 (parameter reduction). The SKG learning flow uses 1 parameter; the standard MLP uses 1185 parameters. The parameter count is reduced by a factor of 1185.

Finding 3 (OOD error reduction). The SKG learning flow OOD MSE is 70.6% of the standard MLP, a reduction of 29.4%.

Fairness discussion. The comparison is a paradigm comparison, not a controlled-variable experiment. The SKG paradigm uses physical law priors (symplectic force form, residual equation, quadratic energy structure), reducing the problem to learning few parameters. The MLP paradigm uses no physical prior and learns the entire dynamics from scratch. The standard MLP is not given −q, which is correct because “no physical prior” is the definition of the MLP paradigm. Giving the MLP the −q term would make it an SKG variant, not a pure MLP. The comparison is therefore fair between the law-induced paradigm and the data-driven paradigm on OOD performance.

Degree of validation of Theorem 9.1. The directional content is validated: when the metric is determined by Fisher information, learning requires only 1 parameter, and the OOD performance is better than that of the unstructured MLP. The quantitative prediction of the theorem (absolute values) is not reached in this implementation; Fisher estimation accuracy and optimization convergence afect the absolute values. A finer experimental design would be needed to validate the quantitative prediction.

Conclusion. The E10 experiment passes. The SKG learning flow (1 parameter) has significantly lower OOD MSE than the standard MLP (1185 parameters), with $p = 0 . 0 0 2 8$ . Parameter count is reduced by a factor of 1185, and OOD error is reduced by 29.4%. The experiment supports the directional content of Theorem 9.1 and validates the OOD advantage of the lawinduced paradigm over the data-driven paradigm. PASS.

## 10.5 Honesty Declaration

## 10.5.1 Rigorous Conclusions (T1–T6)

All have numerical validation, with errors at machine precision $( < 1 0 ^ { - 9 } )$ . These are implementation validations—the mathematical theorems are proven, and the experiments confirm the numerical implementation is correct.

## 10.5.2 Core Conclusions (E1–E8, E10)

Validated on the 1D damped system and 2D rotation-invariant system, with multi-seed paired t-tests $p < 0 . 0 1$ . These are empirical validations—data are generated from physical systems, and ground truth is obtained by independent measurement.

## 10.5.3 Overly Loose Bound (E9)

The OOD extrapolation bound of E9 holds for all seeds, but the tightness ratio is $6 \times 1 0 ^ { - 4 }$ ， about 1700 times larger than the actual error. The bound is valid but requires improvement of the estimates of R and $\kappa _ { \Delta }$ in practice.

## 10.5.4 Triviality of the Lower Bound (E5)

The lower bound of E5 holds for all seeds (0 violations), but the proportion of meaningful lower bounds is zero. The lower bound holds formally but provides no quantitative constraint. The substantive contribution of Theorem 8.15 is the upper bound part.

## 10.5.5 Unvalidated Core Theorems

The following theorems have rigorous proofs in the paper but have not yet been experimentally validated: intrinsic metric reconstruction (Theorems 3.9, 3.11, 3.14) is a mathematical theorem, no experiment needed; sample complexity and convergence of the state-dependent intrinsic metric are theoretical derivations, unvalidated; three-layer projections are theoretical derivations, unvalidated; symplectic contribution ratio $\rho _ { H }$ is a theoretical derivation, unvalidated; convergence of the learning flow and time-varying Fisher are theoretical derivations, unvalidated. The mathematical correctness of these theorems is independent of the experiments.

## 10.5.6 Reproducibility

All experimental code, data generation scripts, and training configurations are publicly available.   
Random seeds are fixed, and results are reproducible.

## 10.6 Chapter Summary

This chapter reports the experimental validation of the paper’s core conclusions. A total of 16 experiments, all passed. 6 unit tests validate rigorous conclusions (machine precision); 10 core experiments validate assumption-dependent and empirical conclusions (multi-seed paired t-test $p < 0 . 0 1 )$ . Among them, the upper bound part of E5 is strongly validated, the lower bound part is trivial; the bound of E9 holds but is overly loose; E10 validates the learning-decoupling theorem (parameter count 1 vs. 1153).

## 11 Discussion and Conclusion

Chapters 2–10 established the complete SKG framework, from geometric foundations to metric construction, from inference flow to learning flow, from theoretical guarantees to experimental validation. This chapter discusses the overall positioning of the framework, the classification of conclusions, generalization paths, and open problems.

The structure of this chapter is as follows. Section 11.1 describes the core positioning of this paper. Section 11.2 summarizes the rigorous conclusions. Section 11.3 summarizes the assumption-dependent conclusions. Section 11.4 summarizes the heuristic observations. Section 11.5 discusses generalization to general law structures. Section 11.6 lists open problems. Section 11.7 gives concluding remarks.

## 11.1 Core Positioning of This Paper

## 11.1.1 Holistic Nature of the Framework

The core of this paper is to build a law-induced inference and induction engine. The symplectic– dissipative system is an instance of SKG, not its boundary. The holistic nature of the framework is reflected at three levels.

Level 1 (formalization). The SKG octuple $\boldsymbol { \mathcal { S } } = ( M , g , \omega , H , S , G , \Delta , \Pi )$ converts lawinduced inference from a philosophical concept into a mathematical object. The four levels of laws—symmetry group, axiom system, conservation laws, constitutive relations—map to the four components ∆, Π, ω, H, S of the framework. This mapping makes the framework analyzable, implementable, and verifiable.

Level 2 (construction). All components of the framework are constructible, implementable, and diferentiable. Invariants enter the metric construction through the Jacobian: $g = J ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J + \varepsilon _ { G } I$ . The kernel bandwidth matrix is uniquely determined by Fisher information: ${ \boldsymbol { \Sigma } } = { \boldsymbol { F } } ( { \hat { \boldsymbol { \theta } } } )$ The three-layer projections $\Pi _ { A } , P _ { \bot } , \Pi _ { E }$ handle axiom, symplectic-step, and conservation constraints respectively. The residual equation $r = - g \nabla S$ gives the observable.

Level 3 (theoretical guarantees and operability). Kernel parameterization yields the RKHS extrapolation bound (Theorem 4.15). The strict OOD gain theorem in group directions (Theorem 8.15). The learning-decoupling theorem (Theorem 9.1) proves that the sole object of induction is the task energy. Residual theory gives the observable formula, rank condition, and noise propagation.

The three levels constitute a complete chain from formalization to construction, from theory to operation.

## 11.1.2 Paradigm Diference from Existing Methods

The diference between SKG and existing methods (PINN, HNN/LNN, equivariant networks, Neural ODE, RKHS methods) is paradigmatic.

Table 44: Paradigm comparison of SKG and existing methods
<table><tr><td>Dimension</td><td>Existing methods</td><td>SKG</td></tr><tr><td>Role of laws</td><td>Soft regularization or architectural constraint</td><td>Hard structure</td></tr><tr><td>Violating laws</td><td>Tolerable (cost is loss)</td><td>Mathematically forbidden</td></tr><tr><td>OOD guarantee</td><td>None or partial</td><td>RKHS bound</td></tr><tr><td>Constraint handling</td><td>Loss term or architecture design</td><td>Three-layer projection</td></tr><tr><td>Equivariance</td><td>Data augmentation or architecture design</td><td>Structural guarantee</td></tr><tr><td>Learning object</td><td>All network parameters</td><td>Only parameters  $\phi$  of  $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ </td></tr></table>

Existing methods are “adding law elements within a data-driven framework.” SKG is “taking laws as the core of the inference engine, with data only for calibration.”

## 11.1.3 Applicability Boundary

The applicability boundary of SKG is: inference tasks with law structure. Specifically, the task must satisfy three conditions:

Condition 1 (existence of symmetry). The system has a symmetry group G, and the invariant $\Delta$ is definable.

Condition 2 (existence of constraints). The system has hard constraints, degeneracy conditions, or conservation laws, handleable by three-layer projections.

Condition 3 (law decomposability). The system’s laws decompose into a known part and a to-be-estimated part. The symplectic–dissipative structure is the simplest decomposition, but not the only one.

As long as the task satisfies the three conditions, the SKG framework applies. Beyond these three conditions (e.g., pure data fitting, no symmetry, no constraints), SKG does not apply.

## 11.2 Rigorous Conclusions

Rigorous conclusions are the core of this paper. They have complete mathematical proofs, some with experimental validation. The following summarizes by chapter.

## 11.2.1 Geometric Foundations (Chapter 2)

Conclusion 1 (GENERIC degeneracy conditions). The GENERIC decomposition ${ \dot { z } } =$ $L \nabla E + M \nabla S$ satisfies the two degeneracy conditions $L \nabla S = 0$ , M∇E = 0. Proof: direct verification of energy conservation and entropy increase.

Conclusion 2 (symplectic-step projection). When $S = S ( \boldsymbol { p } )$ , the degeneracy condition $L \nabla S = 0$ is violated. The symplectic-step projection $P _ { \perp } = I - \nabla S \nabla S ^ { \top } / \| \nabla S \| ^ { 2 }$ corrects the symplectic step and restores the degeneracy condition (Theorem 5.6).

Conclusion 3 (octuple). The SKG system is defined by the octuple $\begin{array} { r } { \boldsymbol { S } = ( M , g , \omega , H , S , G , \Delta , \Pi ) } \end{array}$ (Definition 2.1). Four components correspond to the four levels of laws.

Conclusion 4 (geometric force). The second-order SKG flow contains the geometric force $\Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } )$ after expansion. The geometric force vanishes in the constant-metric case and is nonzero in the state-dependent metric case.

## 11.2.2 Metric Construction (Chapter 3)

Conclusion 5 (kernel Hessian gives metric). The Hessian of the kernel at $\Delta = 0$ gives the metric $g _ { \Delta } = \Sigma ^ { - 1 }$ (Theorem 3.4). Proof includes positive semi-definiteness and positive definiteness analysis.

Conclusion 6 (solvability rank condition). The intrinsic metric is uniquely solvable if ran $\dot { \boldsymbol { \mathfrak { s } } } ( \mathcal { A } ) = M ( M + 1 ) / 2$ (Theorem 3.9). Proof includes suficiency and necessity.

Conclusion 7 (consistency criterion). Multiple embeddings are consistent if vech $, ( g ) \in$ Col(A) (Theorem 3.11).

Conclusion 8 (uniqueness). Full rank implies uniqueness; rank deficiency implies infinitely many solutions (Theorem 3.14).

Conclusion 9 (Fisher objectivity). $g _ { \Delta } = F ( { \hat { \theta } } ) ^ { - 1 }$ is task-independent (Theorem 3.19). Proof includes a four-step argument.

Conclusion 10 (error decomposition). The metric reconstruction error decomposes into four terms (Theorem 3.21).

Conclusion 11 (condition number amplification). $\| \delta g _ { \Delta } \| / \| g _ { \Delta } \| ~ \le ~ \kappa ( \Sigma ^ { * } ) ^ { 2 } \sqrt { M / N }$ (Theorem 3.22). Proof includes Neumann series and submultiplicativity.

Conclusion 12 (sample size criterion). $N \geq M \kappa ^ { 4 } / \epsilon _ { \mathrm { t o l } } ^ { 2 }$ (Corollary 3.23).

## 11.2.3 State-Dependent Intrinsic Metric (Chapter 4)

Conclusion 13 (contradiction of the constant assumption). Constant $g _ { \Delta }$ implies $\Gamma _ { \Delta } = 0 .$ $R _ { \Delta } = 0$ , contradicting curved manifolds (Proposition 4.1).

Conclusion 14 (pointwise identifiability). At sampling points where the rank condition holds, $g _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { 0 } )$ is pointwise identifiable (Theorem 4.8).

Conclusion 15 (function-space identifiability). Under standard RKHS conditions, $g _ { \Delta } ( \cdot )$ is unique in $\mathcal { H } _ { \kappa _ { \Delta } }$ (Theorem 4.9).

Conclusion 16 (sample complexity). $N \ge C \cdot M ( M + 1 ) / 2 \cdot \mathrm { v o l } ( \mathrm { s u p p } ( \Delta ) ) / \varepsilon ^ { M / s }$ (Theorem 4.10).

Conclusion 17 (existence and uniqueness). Under standard RKHS conditions, the solution exists and is unique (Theorems 4.12 and 4.13).

Conclusion 18 (OOD extrapolation bound). $\varepsilon _ { G } ( q _ { * } ) \leq \varepsilon _ { G } ( q _ { I D } ) + R \| J ( q _ { * } ) \| ^ { 2 } \sqrt { 2 - 2 \kappa _ { \Delta } ( \Delta _ { i n v } ) }$ (Theorem 4.15).

Conclusion 19 (function transfer). If two systems have the same intrinsic metric function, the metric is transferable across systems (Theorem 4.17).

## 11.2.4 Three-Layer Projections (Chapter 5)

Conclusion 20 (properties of symplectic-step projection). $P _ { \perp }$ is symmetric, idempotent, orthogonal, with rank $d - 1$ (Proposition 5.5).

Conclusion 21 (properties of conservation projection). Π<sub>E</sub> preserves energy to first order, projects along the specified direction, idempotent (Proposition 5.8).

Conclusion 22 (projection reduces extrapolation distance). $d _ { g } ^ { S _ { a c t i v e } } ( z _ { * } , z _ { I D } ) \leq d _ { g } ^ { M } ( z _ { * } , z _ { I D } )$ (Theorem 5.9).

## 11.2.5 Residual Theory (Chapter 6)

Conclusion 23 (symplectic part cancels in the residual). The symplectic step $P _ { \perp } \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H$ is orthogonal to ∇S and does not contribute to the residual (Theorem 6.2).

Conclusion 24 (1D observable). $g _ { 2 } ^ { o b s } ( q _ { i } ) = - ( \ddot { p } _ { i } + q _ { i } ) / p _ { i } ~ \mathrm { ( P r o p o s i t i o n ~ 6 . 3 ) }$

Conclusion 25 (4D rank condition). rank $( [ A _ { 1 } ; \dots ; A _ { N } ] ) = 7$ (Proposition 6.4).

Conclusion 26 (noise propagation). $\sigma _ { g _ { 2 } } = \sigma / | p |$ (Proposition 6.5).

Conclusion 27 (structural unidentifiability of $g _ { q q } )$ . When $S = S ( p ) , g _ { q q }$ does not appear in the residual (Proposition 6.6).

Conclusion 28 (potential–metric coupling). $\varepsilon _ { p p } M ^ { - 1 } p = - \partial _ { q } \varepsilon _ { V }$ (Proposition 6.7).

## 11.2.6 Kernel Parameterization (Chapter 7)

Conclusion 29 (kernel approximation rate). $\epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r o x } } \leq C \cdot P ^ { - s / M }$ (Theorem 7.2).

Conclusion 30 (generalization gap). $\mathcal { R } _ { N } ( { \mathcal { K } } _ { D , r } ) \leq \mathcal { R } _ { N } ( { \mathcal { K } } _ { \mathrm { M L P } } ) - \Delta ( N , r )$ (Theorem 7.5).

Conclusion 31 (strict saddle property). When $r > r ^ { * }$ , the loss function satisfies the strict saddle property (Theorem 7.7).

Conclusion 32 (OOD error decomposition). ϵ<sub>OOD</sub> $\leq \epsilon _ { \mathrm { f i t } } + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { a p p r o x } } + \epsilon _ { \mathrm { e x t r a p } }$ (Theorem 7.9).

## 11.2.7 Structural OOD Advantages (Chapter 8)

Conclusion 33 (kernel metric equivariance). $g _ { K } ( T _ { g } \cdot q ) = \rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } g _ { K } ( q ) \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 }$ (Lemma 8.10). Conclusion 34 (OOD bound under orthogonal representation). $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( K ) \leq \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K )$   
(Corollary 8.12).

Conclusion 35 (MLP Lipschitz lower bound). $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ( M ) \geq L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , D _ { t r a i n } ) - C$ (Lemma 8.13).

Conclusion 36 (OOD gain in group directions). If $L _ { M } \cdot d > C + \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K )$ , the gain is strictly positive (Theorem 8.15).

Conclusion 37 (suficient condition for gain). $( L _ { M } - L ^ { * } ) \cdot d > \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( M ) + \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } ( K )$ (Corollary 8.16).

## 11.2.8 Learning Flow (Chapter 9)

Conclusion 38 (learning decoupling). Under the three conditions—metric unsuperviseddetermined, energy decomposed, inference flow determined—the sole object of induction is the parameters ϕ of $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ (Theorem 9.1).

Conclusion 39 (symmetry of inference and learning flows). The two flows share the same geometric structure: state space corresponds to parameter space, metric to Fisher information, energy gradient to loss gradient.

Conclusion 40 (natural gradient form). The learning flow is $\dot { \phi } = - F _ { S } ( \phi ) ^ { - 1 } \nabla _ { \phi } \mathcal { L } ( \phi )$

## 11.2.9 Experimental Validation (Chapter 10)

Conclusion 41 (unit tests). T1–T6 all pass, errors at machine precision.

Conclusion 42 (core experiments). E1–E10 all pass, multi-seed paired t-test $p < 0 . 0 1$ Conclusion 43 (E5 strong validation). K’s OOD/ID ratio is 1.05–1.15 (near 1), M’s ratio is 114–1675 (with lower bound). Upper bound part strongly validated.

Conclusion 44 (E10 validates learning decoupling). Learning flow parameter count 1 vs. standard SGD 1153, lower OOD MSE.

## 11.3 Assumption-Dependent Conclusions

Assumption-dependent conclusions depend on explicit assumptions. If the assumptions do not hold, the conclusions are not guaranteed.

## 11.3.1 Assumption Dependence of Metric Reconstruction

Assumption-dependent conclusion 1 (constant intrinsic metric). The constant-case reconstruction theory of Chapter 3 depends on the assumption: $g _ { \Delta }$ is a constant matrix. If $g _ { \Delta }$ is state-dependent, the equation becomes nonlinear, and the function-space method of Chapter 4 is needed.

Assumption-dependent conclusion 2 $( J ^ { ( 1 ) }$ row-full-rank). The relative transformation $R _ { k } = J ^ { ( k ) } ( J ^ { ( 1 ) } ) ^ { - }$ <sup>+</sup> depends on the assumption: $J ^ { ( 1 ) }$ is row-full-rank and $M \leq d$ . If not full-rank, $R _ { k }$ should be understood in the least-squares sense.

## 11.3.2 Assumption Dependence of OOD Advantages

The group-direction OOD gain theorem (Theorem 8.15) depends on four implicit assumptions: Assumption-dependent conclusion 3 (linear group action). $T _ { g } \cdot q = \rho ( g ) q$ . If the group action is nonlinear, the equivariance conditions are more complex.

Assumption-dependent conclusion 4 (orthogonal representation). $\rho ( g ) ^ { - \top } \rho ( g ) ^ { - 1 } =$ I. If the representation is non-orthogonal, the regularization term $\varepsilon _ { G } I$ breaks equivariance, and the theorem is not guaranteed.

Assumption-dependent conclusion 5 (constant intrinsic metric). $g _ { \Delta }$ is a constant matrix. If $g _ { \Delta }$ is state-dependent, the equivariance transmission needs modification.

Assumption-dependent conclusion 6 (inverse Lipschitz condition). The MLP metric satisfies $\lVert g _ { M } ( q _ { * } ) - g _ { M } ( q _ { 0 } ) \rVert \geq L _ { M } \cdot d ( q _ { * } , q _ { 0 } )$ . If the MLP does not satisfy this, the lower bound is not guaranteed.

## 11.3.3 Assumption Dependence of the Learning Flow

The learning-decoupling theorem (Theorem 9.1) depends on three conditions:

Assumption-dependent conclusion 7 (metric unsupervised-determined). $g _ { q }$ is determined by unsupervised domain data, independent of task data. If the metric is learned from task data, decoupling fails.

Assumption-dependent conclusion 8 (energy decomposition). $S = S _ { \mathrm { o b j } } + S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ with $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } }$ known. If the boundary is blurred, decoupling fails.

Assumption-dependent conclusion 9 (inference flow determined). The inference flow is $\dot { q } = - g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ . If the inference flow is second-order or other, decoupling needs modification.

## 11.4 Heuristic Observations

Heuristic observations have no rigorous proof but are used as engineering diagnostics.

## 11.4.1 Curvature and Extrapolation Dificulty

Observation 1. In regions of high curvature, extrapolation error tends to be larger. This is based on the Jacobi field equation. Heuristic bound: ε<sub>OOD</sub> $\lesssim \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } + C \cdot \| R \| _ { \infty } \cdot d _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ^ { 2 }$

This is a heuristic observation, not a theorem, nor an assumption. Reasons: the exponential growth of the Jacobi field holds only locally in high-curvature regions; global extrapolation error also depends on geodesic length, conjugate point distribution, and initial conditions. The lower bound on the curvature tensor is dificult to obtain on general manifolds.

Engineering meaning: if $\| R \|$ is computable, it can serve as a diagnostic quantity for OOD monitoring, but does not constitute a theoretical guarantee. The precise relationship between curvature and extrapolation error is open problem 12.

## 11.4.2 Symplectic Contribution Ratio

Observation 2. The symplectic contribution ratio $\rho _ { H } = \| \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H \| / ( \| \omega ^ { \sharp } \nabla H \| + \| g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S \| )$ measures the relative weight of the conservative and dissipative parts. In systems with large $\rho _ { H }$ , the SKG OOD advantage is more significant.

This is a diagnostic quantity with no theoretical guarantee. $\rho _ { H }$ takes values in [0, 1]. $\rho _ { H } = 0$ is pure dissipation; $\rho _ { H } = 1$ is pure conservative.

## 11.4.3 Error Trade-ofs

Observation 3. There are trade-ofs among the three OOD error terms (fitting, approximation, extrapolation). Increasing model complexity reduces approximation error but increases fitting error. Increasing sample size reduces fitting error but does not afect approximation or extrapolation. The kernel function choice afects both extrapolation and approximation. The optimal choice balances the three errors.

This is an engineering observation with no theoretical guarantee. It provides criteria for choosing hyperparameters $( r , P , \kappa _ { \Delta } , N )$

## 11.5 Generalization to General Law Structures

The SKG framework is not restricted to symplectic–dissipative systems. It applies to any law structure with a “known part + to-be-estimated part” decomposition.

## 11.5.1 General Forms of the Components

Table 45: General forms of SKG components
<table><tr><td>SKG component</td><td>General form</td><td>Application examples</td></tr><tr><td>Symplectic structure ω</td><td>Known conservation law</td><td>Mechanics, electromagnetism, quantum mechani</td></tr><tr><td>Energy  $H , S$ </td><td>Known constitutive relation</td><td>Material models, potential functions</td></tr><tr><td>Invariants  $\Delta$ </td><td>Symmetry group representation</td><td>Rotation, translation, scale, permutation</td></tr><tr><td>Projections II</td><td>Constraint handling</td><td>Hard constraints, conservation laws, gauge fixing</td></tr><tr><td>Estimated metric g</td><td>Unknown dissipation/source</td><td>Damping, friction, diffusion</td></tr><tr><td>Intrinsic metric  $g _ { \Delta } ( \cdot )$ </td><td>Coordinate-free form of g</td><td>Transfer learning, OOD distance</td></tr></table>

## 11.5.2 Generalization Paths

Path 1 (other conservative structures). The symplectic structure ω of SKG can be replaced by other conservative structures. For example, Poisson structures (degenerate symplectic structures) for constrained mechanics [30], Nambu structures for multi-Hamiltonian systems.

Path 2 (other dissipative structures). The dissipative structure $g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ of SKG can be replaced by other dissipative structures. For example, the GENERIC framework allows more general dissipative matrices M.

Path 3 (other symmetry groups). The invariant $\Delta$ of SKG can be replaced by invariants of other symmetry groups. For example, permutation groups for many-body systems, gauge groups for field theory.

Path 4 (other constraints). The three-layer projections of SKG can be replaced by other constraint handling. For example, nonholonomic constraints for wheeled robots [29], control constraints for reinforcement learning.

The four generalization paths share the same skeleton: invariant construction, metric construction, pullback, inference or control, inverse transformation.

## 11.5.3 Limitations of Generalization

Generalization has limitations. Three core limitations:

Limitation 1 (orthogonality of the symmetry group). The OOD advantage theorem (Theorem 8.15) depends on orthogonal representations. The regularization design for nonorthogonal representations is open problem 1.

Limitation 2 (clarity of the energy decomposition). The learning-decoupling theorem (Theorem 9.1) depends on a clear boundary between $S _ { \mathrm { o b j } }$ and $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ . When the boundary is blurred, decoupling fails.

Limitation 3 (dimension). The sample complexity theorem (Theorem 4.10) is infeasible in high dimensions (Remark 4.11). Practical methods for high-dimensional systems need to rely on structural assumptions.

## 11.6 Open Problems

The following open problems are classified by topic.

## 11.6.1 Symmetry Groups and Representations

Problem 1 (non-orthogonal representations). For non-orthogonal representations, the regularization term ε<sub>G</sub>I breaks equivariance. Three repair paths:

Path A (equivariant regularization). Replace $\varepsilon _ { G } I$ with $\varepsilon _ { G } J ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J$ . Equivariance is preserved, but the null space cannot be filled.

Path B (orthogonal complement regularization). Replace $\varepsilon _ { G } I$ with $\varepsilon _ { G } P _ { \perp }$ , where $P _ { \perp } = I - J ( J ^ { \top } J ) ^ { - 1 } J ^ { \top }$ . Equivariance is preserved under orthogonal representations, and identified directions are not polluted.

Path C (extended invariant set). Add a logarithmic scale invariant $\Delta _ { \mathrm { s c a l e } } = \log ( w / h )$ Equivariance is automatically preserved.

The three paths each have advantages and disadvantages; no unified optimal solution.

Problem 2 (nonlinear group actions). For nonlinear group actions, equivariance conditions are more complex. The joint rank condition does not automatically hold at all q. The reconstruction theory under nonlinear group actions needs study.

Problem 3 (unification of diferent invariant spaces). If diferent embeddings map to invariant spaces of diferent dimensions (Case C), is there a “largest common subspace” or “fiber product” structure such that some g<sub>∆</sub> components can still be reconstructed?

## 11.6.2 Metric Construction and Reconstruction

Problem 4 (optimal parameterization). Cholesky function, kernel expansion, or hybrid parameterization—which is optimal? Existing results only give qualitative comparison; no quantitative criterion.

Problem 5 (tightness of the convergence rate). The convergence rate $N ^ { - s / ( 2 s + M ) }$ cannot be stably validated in low-dimensional systems (Remark 4.14). A tighter convergence rate or a more efective validation method is needed.

Problem 6 (time-varying Fisher). The theory of flow with one $\Sigma _ { t }$ per step. Online estimation and convergence of time-varying Fisher.

Problem 7 (non-suficient statistics). A quantitative bound on the invariant error. The degree to which the metric is underestimated under non-suficient statistics.

Problem 8 (high dimensions). Alleviation of the curse of dimensionality when $M > 2 0$ Possible paths include sparse kernels, random features, and block kernels.

## 11.6.3 Learning Flow

Problem 9 (convergence of the learning flow). Global convergence, convergence rate, and relation to $F _ { S }$ of $\dot { \phi } = - F _ { S } ^ { - 1 } \nabla \mathcal { L }$ . Online estimation of $F _ { S }$ under time-varying task data.

Problem 10 (non-parametric $S _ { \mathbf { t a s k } } )$ . If $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ is parameterized by an RKHS, what is the convergence of the learning flow?

Problem 11 (multi-task learning). Multiple tasks share the metric $g _ { q }$ but each has its own $S _ { \mathrm { t a s k } }$ . Convergence and transfer bounds for multi-task learning.

## 11.6.4 Geometry and OOD

Problem 12 (precise relationship between curvature and extrapolation error). Appendix C gives a heuristic observation without rigorous proof. Questions to answer: under what conditions can a lower bound on the curvature tensor give a quantitative lower bound on extrapolation error? How does the exponential growth of the Jacobi field translate into a bound on ε<sub>OOD</sub>?

Problem 13 (non-symmetric shifts). For other types of OOD—noise shift, occlusion shift, adversarial perturbation—the invariant method has no advantage. Other types of OOD guarantees need study.

Problem 14 (online learning). In online or streaming scenarios, how to update the metric while maintaining the OOD bound?

## 11.7 Concluding Remarks

## 11.7.1 Core Contributions

This paper establishes a complete law-induced inference and induction engine. Core contributions include:

Formalization. The SKG octuple converts law-induced inference from a philosophical concept into a mathematical object. The four levels of laws map to the four components of the framework.

Construction. Invariants enter the metric construction through the Jacobian. The kernel bandwidth matrix is uniquely determined by Fisher information. Three-layer projections handle three types of constraints. The residual equation gives the observable.

Theoretical guarantees. Kernel parameterization yields the RKHS extrapolation bound. The strict OOD gain theorem in group directions. The learning-decoupling theorem proves that the sole object of induction is the task energy.

Operability. Residual theory gives the observable formula, rank condition, and noise propagation. Engineering criteria guide hyperparameter selection.

The pullback principle. The framework is unified by a single structural principle: pullback of a coarser-space metric to a finer space through the Jacobian. This principle appears twice. In the inference flow (Chapter 3), the intrinsic metric $g _ { \Delta }$ on the invariant space is pulled back to the state space through $J _ { 1 } = \partial \Delta / \partial q$ , yielding $g _ { q } = J _ { 1 } ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J _ { 1 } + \varepsilon _ { G } I$ . In the learning flow (Chapter 9), the state-space metric $g _ { z }$ is pulled back to the parameter space through $J _ { 2 } = { \partial z } / { \partial \theta } , \mathrm { y i e l d i n g }$ $g _ { \theta } = J _ { 2 } ^ { \top } g _ { z } J _ { 2 } + \varepsilon _ { \theta } I$ . The two pullbacks share the same mechanism. The two flows are not independent; they are two instances of the same geometric mechanism, applied at two levels of the hierarchy

$$
{ \mathrm { i n v a r i a n t ~ s p a c e } } \ { \xrightarrow { J _ { 1 } } } \ { \mathrm { s t a t e ~ s p a c e } } \ { \xrightarrow { J _ { 2 } } } \ { \mathrm { p a r a m e t e r ~ s p a c e } } .\tag{279}
$$

The properties of the pulled-back metrics are inherited from the intrinsic metric: task-independence, parameterization invariance, and transferability. This unification is the structural reason the learning problem reduces to determining the task energy alone.

## 11.7.2 Boundary of the Framework

The boundary of SKG is not the symplectic–dissipative system, but inference tasks with law structure. As long as the task has symmetry, constraints, and conservation laws, the SKG framework applies.

Beyond this boundary (pure data fitting, no symmetry, no constraints), SKG does not apply. SKG is not a general-purpose machine learning framework, but a law-induced inference framework.

## 11.7.3 Future Directions

Direction 1 (theoretical extensions). Rigorous theory for non-orthogonal representations, nonlinear group actions, time-varying Fisher, and learning flow convergence.

Direction 2 (algorithm implementation). Implement a complete algorithm library in $\mathrm { P y T o r c h / J A X }$ . Systematic comparisons with Adam, natural gradient, and other optimizers.

Direction 3 (application validation). Validate the SKG framework on real systems (robots, molecular dynamics, language models). Systematic comparisons with PINN, HNN/LNN, and other methods.

Direction 4 (high-dimensional extensions). Generalization to intermediate dimensions $( 5 0 \leq d \leq 2 0 0 )$ . Applicability of sparse kernels, random features, and block kernels.

Direction 5 (deep integration). Deep integration with Transformers. Embedding SKG structure in attention mechanisms.

## 11.7.4 Final Statement

SKG is a law-induced inference and induction engine. Its four components—symplectic structure, energy, invariants, projections—all belong to the objective law system. Laws are given a priori; data only provide instances. Inference equals geometric evolution under law constraints; induction equals the determination of the task energy.

The boundary of SKG is not the symplectic–dissipative system, but inference tasks with law structure. As long as the task has symmetry, constraints, and conservation laws, the SKG framework applies.

## A Construction of the Vectorization Matrix $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) }$

The solvability analysis of Chapter 3 depends on the construction of the vectorization matrix $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) }$ . This appendix gives the complete construction.

## A.1 Origin of the Problem

The vectorized equation of Section 3.5

$$
v e c h ( g ) = { \mathcal { A } } ^ { ( k ) } v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) , \quad k = 1 , \ldots , K ,\tag{280}
$$

where $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d ( d + 1 ) / 2 \times M ( M + 1 ) / 2 }$ is a matrix determined by the invariant Jacobian $J ^ { ( k ) } =$ ${ \partial \Delta ^ { ( k ) } } / { \partial q }$ . This appendix gives the explicit construction of $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) }$

## A.2 Vectorization of Symmetric Matrices

Recall Definition 3.8. For a symmetric matrix $A \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n \times n }$ $v e c h ( A ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { n ( n + 1 ) / 2 }$ takes the uppertriangular components (including the diagonal), arranged in lexicographic order.

Specifically, the index mapping of vech(A) is: $( i , j )$ corresponds to $A _ { i j }$ , where $1 \leq i \leq j \leq n$ The index mapping is

$$
\mathrm { i n d e x } ( i , j ) = \frac { ( i - 1 ) ( 2 n - i ) } { 2 } + j , \quad 1 \leq i \leq j \leq n .\tag{281}
$$

For example, for $n = 2 , v e c h ( A ) = ( A _ { 1 1 } , A _ { 1 2 } , A _ { 2 2 } ) ^ { \top } . \mathrm { F o r } n = 3 , v e c h ( A ) = ( A _ { 1 1 } , A _ { 1 2 } , A _ { 1 3 } , A _ { 2 2 } , A _ { 2 3 } , A _ { 3 3 } ) ^ { \top }$

## A.3 Component Form of the Pullback Formula

The pullback formula is

$$
g ( q ) = J ( q ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J ( q ) ,\tag{282}
$$

where $J ( { \boldsymbol { q } } ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times d } , g _ { \Delta } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times M }$ , and $g ( q ) \in \mathbb { R } ^ { d \times d }$

Expanding the component form:

$$
g _ { i j } ( q ) = \sum _ { a = 1 } ^ { M } \sum _ { b = 1 } ^ { M } J _ { a i } ( q ) ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a b } J _ { b j } ( q ) = \sum _ { a , b = 1 } ^ { M } J _ { a i } J _ { b j } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a b } ,\tag{283}
$$

where $J _ { a i } = \partial \Delta ^ { a } / \partial q ^ { i }$ is the $( a , i )$ element of the Jacobian.

## A.4 Construction of the Elements of $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) }$

Definition A.1 (Vectorization matrix $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } )$ . Given $J ^ { ( k ) } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { M \times d }$ , the elements of the vectorization matrix $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( \dot { k } ) }$ are defined as

$$
( A ^ { ( k ) } ) _ { ( i , j ) , ( a , b ) } = \left\{ \begin{array} { l l } { J _ { a i } ^ { ( k ) } J _ { b j } ^ { ( k ) } + J _ { a j } ^ { ( k ) } J _ { b i } ^ { ( k ) } , } & { a < b , } \\ { J _ { a i } ^ { ( k ) } J _ { b j } ^ { ( k ) } , } & { a = b , } \end{array} \right.\tag{284}
$$

where $( i , j )$ corresponds to $g _ { i j } \ ( 1 \leq i \leq j \leq d )$ and $( a , b )$ corresponds to $( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a b } ~ ( 1 \leq a \leq b \leq M )$

## A.5 Verification of the Construction

Proposition A.2 (Correctness of the construction). The vectorization matrix $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) }$ satisfies

$$
v e c h ( g ) = \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) .\tag{285}
$$

$$
( i , j )
$$

$$
1 \leq i \leq j \leq d .
$$

By the pullback formula (283),

$$
( i , j )
$$

$$
g _ { i j } = \sum _ { a , b = 1 } ^ { M } J _ { a i } J _ { b j } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a b } .\tag{286}
$$

Splitting the sum by the ordering of $^ { a , }$ b:

$$
g _ { i j } = \sum _ { a = 1 } ^ { M } J _ { a i } J _ { b i } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a a } + \sum _ { a < b } ( J _ { a i } J _ { b j } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a b } + J _ { a j } J _ { b i } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { b a } ) .\tag{287}
$$

$\mathrm { B y }$ the symmetry of $g _ { \Delta } , ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a b } = ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { b a }$

$$
g _ { i j } = \sum _ { a = 1 } ^ { M } J _ { a i } J _ { b i } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a a } + \sum _ { a < b } ( J _ { a i } J _ { b j } + J _ { a j } J _ { b i } ) ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a b } .\tag{288}
$$

Comparing with Definition (284) gives

$$
g _ { i j } = \sum _ { ( a , b ) } ( \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ) _ { ( i , j ) , ( a , b ) } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a b } ,\tag{289}
$$

where $( a , b )$ ranges over all $1 \leq a \leq b \leq M$ . This is exactly that the $( i , j )$ component of $v e c h ( g )$ equals the $( i , j )$ component of $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } )$ □

## A.6 Vectorization Convention

We adopt the standard vech (taking the upper triangle once), with coeficients 1. The ofdiagonal elements $( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a b } ~ ( a < b )$ contribute two terms in the pullback, so the coeficients of the of-diagonal elements in Definition (284) are $J _ { a i } J _ { b j } + J _ { a j } J _ { b i }$ . The diagonal elements $( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { a a }$ contribute one term in the pullback, so the coeficient is $J _ { a i } J _ { b i }$

If one adopts the convention of counting of-diagonal elements twice (i.e., vech takes all $n ^ { 2 }$ elements), the of-diagonal coeficients are $\frac { 1 } { 2 } \big ( J _ { a i } J _ { b j } + J _ { a j } J _ { b i } \big )$ . We adopt the standard convention, with coeficients 1.

## A.7 Concrete Example

$$
M = 2 , d = 2 . \ J ^ { ( k ) } = \left( { \begin{array} { l l } { J _ { 1 1 } } & { J _ { 1 2 } } \\ { J _ { 2 1 } } & { J _ { 2 2 } } \end{array} } \right) . \ v e c h ( g _ { \Delta } ) = ( ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 1 1 } , ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 1 2 } , ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 2 2 } ) ^ { \top } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 3 } . \ v e c h ( g ) = ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 1 1 } . ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 1 2 } . 
$$

$$
( g _ { 1 1 } , g _ { 1 2 } , g _ { 2 2 } ) ^ { \top } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 3 } .
$$

Construct $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { 3 \times 3 }$ . By Definition (284):

$$
( \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ) _ { ( 1 , 1 ) , ( 1 , 1 ) } = J _ { 1 1 } J _ { 1 1 } = J _ { 1 1 } ^ { 2 } ,\tag{290}
$$

$$
( \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ) _ { ( 1 , 1 ) , ( 1 , 2 ) } = J _ { 1 1 } J _ { 1 2 } + J _ { 1 2 } J _ { 1 1 } = 2 J _ { 1 1 } J _ { 1 2 } ,\tag{291}
$$

$$
( \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ) _ { ( 1 , 1 ) , ( 2 , 2 ) } = J _ { 1 2 } J _ { 1 2 } = J _ { 1 2 } ^ { 2 } ,\tag{292}
$$

$$
( \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ) _ { ( 1 , 2 ) , ( 1 , 1 ) } = J _ { 1 1 } J _ { 2 1 } ,\tag{293}
$$

$$
( \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ) _ { ( 1 , 2 ) , ( 1 , 2 ) } = J _ { 1 1 } J _ { 2 2 } + J _ { 1 2 } J _ { 2 1 } ,\tag{294}
$$

$$
( \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ) _ { ( 1 , 2 ) , ( 2 , 2 ) } = J _ { 1 2 } J _ { 2 2 } ,\tag{295}
$$

$$
( \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ) _ { ( 2 , 2 ) , ( 1 , 1 ) } = J _ { 2 1 } ^ { 2 } ,\tag{296}
$$

$$
( \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ) _ { ( 2 , 2 ) , ( 1 , 2 ) } = 2 J _ { 2 1 } J _ { 2 2 } ,\tag{297}
$$

$$
( \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } ) _ { ( 2 , 2 ) , ( 2 , 2 ) } = J _ { 2 2 } ^ { 2 } .\tag{298}
$$

That is,

$$
\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } = \left( \begin{array} { c c c } { J _ { 1 1 } ^ { 2 } } & { 2 J _ { 1 1 } J _ { 1 2 } } & { J _ { 1 2 } ^ { 2 } } \\ { J _ { 1 1 } J _ { 2 1 } } & { J _ { 1 1 } J _ { 2 2 } + J _ { 1 2 } J _ { 2 1 } } & { J _ { 1 2 } J _ { 2 2 } } \\ { J _ { 2 1 } ^ { 2 } } & { 2 J _ { 2 1 } J _ { 2 2 } } & { J _ { 2 2 } ^ { 2 } } \end{array} \right) .\tag{299}
$$

Verification: $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) }$ vech(g<sub>∆</sub>) gives

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \left( J _ { 1 1 } ^ { 2 } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 1 1 } + 2 J _ { 1 1 } J _ { 1 2 } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 1 2 } + J _ { 1 2 } ^ { 2 } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 2 2 } \right. \qquad } \\ { \left. J _ { 1 1 } J _ { 2 1 } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 1 1 } + ( J _ { 1 1 } J _ { 2 2 } + J _ { 1 2 } J _ { 2 1 } ) ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 1 2 } + J _ { 1 2 } J _ { 2 2 } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 2 2 } \right) . } \\ { J _ { 2 1 } ^ { 2 } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 1 1 } + 2 J _ { 2 1 } J _ { 2 2 } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 1 2 } + J _ { 2 2 } ^ { 2 } ( g _ { \Delta } ) _ { 2 2 } \qquad } \end{array}\tag{300}
$$

This is exactly the three independent components of $g = J ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J .$

## A.8 Summary

This appendix gives the construction of the vectorization matrix $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) } . \quad \mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) }$ maps the components of $g _ { \Delta }$ to the components of $g .$ The construction splits into diagonal and of-diagonal cases. Under the standard vech convention, the of-diagonal coeficients are $J _ { a i } J _ { b j } + J _ { a j } J _ { b i } .$ and the diagonal coeficients are $J _ { a i } J _ { b i }$ . Correctness of the construction is guaranteed by Proposition A.2.

## B Consistency Check Algorithm

Section 3.5 established the consistency criterion (Theorem 3.11): vech $\iota ( g ) \in C o l ( \mathcal { A } )$ . This appendix gives the engineering consistency check algorithm.

## B.1 Algorithm Description

Input. State-space metric g (estimated from data), Jacobians of multiple embeddings $\{ J ^ { ( k ) } \} _ { k = 1 } ^ { K }$ , tolerance $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { t o l } }$

Output. Consistency judgment (consistent / inconsistent), residual norm $\lVert \boldsymbol { r } \rVert$ Steps.

Step 1 (construct A). Construct $\mathcal { A } ^ { ( k ) }$ by Definition A.1, stacked as

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \mathcal { A } = \binom { \mathcal { A } ^ { ( 1 ) } } { \mathcal { \vdots } } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { K d ( d + 1 ) / 2 \times M ( M + 1 ) / 2 } . } \end{array}\tag{301}
$$

Step 2 (construct b). Stack vech(g) for all embeddings:

$$
b = \binom { v e c h ( g ) } { \begin{array} { r } { \vdots } \\ { v e c h ( g ) } \end{array} } \in \mathbb { R } ^ { K d ( d + 1 ) / 2 } .\tag{302}
$$

Step 3 (least-squares solution). Compute

$$
v e c h ( \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ) = \mathcal { A } ^ { + } b ,\tag{303}
$$

where $\mathcal { A } ^ { + }$ is the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse of ${ \mathcal { A } } .$

Step 4 (compute residual). The residual is

$$
r = \mathcal { A } v e c h { ( \hat { g } _ { \Delta } ) } - b .\tag{304}
$$

Step 5 (judgment). $\mathrm { I f } \ \| r \| < \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { t o l } }$ , consistent; otherwise, inconsistent.

## B.2 Handling Inconsistency

Inconsistency has three possible sources.

Source 1 $( J ^ { ( k ) }$ inaccurate). Numerical diferentiation error or model error. Handling: increase numerical precision or correct the model.

Source 2 (g noisy). Observation error. Handling: increase sample points, use weighted least squares.

Source 3 (insuficient embeddings). K too small, insuficient constraints. Handling: increase the number of embeddings.

Diagnosis: check the structure of the residual r. If the residual is large on specific embeddings, those embeddings are inaccurate; if the residual is uniformly large on all embeddings, g is noisy or the number of embeddings is insuficient.

## B.3 Numerical Stability

The computation of $\mathcal { A } ^ { + }$ depends on the condition number $\kappa ( \mathcal { A } )$ of A. If $\kappa ( \mathcal { A } )$ is large, the computation of $\mathcal { A } ^ { + }$ is unstable.

Numerically stable implementation:

1. Perform singular value decomposition $\mathbf { \mathcal { A } } = U \Sigma V ^ { \top }$ ;

2. Threshold truncation: keep singular values with $\sigma _ { i } > \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { t h r e s h } } \sigma _ { \mathrm { m a x } }$ , where $\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { t h r e s h } } = 1 0 ^ { - 1 0 }$

3. Compute the pseudoinverse $\mathcal { A } ^ { + } = V \Sigma ^ { + } U ^ { \top }$ , where $\Sigma ^ { + }$ is the reciprocal of the truncated singular values.

This implementation avoids numerical amplification from small singular values.

## B.4 Summary

This appendix gives the consistency check algorithm. The algorithm has five steps: construct ${ \mathcal { A } } ,$ construct b, solve least squares, compute residual, judge. Inconsistency has three sources, with diferent diagnostic methods. Numerical stability is guaranteed by SVD truncation.

## C Connection and Curvature

Chapters 2 and 4 established the geometric foundations of SKG. This appendix gives the complete theory of connection and curvature, as a higher-order geometric supplement.

## C.1 Why Connection and Curvature Are Needed

The main text focuses on the first-order dissipative flow $\dot { q } = - g ^ { - 1 } \nabla S$ , which does not require connection and curvature. The complete SKG theory contains second-order dynamics and position-dependent metrics, where connection and curvature cannot be omitted.

The connection describes parallel transport and geodesics. The curvature describes the intrinsic bending of the manifold. Both are determined by the derivatives of the metric, computable through higher-order derivatives of the kernel function [22, 21].

The connection enters the second-order SKG flow (equation (24)) and the residual equation (161) through the geometric force $\Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } )$ . When the metric is state-dependent, the geometric force cannot be omitted; its magnitude is a function of the curvature tensor.

## C.2 Christofel Symbols

## C.2.1 Definition

Definition C.1 (Christofel symbols). Let $g _ { i j } ( q )$ be a Riemannian metric and $g ^ { i l }$ the contravariant metric components (satisfying $g ^ { i l } g _ { l j } = \delta _ { j } ^ { i } )$ . The Christofel symbols of the Levi-Civita connection are defined as

$$
\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } = \frac { 1 } { 2 } g ^ { i l } \left( \partial _ { j } g _ { l k } + \partial _ { k } g _ { l j } - \partial _ { l } g _ { j k } \right) ,\tag{305}
$$

where $\partial _ { i } = \partial / \partial q ^ { i }$

## C.2.2 Properties

Proposition C.2 (Properties of Christofel symbols). The Christofel symbols satisfy:

(1) Symmetry. $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } = \Gamma _ { k j } ^ { i }$ (torsion-free).

(2) Metric compatibility. $\nabla _ { k } g _ { i j } = 0$

(3) Non-tensorial. Under coordinate changes, $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i }$ does not transform as a tensor.

Proof. (1) By definition (305), $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i }$ is symmetric in $j , k$ (since the bracket is symmetric under $j  k )$

(2) Metric compatibility: $\nabla _ { k } g _ { i j } = \partial _ { k } g _ { i j } - \Gamma _ { k i } ^ { l } g _ { l j } - \Gamma _ { k j } ^ { l } g _ { i l } = 0$ . Substituting the definition verifies it.

(3) Under a coordinate change $q ^ { \prime } = \phi ( q )$ , the transformation of Γ includes second-derivative terms, not transforming as a tensor. □

## C.2.3 Christofel Symbols in SKG

In SKG, the covariant metric is $g _ { q } = J ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J$ (ignoring the regularization term $\varepsilon _ { G } I )$ . Substituting into definition (305):

$$
\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } ( q ) = { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } g _ { q } ^ { i l } \left( \partial _ { j } g _ { q , l k } + \partial _ { k } g _ { q , l j } - \partial _ { l } g _ { q , j k } \right) .\tag{306}
$$

The derivative of the metric $\partial _ { j } g _ { q }$ contains three parts:

$$
\begin{array} { r } { \partial _ { j } g _ { q } = ( \partial _ { j } J ) ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } J + J ^ { \top } ( \partial _ { j } g _ { \Delta } ) J + J ^ { \top } g _ { \Delta } ( \partial _ { j } J ) . } \end{array}\tag{307}
$$

The first and third terms are Jacobian derivative terms; the second term is the intrinsic metric derivative term.

The intrinsic metric derivative term is

$$
\partial _ { j } g _ { \Delta } = ( \partial g _ { \Delta } / \partial \Delta ) \cdot ( \partial \Delta / \partial q ^ { j } ) = ( \partial g _ { \Delta } / \partial \Delta ) \cdot J _ { j } ,\tag{308}
$$

where $J _ { j }$ is the j-th row of J (as an M-dimensional column vector).

Meaning: the Christofel symbols depend on the derivative $\partial _ { j } J$ of J (second derivative of the embedding) and the derivative $\partial _ { j } \Sigma$ of the kernel bandwidth (state dependence of the kernel bandwidth). The geometric force $\Gamma ( \dot { z } , \dot { z } )$ is thereby explicitly expressed.

## C.2.4 Special Cases

Constant metric case (J = I, Σ constant). $\partial _ { j } J = 0 , \partial _ { j } \Sigma = 0$ , hence $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } = 0$ . The geometric force is zero.

Linear embedding case (J constant, Σ constant). $\partial _ { j } J = 0 , \partial _ { j } \Sigma = 0$ , hence $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } = 0$ . The geometric force is zero.

Position-dependent kernel bandwidth case $( \Sigma ( q )$ state-dependent). $\partial _ { j } \Sigma \neq 0$ , hence $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } \neq 0$ . The geometric force is nonzero.

Nonlinear embedding case $( J ( q )$ state-dependent). $\partial _ { j } J \neq 0$ , hence $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } \neq 0$ . The geometric force is nonzero.

## C.3 Riemann Curvature Tensor

## C.3.1 Definition

Definition C.3 (Riemann curvature tensor). Defined by the derivatives of the connection:

$$
R _ { \ \sigma \mu \nu } ^ { \rho } = \partial _ { \mu } \Gamma _ { \nu \sigma } ^ { \rho } - \partial _ { \nu } \Gamma _ { \mu \sigma } ^ { \rho } + \Gamma _ { \mu \lambda } ^ { \rho } \Gamma _ { \nu \sigma } ^ { \lambda } - \Gamma _ { \nu \lambda } ^ { \rho } \Gamma _ { \mu \sigma } ^ { \lambda } .\tag{309}
$$

Lowering one index gives

$$
R _ { \rho \sigma \mu \nu } = g _ { \rho \lambda } R _ { \sigma \mu \nu } ^ { \lambda } .\tag{310}
$$

## C.3.2 Properties

Proposition C.4 (Properties of the Riemann curvature tensor). The Riemann curvature tensor satisfies:

(1) Antisymmetry. $R _ { \rho \sigma \mu \nu } = - R _ { \sigma \rho \mu \nu } , R _ { \rho \sigma \mu \nu } = - R _ { \rho \sigma \nu \mu }$

(2) Exchange symmetry. $R _ { \rho \sigma \mu \nu } = R _ { \mu \nu \rho \sigma }$

(3) First Bianchi identity. $R _ { \rho [ \sigma \mu \nu ] } = 0$ (cyclic sum over $\sigma , \mu , \nu$ is zero).

(4) Second Bianchi identity. $\nabla _ { [ \lambda } R _ { \rho \sigma ] \mu \nu } = 0$

Proof. Directly compute from definition (309), or argue from symmetry.

## C.3.3 Sectional Curvature

Definition C.5 (Sectional curvature). For a two-dimensional plane $\sigma = \mathit { s p a n } ( u , v )$ in the tangent space, the sectional curvature is defined as

$$
K ( u , v ) = \frac { R _ { \rho \sigma \mu \nu } u ^ { \rho } v ^ { \sigma } u ^ { \mu } v ^ { \nu } } { | u | ^ { 2 } | v | ^ { 2 } - \langle u , v \rangle ^ { 2 } } ,\tag{311}
$$

where $| u | ^ { 2 } = g _ { \rho \sigma } u ^ { \rho } u ^ { \sigma }$ and $\langle u , v \rangle = g _ { \rho \sigma } u ^ { \rho } v ^ { \sigma }$

The sectional curvature describes the bending of the manifold in the direction of the twodimensional plane. If the sectional curvature is identically zero, the manifold is flat (Euclidean). If identically positive constant, the manifold is a sphere. If identically negative constant, the manifold is hyperbolic space.

## C.4 Relation to Higher-Order Derivatives of the Kernel Function

## C.4.1 Derivative Hierarchy of the Kernel Function

Table 46: Derivative hierarchy of the kernel function
<table><tr><td></td><td>Order Expression</td><td>Geometric quantity</td></tr><tr><td>0</td><td> $\kappa ( \Delta )$ </td><td>Kernel value</td></tr><tr><td>2</td><td> $\partial _ { a } \partial _ { b } \kappa | _ { 0 } = - \Sigma _ { a b } ^ { - 1 }$ </td><td>Covariant metric  $g _ { \Delta } = \Sigma ^ { - 1 }$ </td></tr><tr><td>3</td><td> $\partial _ { a } \partial _ { b } \partial _ { c } \kappa | _ { 0 } = 0$ </td><td>Even function, zero</td></tr><tr><td>4</td><td> $\partial _ { a } \partial _ { b } \partial _ { c } \partial _ { d } \kappa | _ { 0 } = \Sigma _ { a b } ^ { - 1 } \Sigma _ { c d } ^ { - 1 } + \Sigma _ { a c } ^ { - 1 } \Sigma _ { b d } ^ { - 1 } + \Sigma _ { a d } ^ { - 1 } \Sigma _ { b c } ^ { - 1 }$ </td><td>Seed of curvature</td></tr></table>

## C.4.2 Odd Derivatives Vanish

Proposition C.6 (Odd derivatives vanish). The Gaussian kernel $\kappa ( \Delta ) = \exp ( - \textstyle { \frac { 1 } { 2 } } \Delta ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } \Delta )$ is even. All odd-order partial derivatives at $\Delta = 0$ are zero.

Proof. Even function definition: $\kappa ( - \Delta ) = \kappa ( \Delta )$ . After taking an odd-order partial derivative with respect to $\Delta$ and substituting $\Delta = 0$ , the result is zero (since the odd-order derivative of an even function at the origin is an odd function, and an odd function takes the value zero at the origin). □

Meaning: connection and curvature cannot be directly obtained from higher-order derivatives of the kernel in the $\Delta$ space (since odd derivatives vanish). They are computed from the pulled-back metric in the q space.

## C.4.3 Specific Form of the 4th Derivative

For the Gaussian kernel, expand at $\Delta = 0$

$$
\kappa ( \Delta ) = 1 - \frac 1 2 \Delta ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } \Delta + \frac 1 8 ( \Delta ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } \Delta ) ^ { 2 } + O ( \Vert \Delta \Vert ^ { 6 } ) .\tag{312}
$$

The 4th partial derivative is

$$
\partial _ { a } \partial _ { b } \partial _ { c } \partial _ { d } \kappa | _ { 0 } = \Sigma _ { a b } ^ { - 1 } \Sigma _ { c d } ^ { - 1 } + \Sigma _ { a c } ^ { - 1 } \Sigma _ { b d } ^ { - 1 } + \Sigma _ { a d } ^ { - 1 } \Sigma _ { b c } ^ { - 1 } .\tag{313}
$$

This is the “seed” of curvature. Its relation to the curvature tensor is established through the pullback formula.

## C.5 Computing Connection and Curvature from the q-Space Metric

## C.5.1 Computation Steps

Step 1 (compute q-space covariant metric). $g _ { q } = J ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J + \varepsilon _ { G } I$

Step 2 (compute derivatives of $g _ { q } )$ .

$$
\partial _ { k } g _ { q } = ( \partial _ { k } J ) ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } J + J ^ { \top } ( \partial _ { k } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } ) J + J ^ { \top } \Sigma ^ { - 1 } ( \partial _ { k } J ) .\tag{314}
$$

Step 3 (compute the contravariant metric $g _ { q } ^ { - 1 } )$ . By Cholesky decomposition or linear solve.

Step 4 (compute Christofel symbols $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i } )$ . Substitute into definition (305).

Step 5 (compute Riemann curvature $R _ { \sigma \mu \nu } ^ { \dot { \rho } } )$ . Substitute into definition (309), requiring derivatives of Γ.

## C.5.2 Computational Complexity

Complexity of each step:

Table 47: Computational complexity of connection and curvature
<table><tr><td>Operation Complexity</td><td></td></tr><tr><td> $\partial _ { k } g _ { q }$ </td><td> $O ( M ^ { 2 } d + M d ^ { 2 } )$ </td></tr><tr><td> $g _ { q } ^ { - 1 }$ </td><td> $O ( d ^ { 3 } )$ </td></tr><tr><td> $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { \ i }$ </td><td> $O ( d ^ { 4 } )$ </td></tr><tr><td> $\partial _ { k } \Gamma _ { j l } ^ { i }$ </td><td> $O ( d ^ { 5 } )$ </td></tr><tr><td> $R _ { \sigma \mu \nu } ^ { \rho }$ </td><td> $O ( d ^ { 4 } )$  (number of components)</td></tr></table>

The curvature tensor has $O ( d ^ { 4 } )$ components. For $d = 1 0 , \ : O ( d ^ { 4 } ) = 1 0 ^ { 4 }$ . For $d = 5 0$ $O ( d ^ { 4 } ) = 6 \times 1 0 ^ { 6 }$ . Curvature computation is expensive in high dimensions.

## C.6 Curvature and Extrapolation Dificulty: Heuristic Observation

## C.6.1 Observation

Observation. In regions of high curvature, extrapolation error tends to be larger. This observation is based on the Jacobi field equation

$$
\frac { D ^ { 2 } J } { d t ^ { 2 } } + R ( \dot { \gamma } , J ) \dot { \gamma } = 0 ,\tag{315}
$$

where J is the Jacobi field (geodesic deviation vector) and R is the Riemann curvature tensor.   
The growth rate of its solution is controlled by the curvature tensor $\| R \|$ [22].

## C.6.2 Heuristic Bound

$$
\varepsilon _ { \mathrm { O O D } } \lesssim \varepsilon _ { \mathrm { I D } } + C \cdot \| R \| _ { \infty } \cdot d _ { \mathrm { O O D } } ^ { 2 } ,\tag{316}
$$

where $\| R \| _ { \infty }$ is the maximum norm of the curvature tensor, d<sub>OOD</sub> is the OOD distance, and C is a constant.

## C.6.3 Degree of Rigor

This is a heuristic observation, not a theorem, nor an assumption. Reasons:

(1) The exponential growth of the Jacobi field holds only locally in high-curvature regions; global extrapolation error also depends on geodesic length, conjugate point distribution, and initial conditions.

(2) The lower bound on the curvature tensor is dificult to obtain on general manifolds.

(3) Experimentally, E9 only validates the RKHS extrapolation bound, not the curvature– OOD relation.

## C.6.4 Engineering Meaning

If ∥R∥ is computable, it can serve as a diagnostic quantity for OOD monitoring, but does not constitute a theoretical guarantee. The precise relationship between curvature and extrapolation error is open problem 12.

The SKG OOD guarantee comes from the RKHS extrapolation bound (Theorem 4.15) and does not depend on the heuristic observation of this appendix.

## C.7 Special Cases

C.7.1 Constant Metric

J = I, Σ constant. $\Gamma = 0 , R = 0$ , geometric force vanishes.

## C.7.2 Linear Embedding, State-Dependent Kernel Bandwidth

J constant, Σ(q) state-dependent. $\Gamma \neq 0 , R \neq 0$ , geometric force nonzero.

## C.7.3 Nonlinear Embedding

J(q) state-dependent. $\Gamma \neq 0 , R \neq 0$ , geometric force nonzero.

## C.8 Relation to Existing Literature

Table 48: Relation to existing literature
<table><tr><td>Concept</td><td>Existing literature</td><td>SKG&#x27;s advance</td></tr><tr><td>Kernel to metric</td><td>Standard Hessian metric [23, 24]</td><td>Systematized for inference</td></tr><tr><td>Connection and curvature</td><td>Riemannian geometry standard [22, 21]</td><td>Related to kernel higher-order deri</td></tr><tr><td>Curvature of Hessian metrics</td><td>Information geometry [23, 24]</td><td>Applied to OOD analysis</td></tr><tr><td>Curvature and generalization</td><td>Partial work</td><td>Heuristic connection, unproven</td></tr></table>

SKG’s unique contribution: integrating these tools into the law-induced inference framework, as diagnostic quantities for OOD analysis.

## C.9 Open Problems

Problem A. The precise relationship between the curvature norm ∥R∥ and extrapolation error (see open problem 12).

Problem B. How to eficiently compute the curvature norm in high-dimensional systems. Possible paths: sparsification, low-rank approximation, random projection.

## C.10 Summary

This appendix establishes the complete theory of connection and curvature. The Christofel symbols $\Gamma _ { j k } ^ { i }$ are defined by the derivatives of the metric. The Riemann curvature tensor $R _ { \sigma \mu \nu } ^ { \rho }$ is defined by the derivatives of the connection. Both are computed from higher-order derivatives of the kernel function through the pullback formula. The relationship between curvature and extrapolation error is a heuristic observation, not a theorem. In engineering, the curvature can serve as a diagnostic quantity for OOD monitoring, but does not constitute a theoretical guarantee.
